A.21. Executive summary

As a poor, low-lying coastal nation, and with the whole of the country situated within 30 km from and heavily dependent on the Gambia river basin. The Gambia is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change and has been ranked as one of the most vulnerable countries in Africa (2015). It is also among the 53rd least ready country in the world, reflecting its high vulnerability score and low readiness score. Furthermore, it is classified as a Fragile and Conflict-affected States country, with limited resources that could be re-directed to climate adaptation.

As detailed in Annex B, 2 climate change will negatively affect The Gambia's marine, coastal and riverine fisheries by reducing the availability of fish stocks due to increased water temperature, salinity, turbidity and acidity – among others - on many important shellfish species. Overall, climate change will threaten the livelihoods of poor fishermen and decrease the availability of fish from capture fisheries, which is the main source of protein for most Gambia households. The Gambia has a high proportion of its population that depends on fishing to protect (or) the most poor and vulnerable people. The climate change induced increase in the frequency of extreme weather events will also have negative impacts on the livelihoods of fishermen and their families, leading to losses in the fish value chain. The climate change vulnerability assessment of the 28 most important shellfish species in The Gambia, conducted as a result of a survey of species, supports a majority of the catches in the Gambia, is likely to be negatively impacted by one or multiple climate change stressors. Mangrove forests, which are

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## essential spawning grounds and nurseries for many fish and shellfish species, are also expected to be degraded as a result of accelerating sea level rise (SLR) and increased salinity.

### 3. In fact, without sufficient climate-proofing of their fisheries value chain, there is a high risk of damage resulting coastal infrastructure and equipment, with associated costs for repairs increasing as a result of intensified storm surge/processing and climate impacts on transport to markets. Climate impact assessments performed at each major fishing port in the Gambia by the project team have identified that both increased sea level rise and major changes in extreme weather events will contribute to the degradation of the existing coastal fisheries infrastructure. The latter, in turn, will negatively affect the livelihoods of fisher folk, who will be at increased risk both as sea and on shore.

### 4. To reduce the climate risk affecting the fisheries sector, the adaptation responses can be three-fold: first, by reducing the very high exposure and vulnerability of the current artisanal fisheries infrastructure and equipment, next by increasing the resilience of fisher folk livelihoods to climate change impacts, and thirdly, by enabling fisher folk to compensate for climate-induced decreases in capture fisheries. Thus, by restoring degraded mangrove forests that play a key role as breeding grounds and nurseries for fish and shellfish (by investing in restoration through replanting and assisted natural regeneration), the project will contribute towards reducing the vulnerability of fisher folk livelihoods.

### 5. The project aims to assist Gambian fisherfolk to build their resilience against climate change and associated extreme weather events, with a particular focus on climate-proofing fisheries infrastructure and on value chain segments dominated by women (fish handling and processing). The project will promote the adoption of climate-resilient aquaculture production technologies, including oyster culture and rice-fish farming, among others. PROGEWASH II also aims to strengthen the sustainability of its results.

### 6. The project responds to the priorities identified in the Gambia’s National Climate Change Strategy and sectoral policy objectives and it contributes to GCF’s Adaptation Results Areas (ARA) 1, 2, 3, 4, to GCF’s Mitigation

| Component | Strengthening restoration capacity and community management of degraded habitats holding key breeding grounds and nurseries for fish and shellfish (by investing in restoration through replanting and assisted natural regeneration) |

| Component | Improved climate change adaptive capacities, providing the supply necessary for the sustainability of the project’s results (by investing in climate-proofing fisheries infrastructure and aquaculture projects as a result of the project, as well as giving way for other climate adaptation projects and initiatives and enhancing stronger private sector involvement in the sector). |

| Threats | 40% of all fisher folk are women. Of these, 167,843 vulnerable Gambians depend on the fisheries value chain. 52.4% of them are women, to adapt to the impact of climate change and improve their livelihoods through the adoption of climate resilient aquaculture, through climate proofing of existing infrastructure and equipment, and by diversifying their income sources (e.g., if fish availability is limited). These interventions will build the resilience of beneficiaries and will improve their incomes and their number of food security. |

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### The Climate Crisis

7. Risk of climate-related impacts results from the interaction of climate-related hazards with exposure and vulnerability of human-and natural systems (IPCC, 2014). In The Gambia, the high exposure to climate-related hazards comes as a result of its low-lying coastal location, which is highly vulnerable to sea-level rise and storm surges that threaten supporting their livelihoods. As a low-lying coastal nation that is completely dependent on a single river basin, and subject to Land Degradation and Desertification (LDD) and the associated vulnerability to climate change, The Gambia is one of the most vulnerable countries in Africa. It is highly exposed as climate change threats both on land and at sea and due to the small size of the economy, the country possesses limited capacity to autonomously undertake extensive adaptation measures. In 2014, The Gambia was ranked among the top three most vulnerable countries globally by the International Negotiation Initiative (INDI) index. Gambia is the 33rd most vulnerable country and the 53rd least ready country in the world.

8. The story of climate change is largely one of water, as no other country has perhaps as close a relationship with a river than The Gambia. A majority of the national boundaries were drawn tracing lines 10 km north and south of the River Gambia. This river forms the natural border between The Gambia and Senegal to the east and Mali to the west. As such, the Gambia has both a narrow oceanic economic exclusion zone extending 200 nautical miles into the Atlantic Ocean and a wide land-based economic exclusion zone extending 10 km north and south of the River Gambia. The river passes through the river originates as seasonal rainfall in the upland areas of the river's watershed, which aggregate and drain through the length of the country on the way to the sea. Along its path the growing volume of fresh water enters into a dynamic hydrological system of fresh-to-saltwater bodies, and the habitats and ecosystems that it supports is subject to and responds differently to the major climate change forces encountered along its transect.

#### Figure B-1 The Gambia: Gambia River watershed (in green) and economic exclusion zone

9. To address the range of climate change dynamics within the Gambian fisheries, a comprehensive climate analysis has been prepared as part of the project formulation process (see Annex 2 for full details). This analysis presents an integrated pathway through which climate change will impact the full range of Gambia's coastal resources and the livelihoods that depend on them. The analysis also identifies potential adaptation availability for the Gambian populations. The Gambian fisheries can be separated into four ecological segments: 1) the open ocean, 2) the estuarine zone, 3) the riverine zone, and 4) the inland lakes. These are shown in Figure B-2 below. Climate change affects each segment in different ways and by different mechanisms. To provide a more detailed analysis on the topic of climate change impact on Gambian fisheries, new research was conducted for the present assessment.

#### Impact on the livelihoods of Gambia River folk and general population

10. The climate trend analysis and projections of climate change were conducted by downscaling global circulation models® and projecting changes in temperature and precipitation (see Annex 2 chapter 3 for full details). The entire watershed of the Gambia River was included in the geographical scope of the analysis, as desired for a complete understanding of future stream flow.

**Climate baseline**

11. Observed trends for air temperature and precipitation. Considering the whole of the Gambia River watershed over the period 1978-2016, average annual precipitation has shown a non-increase of about 1 mm per year, while temperatures have increased on average 0.02°C per year; or added 0.74°C in total over the period (statistically significant at p<0.05). The observed trends are consistent with the general warming trend noted globally over the entire watershed, while precipitation has declined slightly in the central watershed, with slight increases seen in the upper reaches of the river and along the coast (see Figure B-2).

Figure B-2: Observed annual change in air temperature 1978–2016. The shape represents the Gambia River watershed and the grid cells within it that indicate statistically significant trends (p<0.05) based on T-test analysis of LTS-MOD data.

The five models selected were GFDL-ESM2M, MIROC-ESM, IPSL-CMA-MR, NORESM1, and the HADGEM-ES. All of these models are part of the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project 3 (CMIP3), used in conducting the analysis for the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report (AR5). For more detail see Annex 2, Chapter 3, part 2 and footnotes.


---
**Page 11**
---

12. Historical trends in sea surface temperature: In the Canary Current Large Marine Ecosystem (CCLME), upper ocean temperatures (0-50 m depth) have risen on average 0.28°C per decade over the past 30-year period of 1982–2011 (Vives-Betancourt et al., 2010). or of high skill, with some areas experiencing warming more than others.

13. Global Mean Sea Level (GMSL): The IPCC AR5 shows strengthened evidence for an increase in the rate of sea level rise since the mid-20th century, with an average rate of 2.7 mm yr−1 over the period 1971–2010 increasing to 3.7 ± 2.4 mm yr−1 (high confidence) for the period 2006–2018 (high confidence). GMSL is projected to rise between 0.5 and 0.9 m by 2100 under RCP 2.6, with a likely range of 0.37–0.74 m (SRES B1) and 0.57–0.86 m (SRES A1B). Under the higher emission scenario RCP 8.5, GMSL is projected to rise between 0.39 and 0.82 m by 2100 with a likely range of 0.24–0.70 m (SRES B1) and 0.60–0.94 m (SRES A1B). The projected rise is thus expected to be a clear acceleration in sea level rise (SLR) from 3.7 mm yr−1 in present, to 8.2 mm yr−1 by the end of this century.

14. Salinity: It is virtually certain that since 1950 near-surface high-salinity regions have become more saline, while low salinity regions have become fresher, with medium confidence that this is linked to an intensification of the 25-year hydrological cycle.

15. Future projections for air temperature and precipitation: Future projections were prepared using all five models (GFDL-ESM2M, MIROC5, MPI-ESM-LR, MRI-CGCM3, NorESM1-M) in the CMIP5 ensemble. The results show a sustained upward trend in mean temperatures, consistent with what is known about the future of global climate change, with mean minimum temperatures rising at roughly twice the rate of maximum temperatures. The output from all models shows an increase in precipitation over land areas and decreases in sea surface temperature for RCP 4.5 (top) and 8.5 (bottom) across three time periods (near (2011–2040), medium (2036–2070) and far future (2071–2100). The ensemble mean of the CMIP5 models shows a decrease in precipitation for RCP 4.5 and an increase for RCP 8.5, with the exception of the MPI-ESM-LR model which projects no change.

16. On the other hand, future climate projections show generally mixed signals for precipitation, with variable results between the models at each time interval, and within each model across the three periods. This divergence in projected precipitation is also reflected by the ensemble mean of the CMIP5 models, where there are generally split between those predicting precipitation increase and decreases (Grimm et al., 2014). Figure B-4: Projected geographic trend in precipitation for RCP 4.5 (top) and 8.5 (bottom) across three time periods (near (2011–2040), medium (2036–2070) and far future (2071–2100). The ensemble mean of the CMIP5 models shows a decrease in precipitation for RCP 4.5 and an increase for RCP 8.5, with the exception of the MPI-ESM-LR model which projects no change.

17. Future projections for sea surface temperature: Increases in air temperature must first warm surface waters before this heat can be transferred into the ocean body and impact fish species at varying depths. According to the IPCC AR5, warming will extend to a depth of 200 m by mid-century (high confidence) with some models showing a deglacial transition (medium confidence), with warming extending to depths well below 2000 m (very high confidence). Ocean warming will continue over the 21st century (virtually certain), and will likely continue until at least 2300, even for low CO2 emissions scenarios.

Climate change impacts on the fisheries sector

18. Climate change significantly affects the fisheries sector through a composite pathway, impacting fish life cycle and their productivity, as well as the fisher folk’s activities, infrastructure and livelihoods. Ultimately, some of the greatest impacts of climate change on aquatic species are felt through the direct effects on fish habitat and food availability. The FAO has concluded that PRODEFISH should not target the open ocean for two reasons: (i) for some key species, such as tuna, there is no credible adaptation options; and (ii) for those climate change impacts that are amenable to adaptation, such as changes in the relative abundance of different fish stocks, adaptive measures can only be realized at a much

## Impact on mangroves

The mangrove ecosystems in the estuary and middle river segments play a pivotal role in the lifecycle of major Gambian shellfish species. They also capture and store sediments and thus enhance coastal protection, an essential contribution to climate resilience. Annexes 2, chapter 5, analyses the Gambia River and estuarine mangroves as well as their vulnerability to climate change.

The IPCC AR5 shows strengthened evidence for an increase in sea level of 0.18 m (0.13–0.24 m) by 2100 under RCP 6.0, compared with the current rate of rise between 0.38 m (0.29–0.55 m; likely range) and 0.77 m (0.63–1.02 m; likely range) (SSP5-8). The climate change impact assessment investigated the biological responses of the 28 most important shellfish species in the Gambia (see Annex 2 chapter 7 for full details).

Based on the results of the climate and hydrological model, the mangrove ecosystems are projected to be negatively impacted by one or multiple climate change stressors. The uncertainty in the future pace and magnitude of climate change, as well as the response time that should be allowed before implementing adaptation measures (e.g., increasing water circulation) could trigger a disproportionate, point-of-no-return biological response in some of the key fish species.

Some of these species are already under threat from overfishing and other human impacts such as pollution and distribution, and the negative effect on the mangrove ecosystems.

## Significant mangrove die-back over past fifty years. The significant mangrove die-back that happened as a result of the 1970's and 1980's drought, followed by recolonization of deforested areas by mangroves subsequently, is well-documented.

### 23. Unlike many other tropical mangrove countries, The Gambia has not suffered from large-scale conversion of mangrove forests to agriculture or aquaculture. Instead, the main causes of mangrove degradation are loss of freshwater flow into mangrove ecosystems have been fairly localized, and are not a major cause of mangrove degradation.

### 24. Main causes of mangrove degradation include soil wetness for poles and woodfuel, hence the importance of the proposed investment in obtaining secure community tenure over restored mangrove areas and building capacity to manage these areas sustainably through non-destructive income-generating activities based on mangrove ecosystem services (such as honey production) in order to strengthen economic incentives for continuing to manage restored mangrove areas after the end of the project.

### Impact on fisheries infrastructure (coastal and inland)

1. In addition to this impact pathway, it is important to note that climate change will also have other effects on the Gambian fisheries by damaging the existing coastal fisheries infrastructure and the inland aquaculture facilities. Economic losses as a result of climate-related risks and impacts are observed at all stages of the agri-food value chain, from production through processing and marketing. The proposed investment in restoring mangrove areas and building capacity to manage these areas sustainably will help protect and restore existing coastal fisheries infrastructure to ensure effective storage, processing, transportation and market access. ## The potential to develop climate resilience and reduce vulnerability by increasing climate resilience along the value chain is large.

The climate vulnerability assessments (see Table B-5 below and Annex II of this proposal) for each major landing site targeted by the project indicate that sea level rise, rise in extreme waves, rise in extreme salinity, changes in precipitation will contribute to the degradation of the existing coastal fisheries infrastructure. In turn, these impacts are estimated to result in reduced fish availability and increased fishing costs, which could lead to overfishing and trigger a vicious circle of over-exploitation and mismanagement (by fishing outside the season, using smaller nets than required by law, etc.). The project will work with local communities to develop adaptation measures that can help them to climate proof the artisanal fisheries infrastructure and value chain. To enhance the adaptive capacity and resilience of fisher communities, it is imperative to invest in adaptation measures to proactively plan based on outcomes.

### Impact on livelihoods and fisheries value chain

3. Climate hazards and impacts are also expected to impact the whole fish value chain. Table B-7 below presents the generic impacts of climate change on the agri-food value chain. PROFRESH will specifically address the ones related to the Gambia’s marine value chain by focusing on specific climate change events which are most impacting the targeted beneficiaries.

#### Table B-7 Generic Climate Change Impacts on Agri-Food Value Chain Stages Beyond Fish Production

| Climate hazard | Effects in Agri-Food Value Chain Stages Beyond Fish Production | Markers & retail impacts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Extreme heat | Reduced water availability, reduced soil fertility, increased evaporation, reduced crop yields, reduced livestock productivity, increased pest and disease spread. | Reduced yield of crops, reduced livestock productivity, increased pest and disease spread. |
| Drought | Stressed fields, poor growth due to drought stress, reduced access to irrigation resources, reduced access to fertilizers and other inputs, facilitated bacterial and fungal diseases, physical conditions of infrastructure, dries, reduced food availability, changes in market requirements. | Reduced yield of crops, reduced livestock productivity, increased pest and disease spread. |
| Heavy Rain and Flooding | Damage to infrastructure, loss of soil fertility, damage to agricultural infrastructure, Food spoilage, rapid deterioration of stored crops, facilitated bacterial and fungal spread. | Increased costs, physical conditions of infrastructure, dries, reduced food availability, changes in market requirements. |
| Storms with high winds | Reduced safety of the infrastructures, loss of livestock, damage to agricultural infrastructure | Unfavorable conditions to drive, increased costs, damage to access markets infrastructure. |
| Sea level rise and increased sea surface temperature, El Niño | Reduced capacity for fishery operations, increased marine biomass growth | Unfavorable conditions to drive, increased costs, damage to access markets infrastructure. |

4. Overall, the climate change impacts on the Gambian fisheries will translate into a considerable reduction in fish availability for the local population, for whom this resource represents both a livelihood and food security source. The project will work with local communities to develop adaptation measures that can help them to climate proof the artisanal fisheries infrastructure and value chain. To enhance the adaptive capacity and resilience of fisher communities, it is imperative to invest in adaptation measures to proactively plan based on outcomes.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## Table B-8 Project-specific climate change threats, associated impacts and adaptation measures needed

### Key climate change threats
- **Increased temperature:** Decreases in precipitation, impacting the Gambia River (source of fish) and mangroves (shelter habitats), particularly in areas relevant to artisanal fisheries.
- **Increased salinity (concentration and intrusion length):** Reduction of fish productivity, resulting in less capture for the artisanal sector.
- **Changes in ocean and river water temperature:** Development of aquaculture production and share reduction in total harvest losses.
- **Sea level rise and increase in extreme coastal weather:** Degradation of existing infrastructure and increased human losses.

### Adaptation measures needed
- **Restoration of mangroves and sustainable management.**
- **Development of aquaculture production and share reduction in total harvest losses.**
- **Climate proofing and improvement of small scale fisheries infrastructure.**

## Estimating the exact impact of climate change on the Gambian fisherfolk livelihoods (in terms of income and food nutrition/security) is difficult, due to the long and complex chain of causality from major climate change stressors to impacts on fish stocks and human livelihoods. Based on available data, preliminary estimates have been prepared, starting from the business-as-usual (BAU) situation as presently recorded and modeling the impact of climate change and of the project on the main outcomes of the fisheries and associated activities.

### As such, today's artisanal capture fisheries land about 54,000 t of fish through the landing sites on the coast, estuary and river. The majority (about 80%) is landed at the coastal sites. A significant proportion of these are lost during the landing, handling and processing activities. The losses are higher for the small pelagic fish and lower for the larger, more commercially valuable fish, but most of the volumes are closer to 30%. These losses are due to increased mortality in the capture phase (e.g., due to fishing gear damage), post-capture handling (e.g., recently poor ice melting). Increased exposure to storm waves and run-off from more frequent high rainfall events, and due to increased salinity. The total volume of fish landed is about 43,000 t annually.

### For human consumption is about 43,500 t annually; resulting in 18.4 kg/capita and a gross income of 1,080 US$/household per year.

## Key role of the fisheries sector and vulnerabilities of fisher folk to climate change

### 7 Over a medium term (20 years), climate change is estimated to reduce artisanal capture fisheries by at least 15% (Table B-6), given the fish species vulnerability to climate stressors presented above. More immediately, climate change stresses have already led to reduced catches and equipment at the landing sites, further increasing the vulnerability of fisher folk to climate change impacts.

### 8 Climate change will also reduce the number of days that fishing is possible (see Table B-13 for details). Figure B-2 shows how this will affect the number of fishing days in different project scenarios over the duration of the project, years 6, 11 and per capita fish availability.

#### Projected impacts on artisanal fisheries

* **Fisheries sector:** Climate change is projected to reduce climate change impacts. With project support, aquaculture is estimated to generate an additional 750 t per year. More importantly, the project investments at the landing sites will significantly reduce the post-landing losses (which would increase to 30% due to increased competition for fish stocks and reduced catches). This will also contribute to a faster stock restoration in key fisheries habitats which will contribute to the resilience of fish stocks under climate change pressures. Overall, the number of fishing days is projected to decrease by about 15%, while fish availability increases to some 46,500 t/year (about 23% more than BAU). In turn, this would also provide support for fisherfolk incomes and maintain per capita fish availability.

### Key role of the fisheries sector and vulnerabilities of fisher folk to climate change

#### 9 Agriculture, including fisheries, plays an important role in the Gambia's development, employing nearly half—46 percent—of the labor force and representing the source of livelihood for 80 percent of the rural population. For about 72 percent of poor households and 91 percent of extremely poor rural households, agriculture is their main income source.

#### 10 The fisheries sector is also a major income source of income for coastal fishing communities and are an important complementary activity for rural communities along The Gambia River. The 2016 Fishery Frame Survey (FIS) indicates that the majority of fishers in The Gambia are part-time fishermen, with about 75 percent working as part-time fishermen.

#### Profile of the fisheries value chain

* **Gambian fisheries can be divided into two broad sub-sectors: artisanal fisheries,** whose activities are dispersed across both marine and inland areas, labor intensive, and characterized by low capital investment, and aquaculture. The former is the most important sector in terms of employment (90 percent) and income generation (85 percent). Together with aquaculture, these two sub-sectors provide an average annual official landing of around 65,000 t, including both coastal marine and inland fishery. PROFISH2 will only focus on artisanal fisheries, in alignment with FAO's mandate to target the most vulnerable groups and communities for the local fisherfolk of The Gambia to benefit from the project activities (instead of other countries through export).

## B

### Figure 8-10 below outlines the flow of the fisheries value chain: Gambians consume about 32,000 t of fresh fish, smoked or dried fish per year for a total value of GMD 2,750 million (US$ 5.9 million). Nationally consumed fish is artisanally sourced and processed by fishermen, processors, fish traders or market operators. The remainder of the catch is not landed by the Gambian fishing fleet but is caught in international waters and/or catches abroad and inland processors operating in The Gambia export their products to full, although they source their raw materials from other countries. Exported fish and fish products amount to around 15,000 t per year generating GMD 3,350 million (US$ 6.7 million).

### Figure B-8 Overview of the fisheries and aquaculture value chains

The Gambian artisanal fisheries – the target of this project – are a multi-purpose activity associated with a host of ancillary activities such as fishing for recreational purposes, fish farming, processing, marketing, offloading of catch, onshore auctioning/marketing, processing (especially fish drying and fish smoking), both in-situ and at sea. The artisanal fisheries sector is the largest contributor to the national fish production and output which are sourced and supplied accordingly, linking fisheries to other sectors’ value chains. The artisanal sub-sector fully supplies the fresh and processed domestic fish market, as well as 43% of the unofficial export market.

### Figure B-8 Overview of the fisheries and aquaculture value chains

United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1994, the Kyoto Protocol in 2002, and the Paris Agreement in 2016. In fulfillment of the Gambia's obligations under the UNFCCC, The Gambia has submitted its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 2011, outlining appropriate measures to adapt to the impacts of climate change and to reduce emissions from the forest sector, and its first Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) in 2016, reiterates in 2021 with the Second NDC. According to Gambia's Second NDC, the country aims to achieve a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions by 35% below business as usual levels by 2030. The Gambia's NDC proposes investments to achieve annual emission reductions (ER) of 3.29 million tCO2e by 2030. Of these, 71% are conditional on the availability of concessional finance from international sources and 28% are conditional on the national budget, whereas 47% are conditional on development financing for their implementation.

### Climate-smart agriculture (csa) (objectives 1 & 2)

- **Objectives**: To increase agricultural productivity by 50%, to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from land use change and forestry by 30%, and to enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and fishers to climate change.
- **Investments**:
  - (objectives 1 & 2): Of these investments are market-based ("conditional", i.e., depending on concessional financing from international sources) and include: (i) 10,956 ha of land for the expansion of rice production; (ii) 34,875 ha of land for the expansion of maize production; (iii) 2,000 ha of land for the expansion of cotton production; (iv) 11,926 ICGE, accounting for 3.05% of the annual ER target.
- **The project presents mitigation benefits, primarily through the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from land use change and forestry, and the enhancement of climate change adaptation to artisanal fisheries. The Gambia has also submitted three National Communications (the last one in 2020) which document the status of socio-economic and ecological conditions as they relate to greenhouse gas emissions and sinks.

## Complementary projects related to interventions

### 19. This project will build on past and current funding proposals by various technical and financial partners. Two projects are particularly relevant for PROFRESH, in terms of synergies, co-financing, scaling-up and coordination.

### 20. For the first two packages under the aquaculture development intervention, PROFRESH will receive co-funding from the Food Security and Nutrition Project (FSNP) under the Ministry of Agriculture. ROOTS (a six-year project [2021-2027]) focused on improving the food security, nutrition and smallholder farmers' resilience to climate change in The Gambia. With a total budget of US$ 54 million, it is implemented by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) with co-financing from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), OFID and AFD. The Project Development Objective (PDO) is to increase agricultural productivity and access to markets for smallholder farmers in The Gambia. PROFRESH will benefit from the lessons learned, best practices and knowledge expected to be achieved through various interventions, including increasing agricultural productivity, adaptation to climate change and improving food security and nutrition policy dialogue. PROFRESH will also collaborate with the ROOTS project on the integration of fish production (fished by PROFRESH) with rice cultivation and vegetable gardening financed by the FAID Roots project.

### 21. PROFRESH will coordinate with the ongoing GCF project “Large-scale Ecosystem-based Adaptation in the Gambia River Basin: developing a climate resilient, natural resource based economy” (GPIF), implemented by UNDP and funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF). GPIF has received US$ 25.6 million for its first phase. It aims to improve livelihoods of over 100,000 people in The Gambia through sustainable natural resource management within and adjacent to 125 newly established Community Forest Reserves (CFRs) and County Protected Areas (CPAs). These areas are located across the three major river basins in The Gambia (the Upper River, Middle River and Lower River Regions). Component 1 focuses on restoring and building the Gambian natural resource base by transforming degraded land into productive forest landscapes (e.g., through the establishment of agroforestry systems, plantations, woodlands, and mangroves) using climate-resilient tree and shrub species across an area of at least 10,000 hectares. Based on the natural resources generated under the first component, Component 2 provides for the identification and implementation of appropriate policies and institutional arrangements to support sustainable land use management by climate change, through the establishment and strengthening of community-managed, natural resource-based landscapes (e.g., Community Forest Reserves). Component 3 focuses on strengthening the Gambian institution’s capacity for the implementation of existing policies for the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources, and for the UN Convention on Biological Diversity integration into these policies.

### 22. While targeting a different economic sector, PROFRESH will benefit from lessons learned in terms of the implementation of appropriate and marginalized restoration projects aimed at increasing the land-based capacity to adapt in The Gambia and engaging a wide range of national partners. In particular, the targeting of mangroves for climate change adaptation is an area where there are strong complementarity and synergies when possible. A relevant example of sustainable mangroves management linked with improved fisheries governance and value chains comes from the GEF-funded “Coastal Fisheries Initiative” in West Africa (CIFIAWA). CIFIAWA’s coastal restoration intervention is the GEF-UNEP “Landscape Planning and Restoration to Improve Ecosystem Services, and

## Prioritization of project areas and selection criteria of beneficiaries

26. Given the country's size and geography, the project interventions will cover the entire national territory.
With this in mind, and considering the results of the climate change vulnerability assessment in light of the findings on the impact of climate change on marine ecosystems presented in Annex I (see Annex 1 for full details), FAO has concluded that priority should be given to coastal areas. These are the most vulnerable parts of the country's territory to the effects of climate change, such as changes in marine currents and upwellings.
In addition, there are no credible adaptation options: either at a national level or within individual communities. The development of different fish stocks, adaptive measures can only be realized at a much larger scale than a single country’s Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ), especially for an EEZ like the Gambia's. Regional cooperation and stock management is required to ensure that the exploitation of fish stocks does not exceed the carrying capacity of a marine project like PROFISH+. Therefore, the project will geographically target the coastal areas, the estuary and the river – as well as inshore fisheries – for both aquaculture production with the cultivation and vegetable gardening (co-financed by the above-mentioned FADI ROOTS projects).

27. The prioritization of the project areas was driven by the following technical and beneficiary selection criteria, all of which are interlinked:

* For climate-proofing of artisanal fisheries infrastructure onshore, the coast, the Government has proposed the identification of six sites (see Annex 2). These sites were selected based on their relative strengths and weaknesses, as presented in Annex 2. At each of these landing sites, a climate change vulnerability assessment was conducted to identify the most vulnerable areas (e.g., increased frequency and intensity of extreme winds, increase in extreme wave heights, changes in precipitation). Based on the results (summarized in Table B-13 and further detailed in Annex A), six of the seven sites have been identified as being suitable for climate-proofing. The seventh site was not selected to accommodate fisher folk who will lose access to Bakau (no adaptation measures possible) and other nearby fishing areas, which are already overfished. This site is located on a small island in the artisanal sector and include a higher share of the total number of fisher folk. The main strategy for reducing vulnerability at this site would be to relocate the fishers from the island (a small rocky

## Adaptation responses are three-fold: first, increasing fish availability by reducing the high post-harvest fish losses currently experienced by the artisanal sector, which will be exacerbated by the climate vulnerability of the current infrastructure and equipment; second investment in the development of aquaculture production as an adaptation measure to compensate for the climate-induced decreases in capture fisheries. The third response is the rehabilitation of mangrove forests that play a key role as fisheries habitats.

### 1. Increasing fish availability

- **Information barriers**
    - Very limited data and analysis on CC impacts on the sector and weak knowledge on adaptation
        - Limited institutional memory has a great general understanding of climate change, but lack the capacity to collect additional data and conduct the necessary impact analysis.
        - Similarly, the public sector's ability to propose, implement and monitor adaptation measures is low and heavily dependent on donor funding. The private sector also lacks the capacity for this due to limited access to fisheries data collection and the development of early warning systems, yet these outputs are not being translated into action.
    - **Ambiguous separation between CC impacts and fisheries over-exploitation issues**
        - While the two issues are related, there is a lack of tools that can be used by fishers or other stakeholders to identify the root causes of main issues currently affecting the sector and to take appropriate action.
    - **Lack of systematic information relay mechanisms to reach fisher folk.** The MFV/HFMIN has a network of fisheries extension officers, yet their outreach is limited. The available information is complex and difficult for fishers to understand or use in practice. There are no systems in place to communicate with the local actors. Yet, there are no systems in place to reach out to fisher folk and disseminate information.
    - **Widespread lack of literacy and numeracy skills among women**. Women in the fishery sector have enough business acumen to generate more income than their husbands, but are forced to employ male intermediaries who often do not understand or communicate the value of the fishery-related information they receive. They do not venture into formal business (e.g., with hotels, catering companies, supermarkets) because of women's spheres, a perception that is aggravated by their inability to deal with various kinds of documents.

### 2. Technical barriers

- **Limited knowledge about technologies proven successful**. Several international or regional good practices and proven technologies for fish production have not yet been introduced. Most notably, the rice-fish integrated production has not been tried at scale, despite notable successes in neighboring countries and similar contexts. As such, fisheries officers and rice producers do not yet have the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

## B

### Institutional implementation capacity
The geographical size of the country and its economy has hindered technology transfer because (i) The Gambia's coastline is rather small compared with its neighbors, (ii) there are no large-scale aquaculture farms in the country, (iii) domestic public and private investments are very low or non-existent to contribute to technology development and diffusion. For example, in the past, the Gambia has not benefited from the type of donor-funded projects targeting technology development and transfer. The lack of a strong institutional framework for technology development and transfer is also reflected by the fact that there have been no private enterprises invested in the sector, bringing along new technologies.

### Weaknesses in project design and implementation
The weaknesses in project design and implementation are related to the following problems: Project donors-financed initiatives have attempted to introduce aquaculture activities, but design and implementation issues have not been adequately addressed. For example, the FAO funded a project on shrimp production that yielded both positive and unsatisfactory results. For example, the boreholes were not drilled deep enough and with increased salinity intrusion due to climate change; the ponds did not have the necessary infrastructure for water supply and drainage systems, which resulted in high mortality rates and water losses. The lessons learnt from past projects, including FAO aquaculture projects in The Gambia, are that there is a need to address the weaknesses of project design and implementation, such as weak institutional practices and addresses the weaknesses identified.

### Inadequate evaluation of quality inputs for aquaculture production
Recent investments in small-scale fish farming have been hampered by the fact that neither the hatchery nor the quality of inputs are reliable. The majority of the hatcheries are starting to provide locally produced inputs for aquaculture. Yet, domestic production of fingerlings and feed is insufficient to supply the demand. This has led to a situation where imported inputs are used in aquaculture systems, thereby increasing costs, access, increasing costs and not ensuring availability throughout the country. Higher costs associated with imports have also been linked to the lack of infrastructure for storage and distribution of investment costs. If both aquaculture and input production were to kick-started as proposed in the project, it would be possible to reduce the cost of inputs by 50% or more. This would provide cheaper access, while input producers would be sure to have a market for the products. FAO confirms that the inputs have been proven to be environmentally safe and sustainable.

### Lack of infrastructure for women's business
Women are the ones who are formally responsible for cash crops, extension services for women's production, and marketing. Infrastructure used by women in aquaculture is limited; this makes product quality control and running a business difficult. When their infrastructure receives investment, it is often assumed that the men will take over by them.

### Widespread lack of literacy and numeracy skills among women
Given illiteracy, sources of information for women are limited to their immediate social circles, which are comprised of women of similar economic status. The lack of access to relevant information is thus perpetuated.


## Financial barriers

* **No disposable income for investing and weak access to credit:** Gambian fish folk are amongst the poorest and most vulnerable groups, and as a result they cannot meet the high costs of up-front investments in aquaculture production systems. Women, in particular, while dominating the fish handling and processing segment, lack the necessary resources to invest in aquaculture production. The financial sector provides inadequate terms for agricultural financing, especially fisheries sub-sector, with high interest rates and limited access to credit. The informal sector is limited; mostly for crop production and not for fisheries related activities making the access to formal financial products even more difficult for poor and vulnerable households lacking the required collateral.


---
**Page 28**
---

### Financial leeway for women is much smaller than for men. There is a widespread lack of literacy and numeracy skills among women, causing difficult, if not impossible, access to formal finance. In addition, while women have complete autonomy over their incomes, they are the ones who pay for the everyday needs of the household, including school fees (men's contributions are on ad hoc bases).

#### Market barriers

- Poor quality standards for fish products and insufficient linkages between fish value chain producers and processors.
- Women lack access to credit and finance. The informal sector is dominated by inferior quality, which do not fully meet formal market specifications. Climate change, especially changes in precipitation, storms and increased temperature, are expected to further deteriorate the artisanal fishing sector. The lack of a strong women's organization or a well-structured and organized fisherman's union that can represent their interests.
- Gender-based segregation of markets. Women serve the domestic market with low profit margins, while men dominate export markets. The export business is more formal than local business, and hence the market segmentation stems from women’s lack of literacy and numeracy skills and lack of access to infrastructure required.

#### Social barriers

- Enrenched patriarchal norms among all in the society. Not only men, but also most women think men are justified in beating women when men are dissatisfied with how the household is run or their contributions are not recognized.
- Women are expected to be responsible for domestic chores and child-rearing as well as the financial responsibilities related to household. Boys are given priority in education, and it is accepted that they engage in the most prestigious type of work and are given the most attractive production activities. This means that women are often left with less productive activities.
- Gender-based segregation of markets. Women serve the domestic market with low profit margins, while men dominate export markets. The export business is more formal than local business, and hence the market segmentation stems from women’s lack of literacy and numeracy skills and lack of access to infrastructure required.

#### Institutional barriers

- Early marriages for girls borne by lack of viable alternatives in rural areas. As girls in the rural areas are not given any education or training, the only option left for them is usually early marriage, which perpetuates the vicious circle for women.
- Insufficient capacity for natural resource monitoring and management. The available public funding for fisheries’ technical capacity is often insufficient to meet the agreed upon mandates in the fisheries, water resources, environmental sector. Climate change is introducing an additional burden on the line ministries responsible for fisheries, water resources and environment, which are already stretched thin.
- In particular, the ministries lack capacity to act on their broad understanding of CC impacts on the fisheries sector and develop specific strategic and advisory actions. For example there is a broad understanding that climate change will have significant impacts on mangrove forests. If they do so, affecting mangroves and fish, but due to a lack of monitoring capacity, the exact extent is not known. The same applies for other coastal ecosystems such as coral reefs and estuaries.
- Lack of coordination between different sectors. For example, in the Gambia, the fisheries sector is dependent on multiple factors, which are often under the jurisdiction of different line ministries. For example, in a case study, no systematic approach or mechanism exists to bring together fisheries, agriculture, forestry, environment and trade and economic decisions makers to introduce climate change considerations in planning and implementation of public policy and investments.

## B

### Target beneficiaries and formulators of policies, plans and interventions

The project seeks to contribute to all aspects of climate resilience of Caribbean fishers, first by investing in the capacity of fishers and their families to adapt to climate change through improved access to fishing gear and fishing boats affected by climate change and in equipping women with the fundamental skills of literacy and numeracy, then by supporting the development of a sustainable aquaculture sector that will provide an alternative livelihood for fishers and their families. This will be achieved by improving water quality (through the construction and improvement of sewage systems) and improving meshing and drying processes conducted by women followed by supporting the adaptation and improvement of livelihoods through aquaculture development in order to respond to the decrease in capture fisheries. The project will also support the development of a sustainable fishery sector that will provide an alternative livelihood for fishers and their families, while at the same time creating new opportunities for employment and income generation.

The intervention logic of the project will follow three main streams. On a first hand (summarizing the stream of activities under component 1), the project will support the development of fisheries communities in areas with high biodiversity, coupled with activities aimed to strengthen local community capacities to manage natural resource base. This in turn will lead to the creation of new opportunities for employment and income generation, while at the same time sustainably managing these fisheries habitat (outcome 1). In parallel, the forests will generate a positive net increase in carbon sequestration (outcome 2). Jointly with other interventions, this stream of activities under component 1 will result in increased resilience to climate change for fishers and their families (outcome 3).

As second stream of activities (in component 2), the project will provide the fisheries communities with the required skills to develop the scale fisheries infrastructure in a climate adaptive fashion and develop capacities to perform aquaculture as visible economic activity. As a result, fisheries communities will be equipped with and able to use the necessary tools to adapt their livelihoods to climate change (outcome 4). This will also contribute to the adaptation option, thus making fisheries communities more resilient (outcome 3). The improved equipment - especially the new boats - will reduce the exposure of fishers to adverse weather conditions and other hazards, while reducing the risks around the sites, reducing the risk of air borne diseases for the fishers involved in the smoking activities (which represents one of the co-benefits of the project – co-benefit 1).

The third stream of activities (in component 3) will target the enabling environment to ensure that artisanal fisheries communities have stronger capacities to adapt climate change options, including higher participation in markets and we target both public and private actors (outcome 4). Specifically, this will be supported by activities

aimed to align national institutions, policies and regulations to respond to climate risks, to ensure that public services (related to forestry and fisheries resources) are better equipped to support communities in their climate change adaptation patterns, as well as for activities specifically dedicated to strengthening local communities capacity to adapt climate change impacts. The project will also contribute to the reduction of gender-based violence by strengthening women’s economic empowerment and access to financial services. It will also specifically aim to improve gender equality, both intra-households and within the society, by promoting household members’ participation in decision-making processes and by providing training on gender equality.

# B3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

## Project objective

The project aims to build the resilience of the Gambian artisanal fisher folk in the face of climate change and increasing livelihoods. Throughout this proposal, fisher folk are defined as all the fisheries value chain actors, from fishermen, to traders, processors, providers of inputs, etc., both existing and new to the business, e.g. women who have recently started fishing or processing activities.

The project will be implemented in three inter-linked components, with a particular focus on value chain segments dominated by women (fish handling and processing). The project will also address the need for improved access to markets and services, including financial services, through aquaculture. In order to build on the knowledge and experience of the local beneficiaries, they will be consulted prior to and during planned planning and execution of all activities. Specific measures to involved women are found in the Gender Action Plan.

## Project components

### Component 1: Strengthening restoration capacity and community management of artisanal fisheries habitats

Component 1 is designed to reverse the degradation of mangrove ecosystems in key fisheries areas by investing in restoration through repopulating and assisted natural regeneration of more salt-tolerant species, coupled with support to sustainable ecosystem management. As detailed in the climate rationale, mangroves play a key role in the project area as they provide essential services for coastal communities, including protection against erosion and storm surges. Mangrove degradation has continuously reduced the mangrove cover over the last 15 years, at an average rate of 1.5% per year, for a total loss of approximately 20%. This is having a significant impact on fishery productivity by protecting essential fish habitat as well as generating greenhouse gas emissions: estimated annual reductions in CO2e emissions of 11,926 tCO2e (for a total of 238,519 tCO2e) over the 20-year lifetime of the project (where restoration of mangroves is completed).

### Output 1. Mangrove ecosystems sustainably restored in fisheries priority areas

Mangrove forests trap sediments and so doing protect coasts and riversheds against erosion. They are thus essential for reducing the impacts of climate change, such as sea-level rise and increased storm surges. Mangroves also provide a habitat for many species of fish and other marine animals.

While mangrove ecosystems as a whole are highly resilient to changes in salinity, many individual mangrove trees can be killed by saltwater intrusion. This is particularly the case when there is an increase in freshwater vegetation and associated fish and shellfish species. This also provides an important rationale for mangrove restoration with a mix of species, including more salt-tolerant species, as envisaged by the project. For each particular project site...

**Page 35**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

B

Those mangroves most suitable to the current and expected conditions will be selected. Mangrove trees in unused that seeds into seedlings of up to 30 cm long, called propagules, while they are still attached to the parent. Therefore, for mangrove reforestation no nurseries are required – participating communities will collect the propagules from the forest floor or from fallen trees. Propagules should be collected as soon as possible after the fruits have fully fallen. Propagules should preferably be planted the same day that they are collected; but if this is not possible, they can be stored in a cool place (e.g., under a tree) for up to 24 hours before planting. The propagules will be transported with similar ecological conditions and transported in sacks, by boat or by vehicle, depending on the location. Building on the success of the PROGREDI project, the FAO will continue to support the Government of Gambia in its forest-driven restoration approach, working together with the local populations as detailed below, as well as invest in post-restoration monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities. The project will also focus on PROGREDI ON THE GROUND, a growing mangrove-restoration initiative to avoid duplication of efforts.

The project is aimed at mangrove restoration of 2,350 ha of 1 ha of full forest regeneration and 1,200 ha of assisted natural regeneration (ANR) on 10 areas prioritized during project implementation.

## The prioritization of areas was conducted in several steps:

### a. Identification of mangrove areas most relevant to fisheries:
While all mangroves in the Gambia can be considered important for fish and wildlife life cycle, certain species of mangroves are more critical for the fish and wildlife life cycle – in particular in the estuary/river mouth and the middle river segments. These areas are also more vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increased frequency of the identified degradation (mostly/mostly 30% of total degradation, middle river 60% of total degradation). In addition, these areas are critical for fish and wildlife life cycle in terms of nursery grounds, feeding grounds, and breeding grounds. Mangroves are also more vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise and coastal erosion especially in the estuary; increased sedimentation, salt intrusion and salinity increase in the middle river.

### b. Identification of mangrove degradation/fisheries habitat loss areas:
In River Mouth and Middle River segments, both segments, the priority was given to areas directly on the Gambia River (and not on the floodplains) and to waterfowl areas (and not to upland mangroves), given the focus on fisheries habitats.

### c. Identification of sites in non-protected mangrove areas:
Given the community-led approach and the approach of involving communities, the priority was given to sites that fall outside the existing protected areas (for reference, marked in different colors on Map 1).

## The project will also a community-led restoration approach, working with the respective local communities at the 10 identified areas.

### Under Activity 1.1.1:
Reforestation of 1 ha of degraded mangrove area in key fisheries hotspots excluding protected areas and other sites that are already under rehabilitation or reforestation activities will be done by the communities themselves, based on their own knowledge and the identification of locally appropriate reforestation measures. The selection of the sites will be done by FAO personnel in close consultation with the communities. With support of experts mobilized locally, the FAO will carry out a series of training sessions for the communities (and other stakeholders) to identify the most suitable species (communities) and training with each of 10 identified communities, as well as a “rehabilitation course” the subsequent year. Training will be conducted in two phases: first, one community per quarter during the first three years; secondly, four to five, aligned with the community training sub-activities under 1.1.2. The project will fund procurement, transport and installation of equipment and materials for reforestation activities (e.g., seedlings, tools, etc.). Five communities will be trained in year three, plus two more communities in year four. The project will fund procurement, transport and installation of equipment and materials for reforestation activities (e.g., seedlings, tools, etc.).

The project will fund the planting campaigns in close consultation with the FAO. Mangrove planting will be done through local unskilled community labour on a rolling basis, as per the training provided by the communities. The project will fund procurement of materials and equipment for consultations at all stages, but in particular for site selection, training and monitoring, with the possibility of being engaged in other activities such as seedling production, nursery establishment, etc., as well as the costs associated in site selection, mangrove propagate collection as well as planting campaigns and seedling survival monitoring efforts.

### Activity 1.1.2 Support assisted natural regeneration (ANR) on 125 ha of moderately degraded mangroves in key reference locations (executed by FAO and The Government of the Gambia acting through MoA)

The emphasis will be on the rehabilitation of moderately degraded mangroves rather than on outer reforestation of areas that are already heavily degraded. ANR is a natural regeneration process where seeds from mature trees fall to the ground, germinate in situ, and grow into new trees. The seedlings develop into small mangrove plants with characteristics of mangrove trees. When the mangrove propagule (seedling) that develops from seed while still attached to the tree matures, it drops into the water and remains dormant until washed up by the sea water or a river. The seedlings are then transported to suitable locations where they can be planted in gaps in the mangrove vegetation.

The ANR process is similar to other afforestation efforts such as enrichment planting in gaps in the mangrove vegetation. The approach will be similar to Activity 1.1.1, with the following differences: (i) the ANR sites are not pre-identified but rather selected by FAO and MoA based on community consultations; (ii) the ANR sites will be identified as "reforestation zones" in the project area; (iii) the ANR sites will be established through a series of activities including seed collection, nursery establishment, planting, etc. (iv) the ANR sites will not require protective measures, etc.). FAO will first identify precise ANR sites and specific ANR measures required in each site (5 days per 100 ha) in consultation with the local communities. With support of experts mobilized locally, FAO will carry out the activities on the ground. The ANR sites will be established as "reforestation zones" within the project area.

The ANR sites will be referred to as a "reforestation course" the subsequent year. Training will be done on a rolling basis, starting with 2 communities in year one and expanding to all communities by year three. The training will include seed collection, nursery establishment, planting, etc., and will be conducted through community-based training sessions.

The unsolicited labour required for mangrove restoration will be procured by the project (as casual labourers) and sourced from the related communities. In order to avoid unlawful of the project activities through illegal tree felling, sand mining, etc., monitoring will be conducted by the community members with strong involvement of local authorities.

## Component 2: Climate resilient fisheries infrastructure and aquaculture

### 51. Component 2 aims to improve fish availability and provide additional income to fisher folk by improving the small-scale fishing infrastructure used by the communities, and by promoting aquaculture production through different technologies. Please refer to the feasibility study part 2 for technical description of the infrastructures.

#### Output 2.1 Fisheries communities are equipped with climate-proofed and improved critical small scale fisheries infrastructure (e.g., fish landing sites, fish processing facilities, fish drying areas, fish driers and improved ponds).

As described in the content and climate resilience section, the fish harvested in the Gambia is highly dependent on capital inputs. Fishery consumption is exclusively caught by artisanal fishermen. Their catches are landed at an estimated 132 sites across the country, yet the majority of the landed volumes are concentrated in 10 landing sites. These landing sites are located along the river banks and are vulnerable to flooding during high tides and water inflows, and changes in high rainfall events and the resulting run-off; these landing sites are the ones most at risk from climate change impacts. The Gambia has a very small aquaculture sector with no established fish farming operations and landings continue to increase leading fish-basins. In this context, the Government of the Gambia originally proposed to focus on one of the most important and vulnerable sites (namely commercial landing site at Bakau) as an adaptation measure.

Subsequently, a detailed assessment was conducted in order to assess vulnerability of these fish landing sites and identified climate change and other challenges as well as, to the extent possible, detailed information about the existing infrastructure and its capacity. The assessment also included an analysis of investment needs. Six of the seven sites assessed were retained for project investment, based on identified vulnerabilities and opportunities. Bakau was not included in this list because it is already being used as an adaptation measure to accommodate the loss of landing capacities at Bakau and other nearby sites.

The project will invest in climate adaptation measures (climate-proofing of landing sites) and in the introduction of new technologies for fish processing, including a floating fish processing facility. The project will also invest one river landing site (on the Biringa Major, a tributary of the Gambia River) (see Map 2). The existing climate exposed fish smoking and drying facilities and equipment will be replaced with improved, climate-resilient technologies (e.g., solar-powered dryers, cold storage units, etc.). A floating fish processing facility will also be introduced to fish rising lines. The FTV uses well-proven technology that reduces the level of Polyacryllic Amorhizations (PAA) in fish products and is a cost-effective alternative for smoking and drying fish.

### Activity 2.1.1 Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), detailed design and supervision for all fish landing sites executed by FAO covers the design and supervision work for all the construction activities at the seven fish landing sites, including the installation of fish smoking units, dryers and rinsing sinks. - The project will provide capacity development through training for the following: a representative of the private sector industry groups, a representative of the Village Development Committee, and the Village Head (Alkalo) as Chairperson.
- Each CFC employs people from the community on a casual or permanent basis including a secretary, a watchman, a cleaner and a pump mechanic. Under Activity 2.1.7 Introduce energy efficient technologies for fish handling and processing in 10 mangrove management communities** executed by FAO, the project will support the mangrove communities supported under component 1 to introduce energy efficient technologies for fish handling and processing such as small scale solar powered ice making units, cold storage facilities, and other energy efficient equipment for fish processing operations. **64. Overall, the climate-proofing and improvement of critical small-scale fisheries infrastructure at these landing sites will contribute to the resilience of the livelihoods of artisanal fisherfolk in the mangrove communities since the increase of the both infrastructure and the livelihoods of the artisanal fisher folk. For each of the fish landing sites supported by the project, specific climate vulnerabilities have been identified and the proposed investments are designed to be resilient against these projected climate change impacts (see section 2.1.4 vulnerability assessment in section B). For example, to build resilience against sea level rise and beach erosion, defences such as seawalls, groynes, breakwaters, and other structures will be installed along the shoreline or near the shore, including raised platforms. Against increased extreme waves and winds, and changes in precipitation patterns, investments have been designed to be resilient against these projected climate change impacts. These investments have been designed to be resilient to the projected climate change for a period of 20-25 years. Lessons learned from the 2016 IFAD Country Portfolio Evaluation for The Gambia have been integrated in this activity, as follows: (i) use of concrete instead of wood and bamboo; (ii) use of non-corrosive materials such as stainless steel or galvanized iron; (iii) use of salt-tolerant cement will be used where necessary, and rust resistant steel rather than rust vulnerable iron. The estimated cost for the construction of these structures is US$ 10 per ton/year with a reduction in PHL from current levels of 20-30%, PHL to 5% and an increase in the quality of the processed fish (smoked and dried) in the project area. This will result in an increase in the quantity of fish produced, a 10% decrease in PHL, which would amount to 3,500 tons per year, with an approximate market value of US$ 3.5 million.

## Introduction and adoption of the four aquaculture packages mentioned above is contingent on the increased availability of domestically produced fingerlings (juvenile fish) and fish feed.

### As such, this project will contribute to its success by supporting the expansion of the Centre for Aquaculture Development under activity 2.3.1 of FAD. The Jaha Centre is run by the government, with the most recent investments (habilitation) provided by FAD, and recurrent costs come from the Government of Gambia through a grant from the African Development Bank (AfDB). The Centre has been in a non-producing state. The water supply of the centre originates from a borehole, installed by FAD, which is essential for fish production. In 2013, the borehole was damaged and the Centre ended up in a state of disrepair, being brought back to its original capacity. Over the years, the positive experience with integrated rice-culture-fish farming elsewhere in West Africa, the history and demonstration capacity have to be taken into account when considering the potential for aquaculture development in Gambia. To increase production capacity, the centre needs additional ponds and start-ups (imported quality fingerlings and feed), as current public resources are insufficient to meet demand. The Centre has a capacity of 10 tonnes per year of fish produced to market. To produce and deliver increased numbers of catfish and tilapia fingerlings to meet the demand generated by project investments, the Centre needs additional ponds and start-ups (imported quality fingerlings and feed). The Centre will play a much more active role in the scaling-up of smallholder aquaculture investment in the Gambia. In year 1, except where explicitly indicated (e.g., financed and executed by the Government of the Gambia acting through the Ministry of Agriculture), all project activities are to be financed by FAD and executed by FAO.

### In parallel, under Activity 2.3.2 Promote the increase of fish feed production through their manufacturing units executed by FAO, the project will support the increase in capacity of fish feed production from four small-scale private manufacturing units (two existing units and two new ones). The outputs of the Centre and of the feed production units are expected to be marketed locally and regionally. In addition to the Centre for Aquaculture Development, FAD, through its Technical Cooperation Programme, has provided two small-scale manufacturing units to the Gambia Fish Feed Company (GFFC) in 2013. The GFCC is a private company that produces fish feed. A third small-scale hatchery has been provided with fish feed manufacturing units. As fish feeds require 30-35% fishmeal and fish oil for optimal growth performance, alternative sources of protein are required to reduce the dependency on imported fish meal. This project will be executed in close collaboration with the African Catfish Clariapia group, reducing fish meal dependency by using locally available resources.

69. It may be clear that the above description of fish feed production is of a small-scale operation. There is no indication that this type of activity could not be carried out on a larger scale. The natural water will be used to water and fertilize the crops. This will reduce the use of (fertilizer) ferrous to be purchased by the gardens considerably. The fish will be introduced into the water body through a specially designed pipe that will allow for the intrusion study for the targeting of borehole sites. Aquifer level and salinity will be monitored to avoid environmental problems in the future. The project will also benefit from the experience gained during the implementation of the FAO funded projects.

72. FAO has introduced the smallholder cultivation of Alosa alosa, Chilean characin. In some countries, this species is known as the "rainbow fish" because it swims at the atmospheric level on the water surface. As a result, the fish can be kept in tanks with water that is low in dissolved oxygen. The behavior of this species allows for high fish densities, as are common in aquaculture systems.

Fish are fed to saturate to optimize growth performance. Obviously, the fish excrements (digested food) will enter the water, but they have a good nutritional value, especially for crops. Some of the tank water will have to be replaced from time to time and this will be used in agriculture. The use of fish excrement as fertilizer is common in aquaculture; the effluent from these small tanks does not contain any harmful substances such as antibiotics. This method has been successfully applied by FAO in several countries, including Ethiopia.

The project beneficiaries obtain a financial gain by reducing the use of artificial fertilizers for the crops in the vegetable gardens, with the exception of those that are grown on the land where fish are reared. The water will be used for irrigation and for inorganic fertilizer and the absence of run-off and water pollution. This activity, which will have 900 beneficiaries (at tenas), 30 beneficiaries per horticultural garden, will be implemented in year 2 to year 5, to allow prior up-scaling of fish production.

The project will be implemented as a joint venture between the Government of Ethiopia and FAO. The project is funded by GCF and executed by FAO. The project will be implemented through the Jaha Centrality and executed by the Government of the Gambia acting through MoA or MiFWARN, and FAO financed and executed). The activities under this component are intended to be financed by GCF and executed by FAO. The activity will comprise the following sub-activities:

* 2.2.3.1 Construction and infrastructure works (co-funded and executed by the Government of the Gambia acting through MoA)
* 2.2.3.2 Provide fish tanks, farming ovens, shelters and start-up aquaculture kits (tools and inputs)
* 2.2.3.3 Training on integrated rice-fish culture and smoking, use of water with fish excrements as fertilizer in horticulture, and negotiation skills.

### Under Activity 2.2.4

The project will finance technical assistance for developing the adjusted design and technical specifications for integrated rice-fish culture, will equip the additional cost of the works, will provide start-up kits (tools and inputs include seedbeds, wheelbarrows, wettirning boots, gloves and other protective clothing, bioconcepts, seine nets, electronic balances) to beneficiaries.

73. Under Activity 2.2.4 introduced integrated rice-fish culture in 300 ha of new irrigated schemes (Aquaculture Project 2) executed by FAO and the Government of the Gambia acting through MoA, the project will set up a pilot project for the development of integrated rice-fish culture on 15 hectares of land within the framework of the Jaha Centrality. The project will be implemented in collaboration with the beneficiaries those chosen by the Jaha Centrality, to ensure easy access to inputs and training for the beneficiaries.

74. The rice plants will be planted in the fields (and partly submerged). The fish will finish growing among the plants and eat the insects that are found on them. This method of rearing fish is called "rice-fish culture". It will fertilize the crops, benefiting the plants considerably. With time, the farmers will find a balance between the number of fish and the amount of rice to be planted in each field. The fish will be fed with fish food and other organic materials such as fish meal or external inputs can be made by the combination of rice and fish. Eventually the fish and rice culture will not require any chemical fertilizers, pesticides or herbicides.

The water level in the fields will be kept at a very low levels; the fish will withdraw into their refuge canals and pond. The pond will be deep bigger than the refuge canals so that it is possible to harvest the rice when harvested. Depending on the size of the paddy fields and the fish density it may be desirable to supply irrigation water from time to time.

**B**

nature has vast potential as they are not exploited everywhere. The proposed project will introduce artificial substrates near the mangroves forests to collect spat fall (the gonadal products of oysters after mass spawning) and to manipulate the density of oyster larvae to achieve an optimal oyster harvest. The advantage will be that in case the environment is not suitable for oyster culture, the collected spat can be used as a substrate for other species such as prawns or shrimp. This low-input form of aquaculture (no feed required) is obviously an interesting form of secondary production and it will also provide additional income to the local communities. The project will also include a FAO intervention (FishAACIP) to strengthen the value chain of oysters. Furthermore, the project will improve the safety and security of women in coastal areas by providing them with training on clam culture and cockle culture under certain circumstances in deep waters without any safety gear like life jackets. The project will undertake juvenile clam collection from the wild and train 30 women's groups in clam and cockle culture on mussel bags. These women will follow tidal movements during which they collect clams and feed them to grow a certain harvestable size, again without the need for any supplementary feed. Women collecting these organisms will be able to continue their activities under certain circumstances in deep waters with no safety gear like life jackets. The project will also increase the densities of the bivalves will be created. As the women's groups have positive experiences with managing bivalve resources, they will be encouraged to participate in the mangrove restoration activities in Component 1 and 2. In addition, the project will train 30 women's producer groups in 40 communities in total, of which 30 are in oyster culture production and 10 in oyster processing. The training will include the following sub-activities: 2.2.6.1 Train 30 women's groups in clam and cockle culture on mussel bags and train 10 women's groups in oyster culture production and recognition skills.
