# Output 3.1 Public services strengthened to facilitate local communities capacity to prepare for and respond to climate change risks

81. This sub-component will focus on enabling key public services to better support the fisheries sector adaptation to climate change impacts, specifically the Ministry of Fisheries, Water Resources and National Assembly Matters (MoFWARNAM), Departments of Fisheries and Water Resources and the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Natural Resources (MECCNRN) in developing a national climate change action plan. The project will strengthen skills and build capacities of line ministries and agencies to better analyse and include climate risks in national policy and decision making processes. The project will also support the development of sectoral adaptation plans for fisheries that have capacities and implement monitoring of fisheries sector and the associated ecosystems, and will support transmitting the required knowledge and capacities to the beneficiaries: farmers, fisherfolk and communities via an e-learning platform.

82. In particular, under Activity 3.1.1 Strengthen the sectoral institutions and policies and develop additional capacity to respond to climate risks explicitly by FAO and MoFWARNAM, in collaboration with the Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources and MECCNRN, the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Natural Resources (MECCNRN) Departments of Forestry and Parks and Wildlife (DFP and WPW) to gather and analyze data on climate change impacts and adaptation needs. The project will also support the development of sectoral policies that finance short-term specialized trainings on climate risks, climate analysis and mainstreaming climate change in national policy and decision making processes. In addition, the project will support the development of a national action plan for climate change and natural resources, the project will facilitate inclusive policy dialogues and facilitate collaborations among key stakeholders to ensure that the fisheries sector is better prepared to respond to climate risks and improve quality of forest cover, including through policy and knowledge sharing opportunities. The project will also collaborate with national training and education institutions on updating or developing the curriculum on fisheries, aquaculture and environmental management in line with climate change adaptation needs. The project will support the development of specialized training on climate change and fisheries topics for key staff in the ministries. In addition, the project will set up a national e-learning platform to disseminate knowledge and skills related to climate change impacts and example climate-resilient construction standards for fish landing sites and related infrastructure. Women and female youth are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts and their needs must be addressed through trainings and workshops, including on sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment. Women and female youth experts/practitioners will be consulted on the development of updated curricula on fisheries, aquaculture and ecosystem management and discussions with women and female youth representatives will be held during training sessions. The implementation of national policies and strategies will include detailed analysis on and proposed strategies for women, including reducing their vulnerability to climate change impacts through capacity building and awareness raising activities in Gambia acting through MoF or MoFWARNAM, and FAO financed and executed (i.e., all sub-activities described in the following paragraph are intended to be financed by UFO and executed via FAO). This activity will comprise the following sub-activities:

## Under Activity 3.2.1: Capacity development for field fisheries and forestry offices to support fisheries, aquaculture and mangrove investment executed by FAO and MoFWRMN

The focus of this project will be to build capacity in the field fisheries and forestry offices (FFOs) at the national level to provide technical assistance and implement projects related to fisheries, aquaculture and mangrove management. The FFOs will provide implementation support to the project activities and to accompany other public and private initiatives in the field of fisheries, aquaculture and mangrove management. Female and female youth officers will be selected on selection (accuracy, utility of data generated, operation, maintenance, budget, etc.) and will be trained in the field of fisheries, aquaculture and mangrove management. **Under Activity 3.2.3 Support to gender and youth empowerment through the application of the Household Methodology executed by FAO:** The project will introduce and use IFAD's Household Methodology[1] in order to empower women and female youth, and to provide them with skills for income generation and to implement climate change adaptation measures in the artisanal fisheries sector in the Gambia. The Household Methodology recognizes that promoting gender equality cannot be done solely through working with women; therefore it must also include men. Women will be trained on how to use the methodology as a tool for empowerment, and in household decision-making. The methodology will be rolled out through a "training of trainers" approach, first training the women and female youth, then training the men. Women and female youth will be empowered against violence, abuse and harassment, and community gatekeepers will also be sensitized by the Household Methodology trainers serving as facilitators. This activity will be implemented via the following sub-activities: The household methodology will target a total of 2,000 households[1] and 2 community gatekeepers per session, four sessions a month, five months for a year three years (total of 36 sessions). Except where explicitly indicated (e.g., as financed and executed by MuFWRMAN or through MoKH and MuFWRMAN), and FAO financed and executed), all sub-activities described in the following paragraphs are intended to be funded by GCF and executed by FAO. This activity will be structured in the following sub-activities:

32.2.1 Household Methodology Training of trainers (co-funded and executed by FAO)

32.2.2 Household Methodology large scale training

## Accredited Entity (AE)

93. FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project. As such, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight of project implementation; (iii) ensuring that the project is on track to achieve its expected results and achieve objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting required under GCF’s (pro) project cycle management framework; and (v) reporting in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF.

94. The GCF and FAO will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA), under which FAO shall administer the related financial resources on behalf of GCF, as well as ensure that all project activities are carried out. Accountability for the use of financial resources will be assured through the review of annual and bi-annual project reports, as well as through monitoring reports. These reports will include environmental and social performance.

95. FAO role as AE will be attributed to the relevant offices and divisions in FAO Headquarters located in Rome, Italy (the Lead Technical Officer (LTO)), and in the Field Offices (FOs) located in Dar es Salaam, Country, and the Country Representation Office for Project Country (FAO-The Gambia).

96. In order to fulfill the AE functions, FAO will set up a dedicated Project Task Force (PTF) in line with project financial guidelines. The PTF will be composed with the Budget Holder (BH), the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Logistics and Financial Management Team (FLMT), and other relevant staff as needed. The PTF will design a dedicated national climate finance specialist and project assistant to ensure the AE function on the ground.

97. A Lead Technical Officer (LTO) will be appointed in the regional office, and will be responsible for the technical supervision and oversight of project activities throughout project implementation. More specifically, the LTO will have overall responsibility for ensuring that the project is implemented according to the approved project design advantage for projects and to separate the function of FAO as its role as AE and as Executing Entity (EE). The LTO will oversee and carry out technical backstopping to the project implementation. The LTO will support the Budget Holder (BH) in preparing the Project Implementation Plan (PIP), including the preparation of the Annual Budget (ABB), including work plan and budget revisions. The LTO is responsible and accountable for providing technical assistance and advice on project design, planning, monitoring and evaluation, as well as through supervision missions (shall be called other experts to participate and advise) will provide technical backstopping to the Project Implementation Management Team to ensure the delivery of quality technical outputs. The LTO will coordinate the provision of appropriate technical support from the PTF in response to requests from the Project Steering Committee (PSC).

98. The PFT will remain independent of the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAO (see project execution guidelines below). In line with GCF policy, and adopting the GCF Board Decision B6/105, the above mentioned responsibilities of the LTO shall ensure that only the Project Implementation Management Team performs the types of AE functions listed in the GCF General principles and indicative list of eligible costs covered under GCF fees and project management costs.

**Executing Entities:**

104\. The project will be executed by the Government of the Gambia acting through MoA and MoFWRNAM and FAO in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the Project Agreement. The Government of the Gambia is a country-driven execution of project activities. As Executing Entity, FAO will act through the FAD Representation in The Gambia; it will be responsible for the management of the GCF proceeds and it will bear the overall responsibility for fulfilling the terms and conditions set out in the Project Agreement. The Government of the Gambia will employ a FAO supervised consultants and procured parties. FAOs mandate as a global stakeholder in the field of agriculture, forestry and fisheries is to provide technical assistance and complementary services including technical assistance and quality assurance.

105\. FAO Gambias has a strong, demonstrated record in which the Government of the Gambia, through the co-financing mechanism, has provided vital support to the sustainable development of the agriculture and natural resource sectors in the country, having implemented 130 projects. The Organization is currently supporting the Government of the Gambia with the implementation of the National Agriculture Investment Plan (NAIP) for food security and resilience in the agriculture, forestry and fisheries sector. FAO assistance in the Gambia is shaping by the 2018-2027 Country Strategic Plan (CSP), which has four main strategic objectives: 1) Strengthened agricultural development and capacity building for increased, sustainable and diversified agricultural and fisheries production and nutrition; 2) Sustainable natural resources management for climate change adaptation and mitigation; 3) Strengthened food security and resilience in the agriculture, forestry and fisheries sector; and 4) Strengthened resilience and capacities for disaster risk reduction and management, and climate change adaptation. The CSP is aligned with the Gambia’s National Development Plan (2018-2025), FAO Country Programme Document (CPD) and the Technical Cooperation Programmes (TCPs) focusing on strengthening the capacity of MoFWRNAM in its vessel monitoring capabilities, on rehabilitating the Jahally hatchery, on investing in small-scale fish feed mills and禁烟吸烟者。

110. The PMU will coordinate directly with the TAC and the PSG. The PMU will facilitate a operational & financial management system in collaboration with the TAC and will provide support to the TAC on all operational and administrative aspects of the project (such as procurement plans, financial plans, periodic reporting etc.). It will be cleared with the TAC issues for special consideration beyond the mandate of the TAC will be brought to the PSG.

### Project Governance

111. A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established to provide strategic guidance for the project. The NPC of the PMU for this project and the PD as acting as Rapporteur to the PSC. The PSC will be composed of high-level representatives (at Permanent Secretary level or equivalent) from MiEFA, MoA, MoFWRMN, MoA, MoECETT, CoG, MoF, MoH, MoL, MoW, MoC, MoS, MoD, MoI, MoP, MoE, MoA, and the NPC as a non-voting co-chair. The PSC meetings will be organized once a year to review progress and address any implementation bottlenecks. The NPC of the PMU for this project will be in attendance with the TAC. The NPC will have an advisory role for record keeping of the PSC’s activity and decisions. Through community mobilization and as part of the general public awareness campaign, the NPC will keep the communities informed on the progress of the PSC discussions and will ensure their instances are reported. On no less basis, representatives of local communities will be involved as observers.

112. The role of the PSC will be to:
- (i) ensure the quality of results, and the sustainability and impacts of the Project in line with the policy direction;
- (ii) approve the Annual Work Plan and Budget (“AWB”) to be sent to FAO;
- (iii) approve semi-annual Project progress reports to be sent to FAO;
- (iv) approve adjustments to the distribution of budget between items on the basis of information provided by the Project Management Unit;
- (v) approve possible adjustments to indicators and the targets of results and outputs, based on information provided by the NPC;
- (vi) approve possible modifications to the Project implementation agreements;
- (vii) invite specialist professionals to participate in PSC meetings, in accordance with the issues under consideration; and
- (viii) support Project activities for promoting results-based approaches within the Government of The Gambia and requesting relevant policy makers.

113. The Technical Advisory Committee (TAC) will be responsible for the continuous technical oversight and support to project implementation. The TAC’s core membership will include the Directors (or equivalent, or other

designated funds from the Departments of i) Fisheries, ii) Water Resources, iii) Forestry, iv) Parks and Wildlife, v) Agriculture, as well as from vi) National Environment Agency (NEA), vii) National Agricultural Research Institute (NARI), viii) ROGITS PUJ, and ix) FAO. The TAC will be chaired by the Department of Fisheries, with FAO as vice-chair and secretariat. The TAC will also include representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Women's Affairs, Youth and Sports and of female professional associations related to the project, such as cypter honeybee keepers' association, women's groups, and other relevant stakeholders. The TAC will review Work Plans and Budgets (ARPBs), reviewing progress, providing technical guidance and reporting to the PSC.

114. The TAC will be supported by a Technical Advisory Committee (TAC) that will be chaired by FAO and will contribute to the PSC's and TAC's activities and meetings, in particular to share good practices, present emerging issues, provide technical advice on project implementation and propose solutions. It is important to note that partners will not have decision-making power in the two bodies. Some of the pre-identified partners include: the Ministry of Youth and Sports, the Women’s Bureau, the Bureau of Standards, the National Association of Artisanal Fisheries Operators (NAFAO), the River Oyster Women's Association (TOWA), etc., as well as representatives from other projects and from the private sector.

115. In accordance with the cooperation arrangements outlined above, the funds that FAO receives from GGF in its capacity as Accredited Entity will then be transferred to the FAO Representation in The Gambia, who will hire technical expertise for the PMU in charge of executing the Project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 57

## B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### 118.
The country is classified as a least developed country (LDC) by the UN and as low income by the World Bank, and it ranks 17th in the 2020 Human Development Index. In addition, the Gambia is also now recognized as a Fragile State. The country has been facing multiple challenges over the past decade, including political instability, high levels of poverty, and limited access to basic services such as education and healthcare. Over the last decade, this has proven insufficient to accelerate development, reduce poverty and improve food security. The Gambia is also highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, with a significant proportion of its population living in areas that are at risk from flooding and other natural disasters.

### 119.
The country's limited fiscal space means that it cannot afford to invest in the adaptation measures required for building resilient, improved livelihoods of the fisher folk. Given the country's development and adaptation needs, there are limited resources available that could be channeled or redirected to the fisheries sector. Furthermore, the new budgetary constraints due to the economic deterioration caused by the COVID-19 pandemic have made it even more difficult for the government to address these challenges.

### 120.
The Gambia is also facing a significant challenge in terms of limited availability of public and private funding where resources for development are limited. In terms of public funding, the Government of the Gambia has very limited budgetary resources, which were further reduced by the economic deterioration due to the COVID-19 pandemic. The Ministry of Fisheries, Water Resources and National Assembly Matters (MoFWANAM) was allocated roughly €35 million per year (approximation of €80–150 million in 2021) as part of the latest approved Public Budgets.

**Page 60**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

## Lack of investment and vicious spiraling of unsustainable resource use

124. The extent of investment in the fisheries sector, the continuous increase in fish availability will likely trigger a "race to the bottom" in terms of fishing effort, leading to overfishing of the same pool harvest losses and without aquaculture alternatives, fishers fall at accelerated depletion of fish stocks. An example is already visible at many times in the artisanal fisheries sector where small-scale fishers are increasingly using larger mesh sizes that can target small juvenile fish, which in turn further diminishes future stock. The paradigm shift requires investment with the lowest possible concessionality to generate immediate adaptation alternatives.

## Exit strategy (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

126. The exit strategy of the project is based on (i) a joint country and stakeholder ownership; ii) investment in durable infrastructure and capacity building for fisheries equipment with robust local management arrangements including stakeholder capacity building and women's empowerment – and a user fee mechanism empowering communities to manage their resources sustainably; iii) development of an enabling policy environment, regulatory framework and enhanced government capacity to mainstream climate change in the artisanal fisheries sector; iv) improved enabling environment for artisanal fisheries value chain development, including access to talon.


---
**Page 61**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 59

## Strong country and stakeholder ownership

128. The project has been developed at the request of the Government of the Gambia, in close alignment to its national priorities for adaptation to climate change. In particular, the Government's priorities (such as targeting of the most important coastal sites and the support to aquaculture) have been fully included as core interventions in the project. National institutions and artisanal fisherfolk stakeholder groups have been engaged throughout the project development process.

129. The project design choices, in particular for landing site infrastructure and fish processing technologies, have focused on ensuring that these are locally owned and managed by the communities themselves. For example, some of the landing sites include floating portions with longer mops, raised concrete platforms to accommodate post-landing operations, solar-powered seawater fish-milling lines, etc. The FTTT smelt-pond and the CEAS dry-dock technologies have been designed in such a way that they can be locally constructed and maintained. These are efficiently constructed, as demonstrated by successful pilot operations conducted by FAO in the sub-region. For several sites, the project will provide technical assistance to local communities for the construction of landing sites, with international procurement, particularly from Senegal, to ensure the best quality execution. All physical investments will be transferred during implementation and operation and maintenance delivered to the local management committees or to the groups of individual beneficiaries.

130. At local level, the project will work in close collaboration with the local communities in place where such structures are constructed. The project will also provide training for the communities on best practices (such as the CFCS at landing sites and the TRY group of seven hundred woman oyster producers in the mangroves). CFCSs have been designed to ensure that they can be locally managed by the communities themselves.

Where necessary, the project will support the creation of local management structures (for example, at the mangrove restoration sites or at the fish pond clusters). In addition, the CFCS will own the climate-product infrastructure at the fish processing sites. This will allow for a more efficient and sustainable use of the resources, including fish-smoking and drying equipment transferred during implementation, allowing for more buy-on-and more sustainability in the value chain.

131. Recognizing the critical role of women during and after implementation, the project has dedicated substantial resources to ensure that they are fully involved at all levels of planning, implementation and monitoring of best practices. In particular, the project will require an equal representation of women in all local management committees at intervention sites (such as the CFCSs at landing sites or the community forest management committees). In addition, the project will provide training for women on fish processing technologies and other related skills to improve their labor-saving potential for women, the main users. Under Output 3.2, the project will also introduce the Household Management Plan (HMP) tool in all communities where CFCSs are established. This tool will help households balance workloads and increased equity. In parallel, under the same output, the project will implement a literacy and numeracy program for women.

## Improved policy and regulatory framework and enhanced government capacity to mainstream climate change


---
**Page 62**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

B

132. The project will strengthen national capacities to plan for and respond to climate change. As detailed in Section B.3, under Outputs 3.1 and 3.2, the project will implement a mix of measures to mainstream climate change and enhance public service delivery in the fisheries sector, followed by adapted outreach and support to fish-folk and other value chain actors (including women) for improved livelihoods and food security. The new enabling environment will continue its commitment to adaptation in the fisheries sector after project completion. The new enabling environment will also provide a platform for climate change communication and awareness raising for illiterate users, among others – who will produce and disseminate at scale and low-cost timely messages on weather, stock levels, market prices, etc., through radio, SMS, and other means.

Enhanced enabling environment for private sector involvement in artisanal fisheries, including financial services

133. The PROFISH has also planned for an active involvement of the private sector, in particular to attract its investment and financial services provision after the project builds the necessary capacity and demonstrates the economic potential of aquaculture and improved fish processing. The project will also strengthen the capacities of private sector actors involved in artisanal fisheries (e.g., fishers, processors, traders) through participation in the Fishery Co-Management Committee (NASCOM) and the National Fisheries Platform, so that they can support their needs for information on weather, stock levels, market prices, etc. The project will also provide training to weekly (LUMCO) markets at fish landing sites and with the buyers from fish-processing industries. Under Output 3.2, the project will support for a yearly “match-making” engagement with the private sector off-takers and investors in order to promote the development of aquaculture and improved fish processing as well as small-scale commercial agriculture. Furthermore, the project will promote the local production of equipment and fish feed (producers) through the establishment of a network of producers and traders that will reduce the country’s dependence on expensive imports. As part of the project governance, the Gambia Chamber of Commerce and Industry (GCCI) will participate in the project steering committee to ensure that the private sector is adequately represented within a broad-based dialogue among public and private sector actors, including financial institutions, to improve access to financial products and services for the artisanal fisheries sector.

134. The project will also build the capacity of fish space bodies and their members to implement fisheries conservation measures, such as NASCOM’s one-metre tide exclusion zone and the temporary ban on night fishing.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

136. The project will support vulnerable Gambian fisher folk to adapt to the impact of climate change and improve their livelihoods through the adoption of climate-resilient aquaculture, through climate-proofing of small-scale fisheries, and by improving access to markets for fish products.

137. Given the size of the Gambia and the project targeting, the project is expected to have a transformative impact on the fisheries sector’s adaptation to climate change. Building on previous projects and initiatives to support the development of aquaculture in the country, this project will directly benefit 20,548 small-scale value chain actors, who with their household members constitute the 167,643 direct beneficiaries of the project. In addition, the project will indirectly benefit an additional 90,000 people from the fisheries sector and other vulnerable populations – the country’s most vulnerable and largely below the national poverty line – will benefit from better and safer working conditions at fishing landing sites, from increased fish availability and higher quality and healthier protein products.

138. The project responds to the priorities identified in the Gambia’s National Climate Change Strategy and the sectoral policy and contributes to two of 17 adaptation impact areas and one GCF’s mitigation impact area:

- **GCF Adaptation Impact Area A2**: PROPREISH will contribute to increasing the health and well-being, food security, and income generation by improving access to markets for fish products in the country for local consumption, as well as better hygiene conditions and quality standards for fish products. In addition, the proposed infrastructure and equipment investments will ensure healthier conditions (cleaner water, less pollution) at fishing landing sites.

- **GCF Adaptation Impact Area A3**: The project will have a strong focus on increasing the resilience of infrastructure and the built environment to climate change. Under Output 2.1, the project will climate-proof and improve existing infrastructure in the fisheries sector, which is currently under threat due to climate vulnerability. The estimated value of the physical assets supported by the project is at least US$6.7 million, out of a total investment cost of US$80 million.

- **GCF Adaptation Impact Area A4**: Acknowledging the critical importance of mangroves for the fisheries sector in the Gambia, the project is also focused on improving the resilience of mangrove ecosystems and ecosystem services to climate change. Under Outputs 1.1 and 2, the project will complement existing restoration actions by targeting ten mangrove degraded sites in the country. This will contribute to improved livelihoods through sustainable resource management practice and in empowering the local communities for sustainable ecosystem management.

- **GCF Mitigation Impact Area M4**: While PROPREISH is conceived as a predominantly adaptation project, the investment will also have significant mitigation co-benefits. The estimated reduction in emissions is 10% over the 25-year lifetime of the investment. These estimates do not include further reduction in emissions due to the demonstration effect in the local and neighboring communities where the project will intervene, nor the agricultural co-benefits.

139. The project will also contribute to the following GCF outcomes:


---
**Page 68**

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed GCF project will lead to a paradigm shift from an artisanal fisheries sector impacted by climate change towards a more sustainable and resilient sector with adapted infrastructure and equipment, reduced fish loss and waste supplemented with new aquaculture production to maintain fish availability for poor consumers and with improved linkages to formal markets and business opportunities for the fishers. The project will also provide training in value chain management and marketing of fish products to the fisher folk who will be an integral part of a financial and technical position to expand their activities further after project completion.

The project is a significant scaling up and replication opportunity, as it will demonstrate how adaptation measures (such as improved FRT smoking ovens and solar dryers) can improve working conditions, reduce losses, improve hygiene and quality of fish products. The project will also demonstrate the benefits of adopting new aquaculture production technologies and infrastructure, supported by exchange visits and knowledge management, the artisanal fisheries sector will grow in size and diversity. In addition to this, the number of local communities involved with mangrove restoration will grow. In addition, the government is committed to revise fish landing consolidation standards on the basis of PROFISHFIN evidence. As a result, these technologies and practices will be introduced at other landing sites in the country.

The project will also demonstrate how the introduction of new aquaculture production technologies and infrastructure supported by the GCF project. Similarly, aquaculture development's adaptation packages present an enormous opportunity for scaling up and replication as they have been shown to be highly profitable in many countries and could be introduced on thousands of hectares across the Gambian coast.

The project will also demonstrate how the introduction of new aquaculture production technologies supported by such gardens can lead to over 60 other vegetable gardening schemes having been developed and could benefit from the same level of support as the mangrove restoration activities promoted by the project. The lessons leading up to this replication consideration are applicable in the other two aquatic production sectors (fisheries and aquaculture) where restoration activities built on and scale up previous interventions and fit in a long history of restoring and sustainably managing resources. This is an opportunity for communities to engage with mangrove restoration and this is another innovation that can be taken forward by the project. The project will also demonstrate how the introduction of new technologies at scale and time-limited messages on weather, storms, tides, fishing seasonality, and market information to enhance fisheries and aquaculture production, further enhancing the project scaling up and replication.

PROFISHFIN evidence in fish landing consolidation standards shows that such improvements are based on the successful experiences in the West Africa's fisheries. As such, the introduction of the new technologies and practices will result in knowledge sharing and learning for the beneficiaries and for the public services supporting the fisheries.


---
**Page 69**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 67

but will also have a demonstration effect for other countries. In addition, past experiences have demonstrated that the introduction of a fish processing technology usually results in local reform of the design and process, with additional production gains (for example, the introduction of the FTT smoking oven across the coast of West Africa).

### 143. The project will contribute to the achievement of several U.N. Sustainable Development Goals in the sector of aquaculture in the Gambia. Under Outputs 3.1 and 3.2, the project will focus on mainstreaming climate change in the sector's planning processes and institutional arrangements, with a view to ensuring long-lasting inter-sectoral and intra-sectoral coordination (horizontally - between institutions responsible for natural resources management and climate change monitoring, but also cutting across public and private, and national and regional levels).

144. While a major bottleneck remains on the access to adequate sources of finance for smallerholder investments in adaptation and diversification options in the artisanal fisheries sector (due to the challenging loan terms especially for the women involved), the project will contribute to addressing this issue by creating new opportunities, with high potential for a more substantial involvement of the private sector in the fisheries value chains (Output 3.2). These would include the creation of new markets and access to finance for small-scale producers, as well as support for capacity development to local actors to establish linkages between smallholders and private sector actors (including fish processors) through the project's FTTSOVs. In addition, by supporting the establishment of a climate-smart value chain in climate-proofing the artisanal fisheries value chain, the project will establish dialogue aimed to improve the regulatory framework and the functioning of the financial system to improve smallholder access to financial services in the artisanal fisheries sector.

### 3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

145. PROFISFISH will contribute to the achievement of several U.N. Sustainable Development Goals in the Gambia: SDG1 (No Poverty), SDG2 (Zero Hunger), SDG3 (Good Health and Well-being), SDG5 (Gender Equality), SDG6 (Clean Water and Sanitation), SDG7 (Decent Work and Economic Growth), SDG13 (Climate Action), and SDG 14 (Life Below Water). The project will contribute to the achievement of these goals by contributing towards the national targets, as outlined in the overarching Gambia National Development Plan (2018-2021) and its subsequent phases.

146. In particular, the project will have a positive impact on items:

- Environmental co-benefits: The project will reduce the practices of smoking in culminary ovens exposed women – including many women, and the young children accompanying them – as well as the people living in proximity to these ovens. This will contribute towards improving the health status of women and the traditionally smoked fish products.
- Gender-sensitive co-benefits: The project will have a significant and long-term impact on Gambian women, as a majority of core adaptation measures are targeted at women-led activities such as fish handling and processing, which are often carried out in their homes using traditional cooking equipment. The project will significantly reduce the workload that women experience in fish processing activities by providing them with modernized smoking ovens. This will be achieved through the application of the Household Methodology for facilitated dialogue between men and women, and through the provision of training to both groups on the use of the new technology. These interventions are expected to have several positive spill-over effects in terms of nutrition, health, and education at the household level.
- Socio-economic benefits: With the project's support, 20,198 households (or 167,543 individuals) will have better economic opportunities in the fisheries sector, generating more income and improving their capital base and resilience. In addition, the project implementation is expected to generate over 500 temporary jobs during the construction phase of the FTTSOVs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 68

The project’s job creation potential will support the Gambia’s post-COVID-19 recovery, as the country has significantly reduced its loss to tourism revenues and fish exports. In addition, the increased fish availability is estimated to reduce the food import for other protein sources and generate foreign currency gains through export of fish and fish products. The fisheries sector has already increased its share in the national GDP in recent years and the project’s intervention is expected to accelerate this trend.

- **Social co-benefits:** The project's capacity development activities - that will complement the physical investments – will contribute to strengthening the human capital of fisher folk and their ability to engage with other stakeholders. This will be achieved through training, mentoring, and support for access to formal markets and in information and knowledge. In addition, the project will invest in social organization on one side, in order to strengthen the capacity of fisher folk organizations at sites of planned action, and on the other hand, in the organization of fisher folk and other value chain actors into common economic interest groups, such as cooperatives.

## D.A. Needs of Recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### 147.
The Gambia is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The country’s physical location renders it highly susceptible to flooding and erosion caused by wind and sea-level rise, and due to the small size of the economy, the country possesses limited capacity to autonomously undertake extensive adaptive efforts in response to threats for food security and livelihoods. The Gambia is ranked 14th out of 20 countries most vulnerable to climate change; it is ranked 14th in the world, being the 3rd most vulnerable country and the 53rd least ready country, reflecting its high vulnerability score and low readiness score.

The country’s economic vulnerability is also set in a complex development context. The country is classified as at least a developed country (DCD) by the UN and as low income by the World Bank², and it ranks 172nd in the 2020 Human Development Index. The Gambia has been ranked among the world’s least developed countries since 2003; it is also one of the poorest countries in Africa, given its high institutional and social fragility. The average GDP growth of 2% over the last decade has proven insufficient to accelerate development, reduce poverty and improve food security. The Gambia, particularly in rural areas, remains highly dependent on agriculture for livelihoods and as a source of income for many people to immigrate to developed countries. In addition, the economic shock induced by the COVID-19 pandemic on the tourism sector has had a significant impact on the country’s economy, with an estimated 20% decline in employment among populations, as part of a broader agriculture sector that has stagnated in recent years. Agriculture (including fisheries) is the main livelihood source for about 72 percent of poor households and 91 percent of extremely poor rural households.

### 149.
As indicated in section B.5, there is very limited public and private financing available to address the barriers that hinder the implementation of the project’s activities. The Gambia has been unable to mobilize resources for implementing the adaptation measures at a single landing site. While other international technical and financial partners have supported the fisheries sector, their focus has been on opaque fisheries management, and less on utilization of fishery resources in line with the sustainable development goals (SDGs) and the objectives of the GCF.

The various assessments of public service delivery and implementation capacity conducted during the project formulation have indicated the low technical, human and financial resources available in the fisheries sector. Particular issues include insufficient staffing, particularly on the field, inadequate training and technical expertise, and lack of equipment and infrastructure to support the implementation of the project activities. The Government has requested FAO to implement the project, in close partnership with its ministries and agencies. As such, the project is justified as a means of the recipient country’s efforts to strengthen the sector institutions and building long-term implementation capacity to support the Gambia’s adaptation efforts.

D.5 Country ownership (max. 200 words, approximately 1 page)

151. The Government of the Gambia has long been understanding of the country's climate vulnerability, given the adverse impacts of climate change on its development prospects. As such, the Government and national stakeholders have been involved in recent years in increasing climate-resilience planning and development efforts and in resource mobilization to finance the developed priority adaptation measures.

152. The project design is aligned with the policy commitments of the Government of the Gambia. Indeed, it supports the adaptation measures within the fisheries sector outlined in its National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) 2018-2030 and in the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) 2015-2026, which are both framed under the framework of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. Subsequently, the project design has been informed by national development policies, strategies and plans on development, climate change, and food security, such as the National Development Strategy (NDS) 2018-2025; the National Fisheries Sector Development Plan (NFSP) 2018-2021, which advocates for a modern fisheries sector resistant to climate-related hazards; and the United Nations Development Programme's (UNDP) Strategic Framework for the Gambia 2017–2026. The project also contributes directly to the UNDAF strategic result of sustainable, inclusive, and integrative national response to climate change.

153. The project is also closely aligned with the Gambia’s framework on climate change, namely the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) 2018, which advocates promoting sustainable adaptive management of the fisheries resources in order to ensure food security and livelihoods for small-scale fishers. It also aligns with the UNDP's Project 2017-2026 aiming to optimize value-added, improved export earnings, greater employment opportunities and enhanced food security through the development of a modern fisheries sector that is climate-resilient.

154. The project contributes to continuity in implementation and compliance with national fisheries policy instruments: the Fisheries Act 2007; the Fisheries Regulations 2008; and the Fisheries and Aquaculture Sector Strategy (FAS) 2016-2030, which aims at improving the livelihoods of small-scale fishers through sustainable aquaculture practices through institutional capacity strengthening, key sector stakeholder capacity building and promotion of sustainable optimization of the value chain. Hence, the project embodies their vision of developing the fisheries sector in a manner that is climate-resilient.

155. Table D-1 provides a summary of the project alignment and contribution to The Gambia’s policy commitments and national priorities. Further details are provided in Chapter 2 of the Feasibility Study.

D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The total cost of the project is estimated at US$ 24.2 million, including a proposed GCF grant of US$ 17.2 million, co-financing from the MoA (grant financed by IFAD) of US$ 5.3 million, and in-kind co-financing by the Ministry of Fisheries and Aquaculture (through National Assembly Matters [NAP/NAM]) of US$ 2.9 million. Co-financing represents 29% of the total project costs.

Given the very limited available resources for climate adaptation in the Gambia, the PROGRESSFISH design has focused on maximizing the efficiency of the investments and ensuring that they are cost-effective while increasing the project's efficiency and to ensure additionally of the GCF investments, the first two aquaculture packages (and the investment costs) will be co-financed by the MoA. The third package will be fully funded from the GCF grant.

For rice-fish culture, the ROOTS project will invest in the irrigated rice schemes, the provision of inputs and agricultural extension services to farmers, and the establishment of a fish hatchery. For aquaculture, the project will provide aquaculture start-up kits and training. As such, per aquaculture package investments costs (per hectare) are support 65% by ROOTS and 35% from PROGRESSFISH, ensuring efficiency and a much larger scale for this intervention.

Given that the sites have different levels of vulnerability to climate change, the project will invest in all six sites with gardens, with a nearly identical cost-sharing ratio.


An economic analysis has been developed to evaluate the economic returns of the investments in climate-proofing at small-scale aquaculture infrastructure at the six targeted sites. As the six leading sites represent public infrastructure, an economic analysis has been developed, evaluating the overall impact of the investments at each site. On average, the project is expected to generate a net present value (NPV) of US$ 12 million and a return on investment (ROI) of 30% over a period of 15 years.

The Baruji landing site presents the lowest, yet positive results due to the specific nature of investments. The site's investments are designed to accommodate landing capacity losses at other nearby sites, and less to reduce fish-shore conflicts. At the other end, the Tarji landing site has the highest economic results, reflecting that the site is the most important in terms of volume of production.

To complement the standard cost-benefit analysis (CBA), a break-even analysis over 15 years using the same social discount factor of 6% was conducted to assess the minimum decrease in losses required at each site in order to justify investments. The results indicate that realistic and achievable reduction in losses (ranging from 11% to 11.5%) are sufficient to break-even.

### Notes:
100. For the four proposed aquaculture packages, a financial analysis has been conducted over a period of 10 years. Using a discounting approach, it was noted that each proposed adaptation activity is profitable, with significant returns for the project beneficiaries and with internal rates of return ranging from 15% to 27%. In terms of financial results, the break-even analysis shows that all four packages are financially viable.

The analysis has also tested the financial profitability of these activities assuming (i) a full commercial loan financing, (ii) a mix (30%-50%) of commercial and potential concessional loans. The prevailing cash terms in the Gambia however, preclude this type of financing for small-scale farmers and artisanal fisherfolk.

These elements are severe impediments to financial inclusion especially for small-scale farmers and artisanal fisherfolk targeted by these projects. It is therefore recommended that the project should consider a phased approach over 5 years (5 years in aquaculture packages 1 and 2, 6 years for packages 3 and 4). Under the prevailing conditions, the analysis shows that the profitability of these activities is not sufficient to justify the high level of financial risk and justification for highly concessional financing. The sensitivity analysis of these financial models highlights the importance of fish prices in generating economic returns (see Annex 3 EFAF). In all packages, there are significant price fluctuation effect, profitably which project's interventional actions along this value chain for better marketing and value addition to remove these risks.

Using these three price ranges, and assuming that the project will be able to meet its financial assumptions, the yearly economic value generated by the project ranges from US$1.5 million to US$7.2 million. Consequently, this is a significant contribution for the fisheries and aquaculture activities described above and as such they do not generate their own benefit stream.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE T4

## Replicability

Despite suitable fisheries technologies and infrastructure being available in the region, small-scale fishers are constrained by smallholder producer capacity constraints, including unsuitable financial products and a lack of information on impacts of climate change and insufficient capacity to address these hazards. The project will provide solutions such as restoration of mangrove forests in coastal areas.

## Medium

The paradigm shift of the project will rely on the dissemination and demonstration of results of the evaluation of alternative financing options. The superior durability of sea-based infrastructure, including landing sites and fish processing equipment, combined with better product quality and lower maintenance costs covered by commercial insurance, will provide a blueprint for future public and private investments in coastal fisheries suitable to the Gambian context.

The project will mobilize technology (FTTs, landing sites), transfer to and support small-scale fishers and their communities (CFCs, CFMCs, TRY funds), and develop a business plan for subsequent segments of the fisheries value chain. The project will disseminate evidence and knowledge management will be supported by the GCF. Additional climate change adaptation and potentially mitigation co-benefits are expected in other neighboring countries with similar challenges.

## Sustainability

Small-scale fishers and artisanal fishers who are vulnerable to climate change impacts have limited options to mobilize resources for adaptation, especially when village or community savings is not a viable option due to constraints in the financial regulatory system. This element explores the development of market-based and economically inclusive solutions:

- **Key element of sustainability will be the entrepreneurial capacities gained by small-scale fishers through training and the exposure to private sector opportunities. The project will enhance the entrepreneurial capacities of CFCS and CFMCs, as well as their ability to adapt to climate change will improve operations of sea-based infrastructure, reduce maintenance and durability of the investments in infrastructure, equipment and mangrove forests.**

- **By training artisanal fishers and CFCS/CFMCs on business planning and collective entrepreneurial and financial management skills, including commercial linkages with private sector actors, the project will generate sustainable business opportunities for small-scale fishers within the sector platforms such as the “Fisheries’ fundraisers”, linking up small-scale fishers to larger markets. The entrepreneurs, coupled with adequate financing mechanisms, will be able to access inclusive equitable value chain financing schemes that make smallholder producer groups more attractive to financial service providers.**

#### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

163. In its role as Accredited Entity, FAO will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the agreement signed between FAO and the GCF and as detailed in the implementation arrangement. FAO will be responsible for the establishment of the M&E unit, which will comprise of a dedicated M&E specialist supported by the Office of Evaluation (OE) at HQ.

164. Throughout the reporting period, periodic monitoring and evaluation work will be performed by FAO in compliance with FAO policies with regard to the PMU under FAO GEF projects. The PMU under FAO GEF will implement tools and methods to facilitate monitoring and evaluation of the project. In particular, the PMU M&E Specialist will develop a database for data collection and analysis. The PMU M&E Specialist will also be responsible for the periodic reporting to the PMU, the FSC and the TAC as well as to FAO headquarters and the GCF.

165. At the end of the project implementation period, evaluation and final evaluation, the last two executed through the FAO Office of Evaluation using short-term consultants with experience in these types of assignments.

166. The FAO Office of the Inspector General (OIG) provides oversight of the programs and operations of the Accredited Entity (FAO), through internal audit and investigation. The Office has two Audit Units (Corporate Audit Unit and Field Audit Unit) and an Investigative Unit. They conduct their work in accordance with the Code of Ethics and International Standards for the Professional Conduct of Internal Auditors published by the Institute of Internal Auditors (IIA). Auditors follow


---
**Page 97**
---

a scheduled biennial work plan, determined according to accepted methods of risk assessment, combined with cumulative institutional knowledge and analysis of policy lessons from the Governing Bodies, and they also respect to audit needs identified during the course of scheduled audits.

187. Internal Audit activities are coordinated, as appropriate, with the work of the External Auditor, the Office of Evaluation (OEE) and the Investigations Group of the Office of the Inspector General, to ensure that activities are complementary without wasteful overlap. Upon completion of audits, the Office of the Inspector General will provide a report on findings.

188. With regards to procurement, interim payments to procured parties may be authorized by the FAO Responsible Officer following verification that the corresponding milestone described in the payment schedule has been achieved with the required deliverables received or completed to an acceptable quality standard.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

#### Category | Probability | Impact
- Technical and operational/technical and operational | HighHigh | MediumMedium

**Description**

Inadequate human resources for project implementation

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project will recruit on a competitive and transparent basis the personnel of the PMU, who will benefit from the technical support of FAO regional offices, as well as the FAO Sub-Regional Office for Africa (based in Ghana), a FAO Sub-Regional Office for West Africa (based in neighboring Senegal) and the FAO Headquarters. In addition, the project will strengthen the implementation capacities of key ministries and agencies (for example under Activities 3.1.1, 3.1.2 and 3.2.1).

### Selected Risk Factor 2

#### Category | Probability | Impact
- Governance/Governance | Medium/Medium | Low/Low

**Description**

Changes in the development priorities of the Government of The Gambia

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project's implementation arrangements have been designed with a high level of project governance – the Project Steering Committee (PSC) will include multi-sectoral high-level representatives (at least at Permanent Secretary level or equivalent) from key ministries and agencies. The PSC will be responsible for overseeing the design of the project, its activities and its implementation modalities. The FAO Representative in the Gambia will remain in constant dialogue with the PSC to anticipate any potential changes in policies and strategic directions and to propose – together with the PMU – the necessary measures.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

#### Category | Probability | Impact
- Technical and operational/technical and operational | HighHigh | Medium/Medium

## Environmental and Social Safeguard Management Framework (ESMF) Approach
As called for by the FAO and IFC guidelines, the project will be using an ESMF approach, as the exact beneficiaries can only be known at the time of implementation. The ESMF is a flexible framework that allows for different levels of analysis depending on how clearly defined. Nevertheless, a comprehensive EAS analysis has been done for known sites relative to Coastal and near small-scale infrastructures. Tools for site screening are provided within the ESMF to ensure preliminary risk analysis.

The project will be subject to an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) that covers all aspects of the project life cycle from design to closure. Multiple technical and consultation missions have been held for the Concept Note and Project Design Document phases, with a focus on the social dimension. The ESMP has been developed in close collaboration with the FAO Gambian office through bilateral exchanges, meetings, and plenary discussions. Two complementary and separate missions have been held in March 2021, one over the EAS risk assessment and one on the gender aspects. The project will be subject to a continuous monitoring of its environmental and social performance by the Project Management Team (PMT) and the FAO Gambian office through bilateral exchanges, meetings, and plenary discussions.

# G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 176. Women and men are different actors of the same value chains in the fishery sector. Operate on different scales and have different access to resources. The division is based on the social norm that women are responsible for domestic work.

The division is based on the social norm that women are responsible for house chores and caregiving, which allow only work near the sea. It also originates in unequal access to productive assets. To fill this gap, the project proposes interventions at different levels: individual, household, community, and institutional.

## 177. Gambian households do not specialize in one type of livelihood and have the potential to accommodate additional activities. The project will support women’s participation in aquaculture where they are active or in aquafarming if there is an opportunity. Full integration of aquaculture into women's daily activities will necessitate reduction of their other duties, implying that men will need to shoulder some of women's unpaid work. The Gambian women have a strong sense of responsibility towards their families and communities. They will not allow their wife to justify when she neglects the children, argues with him, goes out without telling him, etc. To achieve better results in aquaculture, it is important that men are involved in decision-making processes about what they wish for the family and construct a common vision as well as a roadmap based on a household methodology.

## 178. Gambian women have little information outside their immediate circle due to illiteracy. They are hesitant to approach extension services or other institutions, which is why they do not know how to manage resources in case of underutilized resources, to help the older generation obtain literacy and numeracy skills. Women in the fishery sector also lack access to credit facilities. The project will provide training on financial management for women who are likely to continue. They have not, however, completely eliminated their habits of co-managing house roofs or fuel.

The project proposes two activities on the environment for women – mangrove rehabilitation and environmental awareness raising - for sustainable management of their livelihoods.

# G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 180. Financial control and procurement processes will be implemented as per FAO rules and regulations, which were updated in 2019. The project will use the Global Information Management System (GIMS) at its world-wide offices, which provides all FAO employees, in all locations globally, with travel, human resource, procurement and finance functionalities. Using GIMS improves the flow of financial information, supports financial reporting and facilitates the preparation of budgets. GIMS also allows for a separation of accounts that is used by the whole organization and that allows for a separation of income and expenditure by donor. The project will use the same system as the FAO to ensure that all transactions data is recorded, classified and summarized to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP188

## Proposal name: Climate Resilient Fishery Initiative for Livelihood Improvement in the Gambia (PROREFISH Gambia)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country/ies): Republic of the Gambia

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project fosters a strategic and enabling environment for the artisanal fisheries sector in Gambia by providing access to markets, improving key landing sites as well as diversifying local farming systems with aquaculture packages. | An exit strategy to replicate and scale up the project beyond this project will need to be developed (e.g., through strengthened partnerships) in order to ensure sustainability of the impact of the project and share the lessons learned from the artisanal fisheries sector. |
| The project supports the diversification of the artisanal fisheries sector that supports vulnerable communities with four innovative aquaculture packages, which provide a clear and significant platform for scale and sustainability. | This will need to ensure that the long-term strategic support to the fisheries sector through appropriate policies and regulatory frameworks, will be sustained. |
| The project targets the fisheries sector which diversifies the GCF investment portfolio. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum VI titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background

The artisanal fisheries initiative for livelihood improvement in the Gambia (PROREFISH Gambia) will build resilience in the artisanal fisheries sector in the country by boosting the fisheries infrastructure and aquaculture production to help diversify local


---
**Page 110**
---

# Livelihood strategies and by restoring the key aquatic habitats in the country through mangrove restoration

## The project aims to support vulnerable and poor fishing communities by introducing technology improvements and processing techniques; increasing the resilience of local fishing infrastructure; and promoting new aquaculture packages, capacity-building initiatives for 417,643 beneficiaries (direct and indirect) who are members of some of the most vulnerable and poorest fishing communities in the country. The project will result in a reduction at 238,519 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) over 20 years as a result of the interventions.

### Objectives

The objectives will be achieved through the variety and interlinkages of key activities, such as strengthening restoration capacity and management of local mangrove forests and replanting degraded areas; improving access to markets for fish products; upgrading fishing and fisheries infrastructure and promoting new approaches to aquaculture packages; and capacity-building initiatives for communities, government bodies as well as boosting extension services for the fisheries sector.

### The fisheries sector in the Gambia is facing numerous challenges from climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, increased salinity levels on coastal areas, and extreme weather conditions. Small-scale, subsistence, artisanal fisheries are the backbone of the livelihoods for vulnerable and rural poor communities who depend heavily on fish for their food security and income. Fish provides access to nutrients and protein-rich diets. However, these local livelihoods are under threat because climate change is impacting marine, coastal and riverine fishing; it is damaging fishing infrastructure and reducing catches by altering water temperatures, increasing salinity levels, changing the range and availability of fish species. This will impact communities by reducing fish stocks and undermining their food security.

### Total project finance costs are USD 25 million with the GCF grants accounting for USD 17.2 million. The Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) through a project under the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) will provide USD 0.8 million, while the World Bank will provide USD 5 million. The Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources and National Assembly Matters (MoFWRMAM) will provide USD 1.2 million. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) will provide a grant of USD 0.6 million. The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is B.

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Restoration of key fisheries habitats (Total cost USD 34.2 million; GCF cost: USD 27.5 million)

Mangrove ecosystems play a crucial role as fish habitats. The first component will focus on restoring degraded mangrove ecosystems through replanting, including introducing species with higher biomass and increased salinity levels, as well as improving the management of mangrove resources.

The component will target a total of 1,180 ha of degraded mangrove areas in key fisheries locations within the Gambia. An additional 2,250 ha will be restored through assisted natural regeneration.

This component will have a strong community focus, with targeted engagement in both area-level planning and reporting activities. The communities will receive training and capacity-building and will become local leaders of sustainable ecosystem management initiatives. They will also participate in the establishment of mangrove management committees to assist with the sustainable management of the mangroves and their long-term conservation. The communities will also receive training on how to develop business


---
**Page 111**
---

### Component 2: Climate-resilient fisheries infrastructure and aquaculture development (total cost: USD 17 million; GCF cost: USD 16.7 million)

- The component aims to improve the current infrastructure used by the fisheries industry, which is of significant importance to the artisanal sector for landing and selling their catches and in turn, will, in parallel, help to promote four aquaculture packages that are tailored to support the local challenges and context across the target communities.

The following supports increasing the resilience of some of the most important landing sites for the artisanal fisheries sector by improving the infrastructure, while also improving and diversifying the processing facilities on these targeted sites to allow fisherfolk better landings and sales opportunities. These improvements will be made in four packages:

1. The processing facilities include wet storage tanks, solar dryers, smoking ovens and solar-powered fish rinsing stations, which will enable fisherfolk to store their catches for longer periods of time on the markets but will also help to reduce loss of catches and maintain quality for a longer period than currently possible. These techniques will enable the fisherfolk to reach more markets, allow for better pricing and thus earn higher revenues if required and, most importantly, increasing the shelf life of the products.

2. The four aquaculture packages are tailored to support the local challenges and context across the target communities. These are (1) integrated shrimp culture through targeted pond production at the village level; (2) tilapia fish culture through targeted pond production at the village level; and (3) oyster and clam production in mangrove forests.

The combination of the packages plus the boost to infrastructure and processing facilities at the landing sites will provide a significant transformation of the artisanal fishing sector and support diversification and commercialization of a multitude of fish products with communities in the lead.

### Component 3: Improved climate change adaptation capacity (total costs: USD 28 million; GCF cost: USD 27.9 million)

The third component supports capacity-building for government stakeholders and key national actors to better understand, plan for, and respond to climate change impacts on the fisheries sector and boost the linkages and partnerships across public and private actors.

This component will enable the relevant ministries to better analyze and integrate climate change considerations into their planning processes in order to improve the resilience of this sector. This will be combined with technical support and training for staff in line ministries, as well as capacity-building for communities to better understand and respond to climate change impacts on their livelihoods.

The component will focus on building a strong and innovative extension service support to the sector for locally based field fisheries and forestry officers for aquaculture and mangrove forests.

### Project management (total cost: USD 2 million; GCF cost: USD 1.8 million)

The GCF portion of the project's management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and in compliance with the GCF policy on fees.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria


---
**Page 112**
---

3.1 Impact potential

The project focuses on revitalizing livelihoods of vulnerable and rural poor communities, benefiting a total of 417,643 beneficiaries, and on delivering important mitigation benefits from mangrove restoration amounting to estimated emissions of 238,519 tCO₂eq over 20 years, further benefiting the future key fish habitats.

The project will also support the revitalization of sustainable local farming systems where artifical fisheries play a crucial role in providing nutrient-rich diets and supporting livelihoods for vulnerable communities. The project will provide the mechanisms to manage and protect the key fish habitats at present and for future generations by actively managing and restoring the mangroves and, by working through the community management groups, will ensure that these benefits are shared equitably among all stakeholders.

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The combination of the three components offers a transformative and strategic approach to improving the sustainability of this key sector in the Gambia by increasing resilience and productivity of the mangroves, enhancing food security through aquaculture systems, and improving processing facilities at the landing sites. There is also a strong emphasis on data management and analysis and extension support to drive the project forward.

The project targets sustainability and scalability at various levels, starting with the focus on mangrove restoration and livelihoods revitalization in the Gambia. The beneficiaries will not only be led through the restoration activities but will also be empowered to identify small-scale business opportunities relating to sustainable management of the mangroves. This will greatly impact the local economy by creating new jobs and providing a strong basis for the sustainable management of the mangroves, and the model proposed by the project can be adapted to other similar ecosystems in West Africa.

The project's second component (i.e., to deliver more climate-resilient landing sites) is crucial and represents a unique opportunity to secure long-term access for the artisanal fisheries sector. The new landing sites will provide better facilities, including cold storage, which will prevent them straight away, thereby securing better access to markets, prices, shelf life and overall quality. The project will also support the development of processing facilities in different aquaculture systems, constituting a very scalable model for numerous communities to scale up.

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The project will support the restoration and sustainable management of the crucial mangrove ecosystems, which are the key aquatic habitats for the current fisheries sector and the livelihoods of vulnerable communities. The project will also promote more sustainable approaches using ovens and sun dryers rather than relying on unsustainable use of firewood, and will provide a strong basis for job creation while creating a strong enabling environment for local stakeholders in the intersecting expansion of mangroves, fisheries and agricultural production.

The project supports key solutions to improve socioeconomic conditions for the targeted beneficiaries and will deliver a clear scalable model for the rest of the country through
