IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

32. Project background: The project’s objective is to build the resilience of the country's fisher folk against climate change and improve their livelihoods through the implementation of three inter-linked components pertaining to strengthening community management of artisanal fisheries as of June 2022.


---
**Page 114**
---

fishery infrastructure and mangrove ecosystems, developing climate-resilient fishery infrastructure and aquaculture development, and improving capacities to adapt to impacts of climate change. Environmental and socio-benefits on benefits include the improvement of the artisanal fisheries' processing capacity, increased access to markets for small-scale fishers, improved livelihoods, and contribute to strengthening the fisher folk's local management capabilities and better access to formal markets, information and business opportunities.

### ESS 1: Environment and risk category and safeguards instrument. The project is classified as Category B and is within the accreditation level of the AFE. The AE has provided an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) that identifies the risks/impacts of the project and mitigation measures to address these risks/impacts.

### Compliance with GCF's environmental and social safeguards standards. The paragraph below outlines the key elements of the project programme in relation to environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards.

#### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts. The environmental and social risks and impacts associated with this project are being managed, mitigated, and supervised both at the framework level and the project operational level. A detailed ESIA has been prepared for the project as a whole and some of the relevant sites such as for mangrove restoration and aquaculture activities are still not fully defined. The environmental and social risks and impacts associated with these and social impact assessments given that these will be further developed during implementation. Nevertheless, initial risk and impact analyses have been done for known sites. The environmental and social risks and impacts include the generation of waste and wastewater. Potential social impacts include the temporary and limited access to traditional fishing grounds.

#### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions. The project may engage workers during construction and rehabilitation of the facilities including in its livelihood interventions. Hence, appropriate measures will be taken to ensure that the workers are protected from health risks. It will promote subcontracting to local entrepreneurs and employ local workers (to the extent possible) for the implementation of the project activities. In order to ensure that the project support will also follow the procedures for managing contractors and the United Nations supplier Code of Conduct. The project will also provide training on occupational safety and health in existing processing facilities at the sites are already old, the project aims to enhance the occupational safety and health conditions by providing training and equipment to workers (to avoid exposure to air pollutants during the smoking process) particularly in the value chain by employing state-of-the-art technology. The project will not introduce any new hazardous substances and has provided an exclusion list if it would. It will also ensure that a good partnership of the host community and that monitoring is adequately implemented and no child labour takes place in its activities.

#### ESS 3: Health, safety and environmental risks and impacts. The project is expected to have limited risk and impacts arising from its interventions in relation to mangrove restoration and co-management, aquaculture production activities, and small-scale infrastructure. For instance, the construction of new facilities may lead to some temporary disruption to local communities due to sand mining and could produce construction solid waste and wastewater. To mitigate these risks, a comprehensive environmental management plan will be developed that includes appropriate treatment and appropriate drains, among other approaches, will be required to be developed by the project team. The project will also ensure that all workers are provided with adequate training as well will also be required. Regular monitoring and reporting, including involving communities will also be required. In relation to the use of pesticides in the mangrove restoration and fishery production systems, the project will avoid the use of any highly toxic pesticide (eg. will only allow reasonable use of low toxicity pesticides) and will promote integrated Pest Management. Training on appropriate limited pesticide use will also be provided including monitoring of


---
**Page 115**
---

residents in cooperation with the relevant authorities. The project will likewise integrate the design of wastewater infrastructure in its sites to minimize pressure on freshwater supply.

### ES 4: Community health, safety and security

The project could affect the communities' environmental health and safety such as in relation to construction activities (e.g., nuisance dust, noise, etc.) and the operation of the facilities under the project. As indicated above, the project will require the development of a C-ESMP to minimize impacts on the community's environmental health and safety.

### ES 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project could result in land acquisitions and/or involuntary resettlements as one of the project’s risks as there could be unequal access to the newly constructed infrastructure. The project will ensure that all land acquisitions are conducted in accordance with the relevant laws and support the strengthening of existing local GRM procedures to be aligned with the AE's standards.

The project will also conduct a resettlement risk analysis and undertake consultations with stakeholders to appropriately assess the level of risk and that mitigation measures, as needed, are developed.

### ES 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The ESMP indicated that all project activities would be carried out outside of any protected areas. The project will also ensure that no new invasive species are introduced through mangrove restoration and management. The ESF further indicated that the project would undertake a risk assessment to determine whether any exotic species, including non-native fish species, would be used for restored mangrove forests. The project should ensure that invasive species (plants and animals) are not released into the environment as part of the activity by considering local species or to avoid any negative impact on the introduction of exotic species into the ecosystem.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES 7: Indigenous peoples

In relation to the Indigenous Peoples Policy, the project will ensure that it complies with the policy. The project will provide an overview of the communities in the Gambella region notes nine main ethnic groups and at least 10 language groups. The project will also ensure that no adverse impacts are caused by the project through structural discrimination based on ethnic identity or language.

The AE confirms that there is no recorded evidence of any adverse impact on indigenous peoples from the use of renewable energy sources, with many engaged in artisanal fisheries. Based on the screening conducted, the AE has determined that the project will not have a significant adverse impact on indigenous peoples' people. ES 7 (GCF) and ES 9 (IAF) standards will not be applied.

At the site-specific level, the project will ensure that it complies with the policy by highlighting their role in the local fishery value chain (e.g., as fishers, fishermen, etc.) and by highlighting gender composition in each. Gerifaction resolution mechanisms at the local level are also identified to address any adverse impacts on indigenous peoples' people.

Further relevant safeguards can be found in the sub-project exclusions that exclude activities that involve land acquisition; involuntary resettlement; land appropriation; and activities that would create adverse impacts on ethnic groups within the village and/or in neighbouring villages. #### Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment safeguarding

The revised GEF-7 ESMF and GEF-8 ESMP include a new safeguard titled the Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) Safeguard. This requires safeguarding against sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GEF-financed activities. The PROGAMA project aims to enhance climate resilience of fisher folk living in the Gambia and reduce their vulnerability to climate change by promoting the conservation and sustainable use of marine biodiversity, the restoration of fish habitats, climate-resilient fishery infrastructure and aquaculture development, and improved access to markets. This will be achieved through a combination of measures such as the construction of various infrastructure, the negative impacts and risks of the overall project and mitigation measures are fully identified and addressed in the ESMP.

#### The ESMP therefore addressed the SEAH and GBV safeguarding including the project’s commitment to addressing SEAH emphasized in the ESMP and the Gender Action Plan – by strengthening the capacity of the Gambia National Fisheries Authority (GNFA) to carry out a survey personnel on SEAH, and establishing and operationalizing referral pathways for GBV. The AE will also ensure that all staff are aware of their responsibilities under the SEAH safeguard in order to support survivors of SEAH (and GBV) through medical care, psychosocial support, legal and social services, and other forms of assistance. In addition, in collaboration with the FAO Gambia country office (separate from the FAO worldbank) and the link and GBV and SEAH referral pathways, it will be ensured that survivors are supported promptly through an accessible, fast-track and non-discriminatory process, that is also survivor-centered and confidential. This will be culturally appropriate and sensitive to the needs of survivors and their families, including their need for confidentiality.

#### The GBV safeguarding measures included in the ESMP and the Gender Action Plan are aligned with the GEF provisions of the revised GCF Indigenous Peoples and Social Policy. The AE will ensure that all project partners and stakeholders are aware of these provisions and implement them to ensure alignment to prevent and respond effectively to SEAH in a survivor-centered and gender-sensitive manner.

### Implementation arrangements

The AE will be responsible for the overall management of the project. The project has a Project Steering Committee (PSC) and a Technical Advisory Group (TAG). The PSC is chaired by the GCF, and includes designated Project Management Unit (PMU)) will be established to oversee implementation on the ground. It will have technical expertise in areas such as climate change adaptation, biodiversity conservation/restoration/natural resource management, capacity development, and an ESS and gender. The project will also establish a Gender Action Plan (GAP) that will ensure alignment with various national laws and regulations and the AE’s safeguard requirements. A capacity-building plan has been developed to address the training needs of all stakeholders in light of the project’s environmental and social safeguards. Monitoring protocols including provision of an external audit have been considered to ensure adequate implementation (including budget and implementation calendar) of the ESMP and its corresponding management measures.

### Gender policy

#### 2. Gender policy

- The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.
    - The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment to enhance gender equality and women's empowerment in the Gambia. The country has ratified the core international human rights treaties, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights.
    - The Constitution incorporates customary law and charts that apply to 95 per cent of the population and rule out application of no-discrimination principles on "adoption, marriage, divorce, burial, devolution of property, inheritance, etc." The National Women's Council and the Ministry of Women Affairs, the National Women's Council and the Women's Bureau are mandated to spearhead the women's agenda in the country. The Gambia has a gender policy that sets out the principles and evaluation of the Gambia's gender policy.
    - The Women's Act covers a wide range of topics concerning women's rights and empowerment. However, the effective implementation of these laws has been hampered by patriarchal norms and beliefs. The National Gender Policy (NGP) was developed in 2013 to provide a framework that coordinates the creation of knowledgeable, skilled and properly equipped women who can contribute optimally to national development.
    - The AE's assessment also notes that in 2018 tensions along ethnic lines were reported in the country. These tensions are believed to have been proportionately more of the most vulnerable, which includes ethnic minorities, young women and girls.

#### Gender assessment

- The gender assessment was compiled based on stakeholder consultations and a desk review.
    - A total of 132 villages in the target areas were consulted, of which 81 were women and 41 were men. Consultations were conducted through focus group discussions exclusively with women. Institutional meetings were held at the national level with state ministries and agencies to discuss the gender equality and women's empowerment issues.
    - The consultations resulted in recommendations on the implementation strategies for the GCF project, including the inclusion of social considerations to ensure sustainable use of natural resources including aquatic habitats.


---
**Page 118**
---

## Gender assessment presents a wide-ranging review of gender issues and gender gaps across the country and within the sectors that are the focus of this project.

It identifies information, technical, financial, market and institutional barriers facing women along with social, cultural, economic and political factors that contribute to these barriers. Women face these barriers in the fisheries sector. However, the sector is heavily segregated by gender, with women generally working as small-scale processors and traders, while men are more likely to be involved in fishing activities. While women have a significant role in the processing and selling of fish and shellfish for the domestic market, which has lower profit margins than for the export market, gender gaps in literacy and access to information and extension services remain high.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The gender action plan provides activities that address the challenges faced by women and include specific indicators, targets, timelines and budgets. The project includes collection of sex-disaggregated key indicators, and inclusive consultations with women and other relevant stakeholders to ensure their participation in all aspects of the project.

The gender action plan has a strong focus on women's participation through consultations, training and capacity building activities. Women will be involved as beneficiaries and partners in project investment in planning, and equally benefitting from project investments with benefits rising to 100 per cent in some cases. The project aims to engage rural young women with an inter-sectoral approach that includes education, skills development, employment rates – to help the older generation of women to gain literacy and numeracy skills. Women will also be trained on business management and financial planning, as well as negotiation skills. In order to tailor the interventions more closely to the needs and strengths of women in the project area, a baseline study has been conducted to assess the efficacy and effects of infrastructure and the appropriateness of each intervention site; invited to observe the training sessions and provide feedback on the quality of extension services for women, and involved in mangrove restoration and conservation, including monitoring and evaluation of mangroves. They will also be part of planning and executing activities related to the project.

The gender action plan is currently being developed with a budget allocated for an expert dealing with environmental and social safeguards, gender, youth, and sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment.

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

FAO will serve as both the AE and the EE in the project. In its role as AE, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including (i) all aspects of project approval; (ii) administrative, financial, and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation to ensure that the project activities are carried out in a manner that achieves objective; (iii) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting on project progress. The AE will be responsible for fulfilling these responsibilities in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the accreditation master agreement.

# Results monitoring and reporting

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

### As a cross-cutting initiative, the project aims at generating both mitigation and adaptation results. On the mitigation side, the interest is expected to result in overall emission reductions of up to 20% by 2030 compared with business as usual (BAU). The metrics of the GCF core indicator for mitigation based on the accounting methodology detailed under annex 2A of the project document are: direct beneficiaries of 15,000 households and 250,000 indirect beneficiaries as expected through adaptation impact. The A4T has provided a methodology and rationale for defining both direct and indirect beneficiaries in annexes 23 of the funding agreement.

#### The theory of change diagram adequately captures different levels of expected changes as well as the logical relationships between project outcomes with identification of co-benefits. The barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes are identified and linked to the corresponding mitigation and adaptation results.

##### The logical framework has been well designed in accordance with the GCF integrated reporting framework. It is based on a theory of change that captures the potential for shift potential since artisanal fisheries serve as a high source of livelihood for about 20% rural population in Gambia. It will contribute to both all GCF adaptation results areas and one GCF mitigation result area. The indicators selected are linked to the project’s enabling core and supplementary indicators are selected to monitor and report the project results respectively (enabling core, technology development and dissemination, market development and transformation, and knowledge generation, capture and learning). These indicators are SMART and have been developed in accordance with the GCF and are set based on data available from credible sources.

##### At the GCF outcome level of “Enabling environment,” four core indicators have been identified: enabling environment for technology development and dissemination, market development and transformation, and knowledge generation, capture and learning. Given that these indicators are linked to all project results areas, the project will build a comprehensive enabling environment at the national level.

##### The project has linked relevant project-specific indicators to project outcomes and outputs. These indicators have been developed in accordance with the evaluation of the project. The indicators are SMART and have considered both qualitative and quantitative aspects of results. It is hoped that these indicators will provide evidence that will increase the

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP18B

Proposal name: Climate Resilient Fishery Initiative for Livelihood Improvement in the Gambia (PROREFISH Gambia)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country/(un): Republic of The Gambia

Project/programme size: Small


## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: N/A

The Gambia is the smallest country within mainland Africa, stretching 540 km along the Gambian coast and covering an area of 16,989 km² and surrounded by Senegal to the north and a 60-km Atlantic Ocean front. With a population of about 2.4 million,* the Gambia is one of the most densely populated countries in Africa.

The country's economy relies heavily on agriculture and fishing; this creates vulnerability to climate change shocks, which affect the country’s most vulnerable people. Overall, climate variability has led to increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, damage to infrastructure and through the effects of increased temperature, salinity and acidity as well as sea level rise on many important fish and shellfish species. This will in turn threaten the livelihoods of artisanal fishermen who depend heavily on these resources for their income, which is now the main source of protein for the Gambian population and a pillar of sustainable diets and food security, especially among the most vulnerable and food-insecure groups and the most vulnerable people.

In this context, the Climate Resilient Fishery Initiative for Livelihood Improvement in the Gambia (PROREFISH) project aims to build the resilience of the Gambian artisanal fishers in the face of climate change and improve their livelihoods. The project involves interventions at the coast and inland areas that will be implemented through three components as follows:

1. Component 1 aims to reverse the degradation of the mangrove ecosystems in key fisheries areas by promoting mangrove restoration, replanting and assisted natural regeneration of more salt-tolerant species, coupled with support to sustainable ecosystem management. The project targets 20 km² of mangroves (representing approximately 5% of the total 413 km² of assisted natural regeneration) in the 10 areas prioritized during project preparation.

2. Component 2 aims to increase overall fish availability and enhance the income of poor fishers by promoting the use of traditional fishing methods (e.g., handline, cast net, and fish weirs/rafts) and by promoting aquaculture production through different climate-resilient technologies in selected areas.

3. Component 3 aims to provide the cross-cutting support necessary for the successful implementation, scaling up and sustainability of the interventions in mangrove restoration.


* See https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/gambia/overview#1

# Infrastructure, climate proofing and aquaculture

## Project components

### Component 1: Climate-proofing of infrastructure in fisheries and aquaculture

#### Sub-component A: Climate-proofing of infrastructure in fisheries and aquaculture

- **Main activities**:
    - Monitor the fisheries sector and its associated ecosystems to integrate climate risks.
    - Develop a monitoring system to assess climate impact.
    - Implement measures such as fish loss reduction, additional fish production, and change in behavior of beneficiaries.

for a greater understanding of future stream flow. The project presents historical and future trends in temperature and precipitation, sea surface temperature, global mean sea level rise and salinity. The analysis concludes that over the medium term (20 years), climate change is estimated to increase the average annual rainfall by 15% on the Gambia River and impact mangrove ecosystems through increased salinity.

However, in a deeper analysis it is important to highlight that only 460 km of the 1,150 km long river are located within the country's borders, with most of its length in Senegal. Moreover, because of Gambia’s precarious position as a country encompassing only a small part of the Gambia River basin, it is important to consider the impact of climate change on the entire basin and analyses of meteorological parameters relied heavily on globally reanalysis data. Such data have been impacted by the lack of in-situ observations and the limited availability of hydrological process that is facilitated by Earth observation remote sensing tools and satellite-based observations. The present study has therefore attempted to develop daily temperature and precipitation series for the Gambia River basin from 1978-2018. It was found that the average annual temperature increased by 0.2 °C per decade, with a total rise of 0.74 °C for the baseline period. It is also found that the soil temperature has increased at an average rate of about 0.35°C per decade, which is slightly higher increases in the upper reaches of the watershed and in the coastal areas. For 1979-2016, the mean annual precipitation over the entire basin (including the Gambia River) was insignificant, exhibiting an increase of about 1 mm per year.

Application of the Thornthwaite equation shows a gradual increase in potential evapotranspiration and a decrease in the difference between the temperature and the potential variation in rainfall availability. At the local level, there have been reports of drought proneness due to increased temperatures and reduced precipitation over the last decade. This has led to the outflow of the river, resulting in inward penetration of salinity along the coastal reaches and loss of riparian vegetation.

The projection of future climate change is found to be somewhat flawed, as the general circulation models employed for the projection have not been validated for the Gambia River Basin. The projections are therefore considered to be indicative rather than definitive on a time timeline. Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 have been used for the projection of future climate change, which is considered to be the most likely scenario with confidence in the projection. Even though the rate of change in temperature and rainfall will remain small for the projections years (being both scenarios), a net rise in average temperature and only a slight increase in precipitation are expected over the next 20 years. This will impact on the watershed and destabilize the flow patterns and the corresponding shift in mixing zones between the river and the sea, which may have significant impacts on the fisheries sector, especially from such understanding, it is highly likely that in the waterbody and also in the near-shore open ocean there would be a significant increase in salinity due to increased freshwater change. Given the high dependence of the Gambian population on artisanal fisheries for both income and food security, as well as the fact that the Gambia River Basin is considered one of the most climate change warrant immediate actions for the fishers and the fishers’ based-value chain to adapt to the conditions.

The project outcomes are highly dependent on the joint actions developed with Senegal as the neighbour country, as the Gambia River and the marine resources are totally dependent on the action taken in both countries. The project will also need to consider the landscape level. The project will need to support Gambian’s New Fisheries Policy and Strategy, that as explained in the Country Ownership section, (see para. 62 below) involves a subregional approach with Senegal.

Overall, the project has a medium to high impact potential in terms of adaptation. There are two points of caution: the need to deliver information how to minimize the impacts of climate change over the lifetime of the project; and the need to prioritize actions in view of

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

## 1.2.1 Innovation

### 18
PROEFISH is one of the few projects presented to the GCF in the fisheries sector aiming to reduce impacts on too many fish stocks. The project proposes a multi-faceted approach, which has an interesting approach that geographically targets coastal areas, the estuary and the river as well as inland areas. It also proposes a new model for integrated aquaculture and vegetable gardening co-financed by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) Resilience of Organizations for Transformative Smallholder Agriculture (ROOTS) project.

### 20
However, it is important to note that the activities proposed do not necessarily relate to each other, giving a sense of the light touches in many proposed outcomes.

### 26
In terms of the coastal interventions, the project expects to support seven landing sites, which are located on both sides of the river. These sites will be used by small-scale fishers who catch a higher share of the total number of fishers. The sites will target food lines through such landing sites alone.

### 28
In terms of mangrove restoration, the project proposes to restore and protect infrastructure is crucial; there is also a need to create strong coordination among the landings sites and communities, as well as contractual power or negotiation with industrial owners and facilities and dedicated authorities.

### 30
The mangrove component has been proposed based on the current degradation level, on the relevant scale of restoration, and on the potential for mangroves to be restored in areas that are non-protected area status in the estuary and middle river zones. Restoring mangrove forests is essential as a buffer zone against coastal erosion and as a natural barrier in order to reduce coastal erosion. In the view of the independent TAP, much of the mangrove has been made and not climate induced. As presented in the project proposal, there should be no incentives for further mangrove restoration.

### 32
Aside from the mangrove component, the project proposes to support the overall protection of the mangrove ecosystems by governmental authorities, including the means for enforcement of laws and the overall impacts of the above mentioned agriculture and infrastructure sectors.

### 34
Aquaculture support will focus on the inland part of the Gambia, where fish availability is much lower than in the estuary. The project proposes to develop a model for integrated fish production in communal vegetable gardens (coordinated by the ROOTS project), which would be used as an alternative livelihood and food source for small-scale farmers (on the open, brackish water). Even though aquaculture is not innovative as a practice, depending on the scale of implementation it could be a very supportive development of local livelihoods and market integration could make a difference to the communities involved.

### 36
The theory of change is well presented, but the project tries to resolve all the adaptation barriers in one go. The project does not provide enough detail about how the different activities proposed in component I, the project aims to deliver two many activities and does not concentrate on one concrete and coherent long-term strategy. For example, supporting mangrove restoration could be better coordinated with the Ministry of Environment to resolve the gap in literature in a more structured manner rather than just delivering a training of trainers for mangrove restoration and then expecting that it will have an impact on local communities.


---
**Page 129**
---

20. However, while the project aims to strengthen institutional capacity, it seems that FAO is leading and executing most of the project activities. It is crucial to ensure ways in which different governmental and national institutions are involved in delivering activities, gaining the capacity to support policy reforms as part of the project.

### 1.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

The project aims to tackle institutional, social, technical, information and financial barriers in an integrated manner, but as explained above, this seems to result in weak outcomes with structural gaps in the country left unresolved. These gaps include women's education and the market access of small-scale fishers.

#### 1.2.3 Contribution to the enabling environment and regulatory framework and policies

According to the proponents, PROFISERIES, through its capacity-building of government institutions, will contribute to the implementation of the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (draft technically validated in May 2022). However, it is difficult to understand how this will be achieved as the document does not present the objectives of the policy and possible ways of supporting its implementation.

Aside from this policy, one of the project objectives is to support policies at the national level. However, the specific nature of these policies is not clear. While the independent TAP identified specific policies being developed or those that need to be reviewed, it was not clear how these questions were answered and whether specific policies and regulatory frameworks are subject to potential review and that reform will be assessed during project implementation.

The project team has attempted to understand the current policy gaps and to structure effective policies based on an analysis of what has worked in the past and what is needed for the future. This includes the need for better governance, including the management of coastal and mangrove ecosystems. Therefore, in its opinion, the overall policy gaps must be addressed at the beginning of the project to ensure that there are concrete and effective actions delivered as part of the activities to support policy reforms during the lifetime of the project.

1.3 Sustainable development potential

### Environmental co-benefits

- The restoration of mangrove ecosystems is critical for landscape sustainability and for ensuring a healthy habitat for fish.
- The project will also reduce the exposure of the communities to high toxic compounds that result from the traditional way of smoking fish products.
- The project will support air quality. Fish smoking in rudimentary ovens exposes workers and their families to harmful smoke, which can cause respiratory problems among them – as well as the people living in proximity to the relevant sites to a high health hazard and results in highly toxic compounds for fishing communities. The project will alleviate this risk with effective technology equipment.

### Economic co-benefits

- The project will support more effective value chains for the communities that live on fish resources, enabling them to improve their income generation and therefore their quality of life.
- There are also opportunities to create new employment from the aquaculture venture and to support the fish and oyster cooperatives in becoming more profitable and sustainable. The project is expected to generate over 500 temporary jobs and 100 permanent jobs, which would be targeted at vulnerable youth.

### Social co-benefits

- The improvement of the artisanal fisheries processing systems through the project-supported FAO-Tharipyang processing technique oven, accompanied by adequate technical and managerial support will improve the livelihoods of fishers.
- More food security will be ensured for households and the intake of fish proteins will be diversified. At the same time communities will be more resilient to the effects of climate change following the diversification of their income sources and the creation of new employment opportunities to live from fish resources.
- The project will also support community-building activities could support more social cohesion and greater community responsibility in supporting their fishing grounds and resources.
- The project will also support the involvement of women in developing effective fishing systems and in improving their ability to support their households with skills to negotiate fair trade agreements.

### Gender-sensitive development

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

#### 1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups

* The Gambia is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change owing to its geographical location in West Africa.
* According to the World Bank’s “World Development Report” (2015) and the Dürre Global Adaptation Initiative index, the Gambia is the 3rd most vulnerable country and the 31st least resilient country globally.
* Despite the presence of the Gambia River, which runs through the middle of the country, only one sixth of the land is arable and poor soil quality has led to the predominance of a single crop, namely rice. This makes the country highly dependent on imports for food security, with most home by Gambians living abroad.
* The country has but a narrow oceanic economic exclusion zone extending 200 nautical miles into the Atlantic Ocean. It is surrounded by landlocked countries and its only sea bordering neighbouring countries Senegal and Gambia, stretching only 10 km on each side of the Gambia River. This makes it highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as rising sea levels.
* The country’s economy is heavily dependent on agriculture and fisheries. Agriculture accounts for 33% of all foreign exchange earnings from exports. However, 79 per cent of households engaged in agriculture and fisheries live on less than USD 1.25 a day.

#### According to the project proposal

* Agriculture, including fisheries, plays an important role in the Gambia’s development, employing nearly half – 46 per cent - of the labour force and representing 30% of GDP.
* The majority of households (87%) are engaged in agriculture. Of these, 72% of poor households and 91 per cent of extremely poor rural households, agriculture is the main economic activity. Agriculture accounts for 45% of total household income, 33% of GDP product and 33% of all foreign exchange earnings from exports.
* However, 79 per cent of households engaged in agriculture and fisheries live on less than USD 1.25 a day.

#### On the supply side

* The tourism sector was the most affected while the demand side, subdued domestic and external demand damaged the economy.


---
**Page 132**
---

10. Fisheries-related activities represent the main source of income for coastal fishing communities and are an important complementary activity for rural communities along the Gambia River. Moreover, fish is the main source of protein in diets of 80 per cent of the Gambian population.
11. The small-scale/artisanal fisheries subsector supplies fish products to local consumers, and shrimps, cephalopods and high-value species to the processing facilities. Originally practiced by subsistence fishermen, this sector has been increasingly commercialized in response to increasing market demand. Despite the fact that most fishers still target fish for personal consumption, there is a growing interest among some of them in fishing high-value species, such as sole, snapper and cephalopods, which are processed for export or sold on local markets.
12. Island fisheries are less developed, with the operators often practicing traditional methods. The fisheries employ both subsistence and traditional commercial fishers, who occasionally sell their catch to small-scale processors or to larger special interest industrial companies.
13. The project has identified several barriers, including ambiguous rights between climate change impacts on fisheries and over-exploitation issues; insufficient data and analysis on climate change impacts on the sector and weak knowledge of adaptation measures; no disposable income for fishers to invest in new technologies; lack of information channels for fish products and insufficient linkages between key value chain actors (producers) and buyers.
14. Moreover, the country lacks technological capacities in the fisheries sector and past experience with climate change adaptation projects is limited. The project proponents explain that the lessons learned from past projects, including FAO aquaculture projects, have not been fully integrated into current policy frameworks. This has led to the scale-up of successful practices and addresses the weaknesses identified. However, the independent evaluation team suggests that the country needs to engage in more depth and focus on the activities that will bring about real transformational change.
15. The country needs to strengthen the institutional capacity to manage both climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts. This includes strengthening the institutional capacity for natural resource monitoring and management and the availability of public funding is limited, which makes it difficult for fishers to invest in new technologies. There are also issues responsible for the governance and management of the complex fisheries sector; as they lack relevant knowledge on its critical importance for the future of the country. There is also a lack of systematic data collection.
16. Overall, there is a need to strengthen institutional and implementation capacity in relevant institutions that undertake research and development, monitor, sustain and restore mangrove and fish resources and to support the most vulnerable fisher population in the country, as explained in the proposal's theory of change.


### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Alignment with national sector strategy

60. The project has been developed at the request of the Government of the Gambia, in close alignment with its national and sector policies and strategies on climate change. The project is aligned with the National Climate Change Strategy (2014) which aims to advance sustainable fisheries sector resistant to climate-related hazards. Moreover, the project also responds to the country's commitments under the Paris Agreement (Paris), third National Communication and second nationally determined contribution (2021) submitted under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

### GCF.31/2018/Add.01

#### Page 5 of 7

**vi.** The Secretariat is also closely aligned with the Gambia’s framework on climate change, namely the National Climate Change Policy 2018, which advocates promoting sustainable adaptive management of the fisheries resources. It also falls into the fisheries sector strategy of the country’s national development plan. This project will contribute to increased fish value-added, improved export earnings, greater employment opportunities and enhanced contributions towards improved nutritional status consistent with the rational exploitation of the resource base.

**vii.** The country developed the Fisheries Act 2007, the Fisheries Regulations 2008 and the Fisheries Management Plan 2013. However, the implementation of these laws and their enforcement and implementation are not explained in the proposal. In May 2012, the Gambia validated the new national fisheries management plan (NFM) for the period 2012-2016. The project will support and implement a cooperation framework with Sengal for the sustainable management and utilization of the common marine resources of the Senegal river through a joint Management Committee to be established in accordance with the agreement between the two countries, with indicators and reference points for monitoring the performance of the fisheries resources.

**viii.** The project will also contribute towards the implementation of other national policies such as the small-scale fish feed mills project (FAO) and the Protection of the Canary Current Large Marine Ecosystems, a joint project of FAO, the Global Environment Facility and the United Nations Development Programme. However, these projects are not explained in detail. Since the project explains its theory of change on barriers to be addressed that the results of these projects will have an impact on, it is important to understand how this will happen as past experiences specifically explain. According to FAO, the project design takes into consideration the lessons learned from previous interventions and addresses some technical issues such as the reduction in pressure from unsustainable harvesting on mangrove forests, both through the development of a sustainable fishery management plan and reducing the use of wood fuel and through improving oyster cultivation methods in mangroves.

**ix.** However, it is important to analyse the challenges in terms of the Gambia’s institutional capacity for implementing these projects as well as its ability to reduce its dependency on the international institutions in delivering climate change related projects.

#### 5.2.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

**x.** FAO will serve as the accredited entity and executing entity for this project. As such, it will be responsible for the overall management of this project. FAO is the agency in the United Nations system that leads international efforts to defeat hunger. It aims to make sure that all people have access to sufficient, safe, nutritious food to maintain a healthy and active life.

**xi.** The role of FAO as accredited entity will be attributed to the relevant offices and divisions of the organization. The project will be implemented through the office of West Africa located in Dakar, Senegal, and the office of the FAO Representation in the Gambia, which has strong development cooperation experience with the country. Since its establishment at the creation of the country office in 1978, FAO has provided vital support to the sustainable development of agriculture and food security for millions of people in the country, having implemented 130 projects.

**xii.** The project will be executed by the Government of the Gambia through the Ministry of Agriculture and the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) and the Ministry of Fisheries, Water Resources and National Assembly Meeting (MoFWRAM) and FAO is in a co-execution modality to jointly deliver this project. As the executing entity for the project as well as the FAO Representation in the Gambia, it will be responsible for the management of the GCF proceeds and it will bear the overall responsibility for fulfilling its relevant executing entity functions.

this project technical assistance will be provided by mobilizing FAD experts or FAO-supervised consultants and procured parties.

### Project Steering Committee (PSC)

The project explains that the Government of The Gambia acting through MoFWARN and MoA will be executing entity-only for the activities and resources. Moreover, the Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established by the FAO in Banjul, The Gambia. These arrangements, as explained below, leave an open question on the role of the PSC in terms of its composition, responsibilities, and decision-making powers upon project completion.

#### Technical Advisory Committee (TAC)

The TAC will also have a Technical Advisory Committee (TAC) responsible for the continuous technical oversight and support to project implementation. The core membership of the TAC will be drawn from the following government departments: Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (Permanent Secretary level or equivalent) from the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (National Project Director), MoFWARN, MoA, MoWR, MoFPA, MoNRM, MoGGR (representing the private sector), and MoFA.

### Engagement with civil society organizations

The project presents evidence of two consultation workshops on 25 February and 29 April 2014 in Banjul, The Gambia. These consultations were held to ensure the active participation of national stakeholders. The consultations involved the communities through the National Federation of Gambian Women, the TRT Oyster Women’s Association, the Gambia Fishermen’s Union, and others.

### Field Missions

Moreover, field missions were developed, visiting the project sites and allowing for exchange visits between the project team and local actors in a very structured manner on these projects. The field consultation allowed FAO to confirm the feasibility and relevance of the project design.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A

73. The total cost of the project is estimated at USD 42.2 million, including a proposed GCF grant of USD 5.0 million from FID in the form of a grant financed by IFAD or USD 5.0 million and in-kind co-financing by MoWIRAM of USD 2.0 million.

74. The finance provided by IFAD is granted under its Debt Sustainability Facility to finance the project components that are most vulnerable to climate change and climatic impacts, such as irrigation systems, water management infrastructure, and nutrition-sensitive agricultural technologies and practices as well as value chain and market linkage activities. In addition, a new component will be introduced in the form of an integrated vegetable garden, where PROFISHERA aquaculture activities will be introduced. To enhance the project’s efficiency and effectiveness, the following measures are proposed: (i) to introduce closure packages (and the most important ones in terms of the budget of output 2.2) build on the significant experience gained from the implementation of the pilot phase; (ii) to ensure that the PRODIT project will invest in irrigated rice schemes, the provision of inputs and agronomic training. PROFISHERA will use top-up funding for the adjustment of the design and by providing support to start-ups and training.

75. The project provides an economic analysis involving the different components of the project. The results show that all four aquaculture packages are profitable, with a return on investment (from currently 25 per cent and expected business as usual scenario with climate change of 30 per cent to a final target of 35 per cent post-harvest loss) and a constant volume of landed fish, the USD 1.8 million annual cost of inputs and services, and the USD 46.7 million annual revenue. The internal rate of return is 41.8 per cent and net present value as a social discount rate of 6 per cent (the IRR is 35.2 per cent) is USD 90.5 million. This analysis shows that all four packages reflect the investment and the volumes of fish, with positive results in all cases.

76. For the four proposed aquaculture packages, a financial analysis was conducted over 10 years. Under business as usual conditions, the aquaculture activity is profitable, with significant returns for the project beneficiaries and with internal rates of return (from currently 25 per cent and expected business as usual scenario with climate change of 30 per cent to a final target of 35 per cent post-harvest loss) and constant volume of landed fish. The USD 1.8 million annual cost of inputs and services, the USD 46.7 million annual revenue, commercial loan financing or a mix of concessional and commercial loans show lower or even negative returns for the project beneficiaries. This analysis shows that all four packages highlight the importance of fish prices in generating adequate returns for the beneficiaries. In all packages, the return on investment is positive (from currently 25 per cent and expected business as usual scenario with climate change of 30 per cent to a final target of 35 per cent post-harvest loss) and the project’s interventions along the value chain for better marketing and value addition to remove these risks. However, in the view of the independent TAF, the project should work to ensure a better price for fish products and to reduce the costs of inputs and services, which will impede the scaling of the aquaculture models.


### 1.6.1 Cost-benefit analysis

78. The project is expected to generate significant reductions in the use of fossil fuels and greenhouse gas emissions reductions expected to be generated by the project. However, high estimates of carbon prices used by the European Union Emissions Trading System and the World Bank ranging from USD 40/CO2 per year to USD 150/CO2 per year, the project estimates an economic value generated of USD 1.1–2.7 million.

79. Currently, three activities have been considered as playing an enabling role for the fisheries and aquaculture activities described above and as such, they do not generate their own benefit streams.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP believes that the overall objective of the project to support the resilience of the Gambian artisanal fisher community is well intended, but that the project involves too many activities in the coastal, mangrove and inland areas, which should be monitored separately. The TAP recommends that the project should start by creating a set of baseline indicators and a monitoring system that could be managed with the government.

On the other hand, one of the project objectives is to support policies at the national level, but the proponents have not provided an analysis of the specific policies and regulatory framework in place. The TAP recommends that the Board approve the project subject to the condition that the proponents provide a second opinion on the policy delivery by the Accredited Entity to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat.

A monitoring and reporting framework document, with concrete set of baseline indicators that should be the basis for the monitoring of outcomes and outputs of the project, tagged to each objective, is recommended. The TAP recommends that the second opinion on policy delivery by the Accredited Entity to the GCF should include at least: the current status of fish-catchments, income and prices, food loss measurement, level of food security of the direct beneficiaries (e.g., households, communities, etc.), poverty levels (household/house), leakages related to the restoration and protection of mangrove forests in selected areas.

project areas, level of water salinity, and social indicators and economic indicators of the selected beneficiaries; and

(b) A document containing a policy analysis around artisanal fisheries development and social and economic welfare of the fisher communities in the host country, specifying the concrete expected outcomes (in terms of the policies that will be proposed) as per Section R.2(c) of the Funding Proposal

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP188)

## Proposal name: Climate Resilient Fishery Initiative for Livelihood Improvement in the Gambia (PROREFISH Gambia)

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/ies:
Republic of The Gambia

### Project/programme size:
Small

## Impact potential

The AE would like to highlight that this is the first GTZ project with a strong focus on fisheries. Given Theory of Change, the climate change issues and barriers related to artisanal fisheries in The Gambia, a Least Developed Country, need to be addressed through a holistic approach to generate sustained adaptation impact by strengthening (i) fisherfolk livelihoods and their adaptive capacity; (ii) institutional capacity for sustainable management and individual capacity to address climate change. The climate resilient artisanal fisheries ecosystems and infrastructures will be developed in the context of the expected paradigm shift and be scaled up without the institutional capacity development component and enabling environment for private sector investment promoted by component 3.

FAO, through other projects, has been and is supporting regional efforts to improve transparency and traceability of fish trade and improving access to information on fish monitoring by CECAF. The inter-ministerial collaboration and related capacity development promoted through the Community Fisheries Centres (CFCs) and the government counterparts will be active contributing partners in project implementation and will not accommodate a progressive handover from FAO to the national authorities for planning and monitoring. Mangrove restoration and aquaculture activities are co-executed and co-financed.

## Paradigm shift potential

In addition to the elements of response provided above regarding project coherence and consistency (see Theory of Change and Adaptation Barriers in Para 33 of the FP), FAO is pleased to confirm that the project design and implementation modalities have been ensured to be consistent with the national fisheries strategy and aquaculture strategy. The Community Fisheries Centres (CFCs) and the government counterparts will be active contributing partners in project implementation and will not accommodate a progressive handover from FAO to the national authorities for planning and monitoring. Mangrove restoration and aquaculture activities are co-executed and co-financed.


---
**Page 139**
---

by government institutions (Ministry of Fisheries, Water Resources and National Assembly Matters [MoFWRMN] and Ministry of Agriculture [MoA]). The AE confirms that the project will assess, at inception phase, the gap in policies and implementation capacity. The project will support the implementation of the Fishery Policy through strengthening the planning, monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) system. This will be achieved by establishing a solid MEL (monitoring, evaluation and learning) system, that will provide evidence for wider integration of climate change related use to promote reforms related to climate change and fisheries in The Gambia.


# Sustainable development potential

## Needs of the recipient

*TAP assessment is acknowledged.*


### Country ownership

The project was designed through a highly participatory approach which generated a high degree of country ownership and Government support. FAO, et al., will work in close collaboration with the co-financing and co-executing Entities (MoFWRMN and MoA), Inland aquaculture farmers’ associations, and other stakeholders to ensure that the project avoids dispersion of project management efforts. To ensure effective and efficient management of the fisheries and forestry resources of the country the project will work extensively in close collaboration with key national government agencies and non-governmental organizations, such as the TRY (Oyster Women’s Association) [TOWA], with over 600 members, the National Fish Farmers’ Association (NFFA), the National Aquaculture Fish Cultivation, the independent Institute for Social Research and Development (ISRAID), National Association of Artisanal Fisheries Operators (NAFAFO), the National Solo Fishery Co-Management Committee (NSFCC), the Gambian Artisanal Fishers’ Development Agency (GAMFDA). These NGOs and CSO have supported projects in the forestry and fisheries sectors and will be key partners to deliver a number of services.

In addition to specific capacity strengthening in Component 3, the project will systematically transfer knowledge and skills from the national level to the local level through a two-span technical functions (extension, monitoring, reforestation, co-management of landing sites) as well as planning/management (annual workplan and budgets, communication).

Finally, the project has a highly participatory governance, coordinated under the Project Steering Committee (PSC), which will be chaired by the MoFWRMN.


---
**Page 140**
---

## Efficiency and effectiveness

The market and sector analysis supporting PREBETH design shows substantial efficiency and effectiveness. The mix of oil and hard investment will be in the gap for innovative technologies, knowledge and skills (public and private) to overcome market failures (adaptation barriers, IP page 33). The three interrelated components will strengthen (I) fisher body innovation capacity, (II) national fisheries policy and governance, and (III) national capacity to address climate change.

Community Fisheries Centres and Community Forest Management Centres will receive a significant share of the investment in modern landing facilities, low-emissions fish processing equipment (84% of Ck or 57% of the total cost), as well as tenure on improved management practices. The project will also support the development of new business strategies for artisanal fish folk and women and equitable partnerships building with commercial buyers (“insert no fisher” roundtables) will generate increased margins and value addition, especially for high-end local consumption and export (IP part 4/7).

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The AE acknowledges ITAP overall assessment and recommendation for the Board approval. FAD confirms that the proposed ITAP conditions can be met prior to second disbursement.

FAD would like to confirm that the project’s logframe includes relevant indicators for monitoring progress, including key outcomes on improved resilience of fishing communities’ livelihoods and food security.

Regular surveys (Activity 3.1.2), together with the baseline survey (to be carried out in year 1) and the annual outcome surveys, will track key indicators covering (i) fisherfolk livelihoods, (ii) access to markets and value addition, (iii) fisheries management-related aspects, and access to basic services; (iv) food security. The project will also develop the Government’s capacity to engage in nation-wide monitoring of mangrove forests to assess leakage.

The results of the baseline survey will serve to provide additional information for a dedicated focused study on the socio-economic impacts of climate change and sustainable fisheries development, carried out in the first year of the project (Activity 3.2.2). This will include an updated and complementary identification of policy and regulatory framework constraints to the climate resilience of artisanal fisheries in the Gambia.
