# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

## TRANSFORMING GENDER ASSESSMENT INTO ACTION PLAN FOR MAINSTREAMING

Women comprise a great majority of the workers in the fishery sector in The Gambia at about 80% of the sector's labor force. Women do not participate in all subsectors of fishing, as work is differentiated by gender. The Gambian women are not involved in capture of marine resources, except for shellfish which is increasingly becoming a major source of income for women. The women also have limited access to quite margins than for the export market and are women's domain. The widespread illiteracy among women (information, technical and financial barriers) makes it difficult for them to access the formal sector, which includes credit facilities, extension services and training opportunities. Women's participation in fisheries is difficult, is more often than not constrained with men-in-mind (technical and institutional barriers). Productive activities are also limited by lack of access to productive resources such as land, water, tools and inputs in the form of machinery. Extension services for women's subsector are rare (technical and institutional barriers), and illiteracy forces women to cede the Treasurer’s positions of their organizations to men (informational barrier).

Despite these setbacks, women processors and sellers in the fishery sector generate more income than their husbands. They have complete autonomy over their incomes and finance almost single-handedly their children's education and everyday household needs. Men's monetary contribution to these matters is at an all-time low. Women are also more likely to be involved in decision-making power within the family, words there is disagreement with their husbands so that the marriage is not negatively affected (social barrier). Close to half of the Gambian women think husbands have the right to beat them if they are not satisfied with their performance at home. The majority of these women also believe that men have very limited opportunities to study or earn a living, the only option left for them is usually early marriage, which perpetuates the vicious circle in the women's socio-economic status.

The most fundamental constraints confronting the women in the fishery sector face are gender imparity in decision-making power and illiteracy. The impacts of these change on women are considered much larger than men as, their lack of literacy and numeracy skills make women inept at conceiving strategic actions to improve their livelihoods. Women's limited access to land, water, tools and inputs and access to productive resources among women do not give them the full range of adaptation options that would be available under gender parity, thus negatively affecting their business as well as management of household resources.

The project tackles these root causes through activities to improve decision-making parity, involving women, men and community gatekeepers and applying household methodology. The discussions under this methodology will include sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) and gender-based violence (GBV). A task force will be established with the aim of developing a framework for community-driven support measures against them. These measures are to mitigate the possibilities that the engagement of women in new activities and their acquisition of new skills upsets the current gender-based power relations within households. The project will also establish a mechanism to address Microfinance of GABM will be strengthened to handle such incidents in an inclusive, survivor-centered manner. A task force will be established with the aim of developing a framework for community-driven support measures against them. These measures are to mitigate the possibilities that the engagement of women in new activities and their acquisition of new skills upsets the current gender-based power relations within households. The project will also establish a mechanism to address Microfinance of GABM will be strengthened to handle such incidents in an inclusive, survivor-centered manner.

The effectiveness of these interventions rests on the willingness of the targeted groups to learn from the trainings and dialogues, which is why community gatekeepers are involved in the process.


---
**Page 148**
---

The project will make use of rural young women with intermediate level of education—the group which suffer from one of the highest unemployment rates—to help the older generation of women to gain literacy and numeracy skills. Women will also be trained on sustainable environmental management under climate change and negotiation skills. In order to tailor the intervention more closely to the needs and strengths of the target group, a participatory approach was adopted. This involved: identifying location, dimensions, efficacy and effects of infrastructure that concern them and appropriateness of exact intervention sites; invited to observe or participate in the construction processes; consulted on the voice-mail messages and other communication tools used for information dissemination and education including monitoring and evaluation of mangroves; and be part of planning and executing various campaigns as well as community-level decision making.


### LEGAL, ADMINISTRATIVE AND SPIRITUAL FRAMEWORK


#### Constitution and National Laws:
The Constitution of The Gambia 1970 declares that women and men are equal in all domains of life, but also incorporates the customary law (and/or), which are not entire compatible with international human rights standards. This has led to the exclusion of women, youth and children draw from the treaties that the country ratified, but their effective implementation has been hampered by the time- honored patriarchal norms and taboos.


#### Policies:
Both the National Gender Policy 2010-2020 and Agriculture and Natural Resources (ANN) Policy 2017-2030 highlight the importance of women in agriculture sector as well as the low productivity of female farmers. One of the objectives of the ANN Policy is to improve participation and representation of female and youth farmers. Lack of negotiation skills among women is among the concerns highlighted by the ANN Policy.


#### The National Climate Change (CC) Policy 2016 aims for achieving, among others, environmental justice and addressing social inequalities, particularly relating to gender, age, infinity and socioeconomic status, which would be aggravated by climate change.[^] With respect to forests, the policies underlines the necessity of adopting fuel-efficient cooking stoves and forest restoration, such as that of mangrove systems.


#### The National Youth Policy 2010-2028 recognises the youth as the most important and valuable resource as well as the agriculture as the source of livelihood for many young people. It lists increasingly high number of school dropouts and early marriages and teenage pregnancies as a threat to this sector. The ANN Policy uses out-migration of rural youths as an obstacle to the vibrancy of the sector. According to the CC Policy, the youth are vital important in the country’s response to climate change.


#### Government Officials:
In terms of the training provided by the women and youth, the government officials interviewed were most concerned about the loss of livelihoods due to climate change and lack of capacity to cope with it. According to them, fishery specific issues were: illegal fishing by industrial fleets; and lack of mangrove restoration and protection. Other issues identified included: poor access to markets; limited literacy/numeracy skills; access to funds; and voice. Additional problems identified were: gender-based domestic violence; unbalanced work responsibilities between genders; poverty of freshwater in the rural areas; and unsustainable use of natural resources. The officials also highlighted the lack of funding, training and implementation. Landing Site Managers did not consider women as landing site users, although they are physically present at the sites. In addition to this, the officials were concerned about the introduction of a relatively new topic to the policymakers, the officials consulted often saw the problems of women and youth together.


[^]


---
**Page 149**
---

Religion: Religions do not rule many aspects of the lives of the Gambians, but are the cornerstones with respect to women’s reproductive health, marriage and inheritance.

### Gender and Youths in Rural Gambia

Women and the youth – including both male and female – are often treated together as one group. While rural women face particularly disadvantaged conditions and deserve attention as a group on their own, their plight is usually in the shadow of broader issues of women or of development. According to the latest Census in 2013, there were about 45% of the population aged 13-39 and over half lived outside the capital. In 2020, it was estimated that slightly over 40% of the population lived in urban areas.

#### Basic Statistics – Gender Parity

According to the Human Development, Gender Development and Gender Inequality Indices of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), Gambia was one of the least developed and the least gender unequal in the world in 2019. Gender inequality is said to be manifestation of social norms and practices that are discriminatory against women.

#### Basic Statistics – Population

The population growth rate peaked in 1984 at 5.0% and was 2.3% in 2019, which resulted in a large youth population (about 35% in 2013). More than 60% of youths lived in the rural areas at the time of last Census in 2013.

#### Basic Statistics – Literacy and Education

A mere 34% of women aged 15 and above and 51% of men aged 15 and above had completed secondary education. The literacy rate for women was 68% and that for men at 64% and 71%, respectively in 2015. The school enrolment rate increased rapidly, but a non-trivial proportion of children were still out of school in 2017, with higher percentage for girls. The dropout rate has been high due to early marriage, lack of access to education, poverty, gender discrimination and forced marriages, teenage pregnancy and child labour. The numeracy skills of children behind their reading age are poor: only 9% of children demonstrated satisfactory skill in 2018. Girls who had satisfactory numeracy skills led by three percentage points in the country, but the rural girls with satisfactory skills were less than 2%.

#### Basic Statistics – Poverty

Urban poverty is more concentrated on women and individuals in three dimensions: health, education and standard of living. In 2019, 38% of urban dwellers practiced in severe multidimensional poverty. It is estimated that the later generations will add a great number of additional people into poverty.

#### Basic Statistics – Employment

It is believed that 70% of Gambian women are engaged in low productivity rural subsistence agriculture. Informal employment is higher for females at all education levels, but the gender gap is wider when the highest education level attained is secondary or tertiary. Around two-thirds than half of the persons aged 13-39 were economically active and comprised 70% of all unemployed. Gender gaps in employment are also evident: informal employment or work is the largest when the highest education level attained is intermediate. Rural unemployment is low for all ages. Emigration is widespread, generating 15% of Gross Domestic Product in 2019.

#### Basic Statistics – Marriage

Nearly 90% of women aged 15-49 were married by age 20 and 87% by the age of 25 in 2018. Compared to men, women are more likely to marry young: half of the women aged 15-24 and married had a spouse who was older by ten years or more. For women, more than often there are no meaningful alternatives to marriage including work opportunities in the rural areas. The statistics imply


---
**Page 150**
---

that women’s empowerment influences the types of unions, including involvement in transactional intimacy, as they depend on gendered economic inequalities.

### Basic Statistics – Religion
Well over 95% of the population in the country were estimated Muslims in 2018. It is believed that religious beliefs and practices, especially regional and religious lines had reached high levels, which are likely to affect proportionately more the most vulnerable.

### Labor Division
Women and men are not only different actors of the same value chains, but also operate on different scales and serve different markets. Anything meant for local consumption is women’s work and of low profit margins. Markets that charging and carrying goods are dominated by men. Most households rely on small-scale agriculture or livestock raising on a subsistence basis, even if their main livelihood is in fishery sector. Commerce is seen as the most lucrative, human trafficking and related exploitation appear to be more prevalent among targeted women.

### Decision Making – Marriage
Men are the default heads of household and men and women are consulted about decisions related to their work, but do not have the same decision-making power as adult men. In case the parents are involved in the same work as the youth, the parents speak for all. At home, women make most of the decisions regarding children’s education, health care, and property such as inheritance, but the husband is the ultimate decision makers of each household. Women have complete autonomy over their own income, which is used to run the household, supplemented by husband's income.

About one in three women in The Gambia experience sexual or physical violence in their lifetime. The latest pandemic has led to loss of jobs and questioning of men’s authority, which is likely to have driven men to violence. In 2018, more than three-quarters of girls and women aged 15-49 had undergone some form of sexual violence at least once in their lives. Women are also more vulnerable in rural areas. Nearly 50% of women were of the opinion in 2018 that beating the wife was justified when: the wife neglects the children; the burns the food, she argues with him, she goes out without telling him; or she refuses to have sex with him. The majority of women (63%) agreed that it is better for a woman to be beaten by her husband than to live in poverty.

Access to Resources and Services
Women do not own land because they are given this role only if male family members die, so landless women crops as land owners are usually for subsistence purposes. Women are often allotted the least attractive units of infrastructure. Extension services are unsatisfactory, in particular, extension officers are more likely to be men and are less likely to visit women than men or men. Formal credit is hard to come by in the rural areas, and combined with illiteracy, women rely on borrowing from family and friends. Women’s access to mobile phones was on par with that of men's. Gender-based division of work confers on women less profitable markets and little investment.

Access to Resources and Services
Women do not own land because they are given this role only if male family members die, so landless women crops as land owners are usually for subsistence purposes. Women are often allotted the least attractive units of infrastructure. Extension services are unsatisfactory, in particular, extension officers are more likely to be men and are less likely to visit women than men or men. Formal credit is hard to come by in the rural areas, and combined with illiteracy, women rely on borrowing from family and friends. Women’s access to mobile phones was on par with that of men's. Gender-based division of work confers on women less profitable markets and little investment.

Women in fisheries know that mangroves are indispensable for their livelihoods, but many continue to harvest the mangroves for fuel and roofing, while being aware that the mangroves will continue to diminish with the current management practices. Women listed insufficient quantity as one of the most concerning factors affecting their ability to meet their needs and acquire access to provisions at home. Gender-based division of work confers on women less profitable markets and little investment.

10


---
**Page 151**
---

Water and Sanitation: More than 90% of the population had access to an improved source of water in 2018. The time required to reach an improved drinking-water source was 30 minutes or more in the rural areas. It was women over 15 years of age who collected water. Less than 2% of households in the rural areas had piped water, and only about half of these were connected to a public water supply system that was available when needed. Improved sanitation was observed less among urban rural than among urban households.

Energy: The vast majority used solid fuel, and little over half used three-stone stove or open fire. The fuel was primarily wood and charcoal; almost all was wood in the rural areas. It was the women who collected or purchased fuel. Artisanal smoking and drying of fish were estimated larger in terms of fuel use than the rest of the food sector and inefficient with landed fish. Electric lighting was available to over half of the population.

Health: Life expectancy at birth in 2018 for Gambian women and men was roughly on par with the Sub-Saharan average. More than half of the deaths were caused by communicable diseases, maternal, perinatal, neonatal, and nutritional conditions, and the leading causes of death among the health sector. The prevalence of undernourishment was 12%; the rate has been constantly below the Sub-Saharan average. Micronutrient deficiencies are common in pregnant women, adolescent girls, children under-five, and under-five children most. The country was evaluated medium by the World Health Organization (WHO) Classification of malnutrition for stunting and for wasting, while high for underweight in 2018.

Women aged 15-24 from the poorest households engaged more in sex than women of the same age group but from richer households. Women's education level or household wealth did not clearly affect their behavior as they do for women; men's engagement in sex slightly increased with education level and was the highest for those from the richest households. The fertility rate has been declining since 1980, falling from 5.2 births per woman in 6.4 in 1983 to 4.4 in 2018. The adolescent fertility rate expressed as number of births per 1,000 women aged 15-19 also declined from 200 in 1983 to 76 in 2018; the number remained high in the rural areas at 108. Health education and counseling by the adolescents, and abortion consultation is considered a major factor in the high maternal mortality rate.

Coping with Negative Shocks: Limitations in fiscal, monetary, and financial buffers do not allow the country to establish a full-fledged system of social safety nets. The practice of informal social protection is based on family support and community solidarity. This is more common among the employed and used method in the rural areas.

Aspirations of the Youth: Many youths believe that self-employment through small enterprises is the way to improve their lives and mentioned acquiring skills. For training and capital for such endeavors, donor support was expected. Prosperity was the social goal for them.


SOCIAL PARTICIPATION OF RURAL GAMBIAN WOMEN

Rural Women's Voice in Politics: Gambian women have made a big progress in terms of political representation at national level—about 10% of National Assembly members and nearly 15% of regional governors/mayors were women in 2015—but women's voice was not as prominent at the local level—slightly over 1% of local ward councillors were women in 2015.

**Page 152**
---

# Risks and Opportunities Facing Rural Gambian Women

## Livelihoods under Climate Change
The women in the fishery sector are mostly illiterate and acquired professional skills through informal training. They have not yet adapted to climate change on their work, but do not have a concrete strategy for the future. The youth facing a similar situation are also yet to embark on a new career adapted to climate change. Aquaculture could help the women in this sector to adapt to climate change.

## New Activities and Unpaid Work
If women are to take up new activities, household members who are not participating in household chores and caregiving must shoulder some of the women's share. If the country is to make the best use of its human resources, all must be given decision-making power regardless of gender. The project will provide training for women on how to advocate for their rights and guide them to more equitable participation in family life. The project personnel may use their power related to the project and engage in sexual exploitation, abuse or harassment (SEAH), or gender-based violence (GBV). The project will ensure that all staff are trained on SEAH and GBV, including how to report when the prevailing gender-based power-division and provide SEAH and GBV by their male family members and other men. The project will also provide training for women on how to advocate for their rights and guide personnel on the subject. FAO Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) will be strengthened so that SEAH and GBV related grievances are adequately managed in inclusion, survivor-centred and gender-responsive ways throughout the project cycle.

## Awareness of Community Leaders
Sensitization of community gatekeepers will be necessary for them to understand the soft issues that prevent women from achieving their full potential and to approve equal distribution of decision-making power between wives and husbands. The community gatekeepers will also be involved in community-driven support measures against SEAH and GBV.

## Limitations from Illiteracy
Many women are illiterate enough to generate more income than their husbands, but they lack the skills or access to financial services, especially when it comes to finance. Women are also reluctant to expand their business and enter the formal sector. Their business acumen could be further sharpened with more information, but unlikely if written information remains off-limits to them.

## Negotiation Skills
Lack of negotiation skills is often cited as one of the prominent factors that hinders women from realizing their full economic potential. While female-headed households without male relatives are more likely to benefit from training with them, negotiation skills will give them access to some income generating opportunities as other women.


12


---
**Page 153**
---

# Social Integration of Female Youth

Poverty and patriarchy pose high risks to female youth of falling victim to violence and at being deprived of opportunities for self-fulfillment. Meaningful empowerment is rare; the unemployment rate for females with intermediate levels of education is higher than that for male youths with the same level of education or for female youths with other levels of education. Teenage pregnancy rates are high. Young women in rural areas could be integrated in the society through work that makes good use of their strength: higher literacy and numeracy skills compared to that of rural women of older generations.

# Ownership, Sustainability and Empowerment

Involvement of women-users in location selection, design, installation and maintenance of equipment to be introduced will not only improve ownership and sustainability of the project but also enhance the capacity of women to make decisions regarding land and skills related to the tools of their trade. Women do not think that they are responsible for safeguarding the environment, limiting the sustainability of their actions.

# PRINCIPLES OF PROJECT FORMULATION AND IMPLEMENTATION

## Principles of Project Formulation
Women will be enabled for strategic decision making and actions, while female youth will be empowered to take action based on their strength – stronger literacy and numeracy skills than the women of the older generations. Often highlighted in various gender assessments of the country as the weak points, will be included in professional training of the project. The project will also build on the strengths of women.

## Principles of Project Implementation
The project will build on and enhance the strengths of women by filling the investment gap left by the society. Female youths will be recognized as a distinct group with their own strengths, which can be leveraged for the whole communities. Gender empowerment will be supported through capacity building in areas such as negotiation skills, conflict resolution, awareness raising and gender-based violence – of technical officers, executing agencies and community gatekeepers. The project will also build on the strengths of women to ensure that they are able to manage such incidents in an inclusive, survivor-centred and gender-responsive way. The project will support establishment and operations of referral pathways for gender-based violence. Awareness on the environmental impacts of the project will be raised among all stakeholders involved in project planning and household management sustainable by taking the environmental impacts into account. Stakeholders must be consulted for organizational/legislative matters and content of all activities, especially women who have been historically marginalized or whose contributions to development have often been seen as unimportant. The project's Technical Advisory Committee as a member will include as members a gender equality mission expert, the representatives of the Ministries of Women's Affairs, Youth and Sports and of female professional associations related to the project, such as oyster harvesters and lawyers.

The indicators of the Gender Action Plan will be monitored by the Environmental and Social Safeguards and Gender Specialist for adaptive management.

# PART I: GENDER ASSESSMENT

## 1. INTRODUCTION

### 1.1 Project Introduction from Gender Perspective

1. Between 25,000 and 30,000 people were engaged in artisanal fishery and about 2,000 people were employed in the industrial sub-sector in 2014[1] in a country of slightly over 2 million. The Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources estimates that about 80% of people engaged in the sector are women.[2]
   Small pelagic fish such as sardellina, bonga, horse mackerel and mackerel serve as important protein sources for the population.

2. Around the world, a significant proportion of workers in the fishery and aquaculture sectors are women, mostly responsible for non-vessel related activities, such as processing and marketing.[3] And it is indeed the case in The Gambia. Women and men are not only different actors of the same value chains, but also have different roles within them. This division of labor by gender[4] means differentiation of products by gender.[5] Anything meant for local consumption was women’s work and it is not limited to the fishery sector; but also exists in crop cultivation and animal rearing, although the boundaries can be fluid for crops.

3. The division of economic activities by gender is based on the social norm that women are responsible for household production and care giving.[6] This means that women’s work remains in unequal access to productive assets, which does not allow meeting the rigor of non-local markets. Women in the sector do no have formal schooling, except for the younger generations. They learned the trade from their mothers or other relatives and they are not equipped with the necessary skills. As a result, they are at great a disadvantage when the business owners document writtens, especially financial matters, putting them at the mercy of men.[7]

4. An evaluation in the fishery sector in The Gambia in 2014 identified integration of gender considerations into policy design and implementation of infrastructure as one of the most critical issues.[8] The project proposes installation of the FAO-Thiayoue fish processing technique (FTT), which has been adopted in many Sub-Saharan countries[9] and reduces the amount of required fuel, a primary concern to women.

The processors (all of whom are women).12 It hence lessens generation of smoke, polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons and other substances harmful to health. The lack of proper ventilation at the smokeries is also a major concern.

5 Women in the sector are well aware that their livelihoods are dependent on mangroves and that the trees have been diminishing, a trend that is likely to continue with the current management practices.13 They have not completely eliminated their habits to cut mangroves for fuel or house roofs,14 while knowledge of alternative uses of mangroves has not yet reached them.15 Women are also less participating in a mangrove reforestation project recognize that the mangroves can be brought back to good health with their own efforts. The project proposes mangrove rehabilitation and environmental awareness campaigns as well as training on alternative livelihoods, such as beekeeping and other methods.

6 Along the Gambian coast, the amount of fish and shellfish catch has been decreasing and the oyster season shortening, both of which have been attributed to climate change and overexploitation. The Gambian households do not specialize in one type of livelihood, and the farming households have the potential to diversify their income sources by engaging in aquaculture.16 Women are interested in aquaculture. The women and the youth consulted were willing to take up aquaculture if there is an opportunity, just as they took up fish farming with support from partners in the past years, generating a considerable amount of cash income for the women involved.

7 Full integration of aquaculture into women’s daily activities will necessitate reduction of their other duties, as they are in charge of housekeeping and caregiving, in addition to their work for cash income.17 Women who have been involved in fish farming or aquaculture in the past years do not engage as the society does not prescribe to do so. Women harvesters, processors and sellers often earn more than men but put their children through school with their income, while men contribute to the daily household expenses.18 The women are also less likely to be involved in decision-making processes for the family.19 In addition, there is a cultural belief that it is not appropriate for the woman to believe that her husband’s beating his wife is justified when she neglects the children, argues with him, goes out without telling him, etc.20 They also as the men have the final say in strategic matters of the family and the women are less likely to be involved in decision-making processes.21 Women and husbands need to engage in honest conversations on what they wish for the family and construct a common vision such as well as a roadmap.

8 Women put more rice on the table than their husbands, but they often delegate the role of treasurer of the household associations to literate men. The lack of written numeracy skills also leave the women vulnerable to financial fraud. Women are reluctant to expand their small business and enter

the formal sector, as they view it as a men’s world, most certainly intimidated because of their lack of completion in literacy and numeracy. The Gambian women are savvy enough to earn respectable amount of cash without any formal education, but they have little information outside their immediate circles and are limited in strategic planning.


### 1.2 Objective of the Assessment

#### 1.2.1 Rationale
9 Climate change impacts the vulnerable members of the society proportionately more than others. It has been estimated that women and children are 14 times more likely to die during a disaster, which frequently occurs due to climate change. Therefore, in order to address the needs of these members in projects and policies is known to improve their outcomes.

10 The assessment examines the situation of female and youth workers engaged in fishery and farming with respect to climate change to find the most effective ways to reduce the vulnerability of the weak members of society. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change, as they represent an important disadvantaged group, half of which are women. The youth need to have the experience and training on the job today to be able to shoulder the national economy in the future.

11 The Gambia has ratified international agreements and goals related to the gender and children, most notably the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Convention on the Rights of the Children. The national laws on women’s and children’s rights are based on the international treaties that the country has ratified, other laws and the Constitution are not necessarily compatible with these.

12 The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) recognizes that gender equality is key to being able to achieve food security for all. As evident by FAO Policy on Gender Equity.[1] The Organization is of one mind with a wide range of global agreements and conventions that guide food security, nutrition, and sustainable development.

Every one of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) concerns the lives of women, youth and children, and The Gambia has adopted the Goals, whose overarching objective is to leave no one behind.


#### 1.2.2 Objective
13 The Gender Assessment for the Green Climate Fund (GGF) project, “Climate Resilient Fishery Initiative for Livelihood Improvement,” studies the socioeconomic conditions of women and men targeted by the project and examines how these conditions can be improved in order to contribute positively to the integration of gender dimensions into the project. The Assessment also briefly examines the youth for the same, as many policies often treat them as one group. The strategies for both are summarized as the Gender Action Plan.

14 The underlying theory of change is: the project will recognize the strengths, constraints and needs of the women that their overall goal of change will be realized for achieving the best outcomes possible under the proposed project. It will also bring about changes in the distribution of resources and decision-making power between women and men in the targeted communities. The same applies to the youth, as their capacities complementary to others' have not been fully taken advantage of.

15 While the proposed project focuses on one subsector of food production systems, i.e., fishery, the subsectors are connected in terms of inputs and outputs, especially for smallholders. The finite amount of land available in the targeted areas will determine how the productivity of one food production system is often used as inputs to others. The assessment examines the importance of women in the food production systems, while keeping the focus on fishery. It will also touch upon the role of youth, a group which comprises nearly one quarter of the total population in 2015,** but alternated and more likely to be unemployed than the older working population.**


# Methodology

The Gender Assessment focuses on the situation of women in fishing and crop agriculture in areas targeted by the proposed project: Banyankole, Kigezi and Kimbiri fishing sites; Tumbi Deficit Complex, and the farming areas of Nipembele and Mweso Lamin Kambo. It also examines the situation of youth, who comprise another vulnerable social group and are likely to be affected by and affect the project.

17 Consultations were the primary means to determine the key issues pertaining to the lives of women involved in fishing and in crop farming (Section 8.2 on the core questions asked). A total of 132 villagers in the target areas were consulted, of which 81 were men and 41 were aged 15-35. Women's views on their situation were also sought through focus group discussions with women. Women institutional meetings were held at national levels within some institutions and organizations. The issues thus identified were assessed in detail with the aid of literature search, whose results are summarized in this document.

The assessment was conducted by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) under the Food Security and Nutrition Sector Development Project (FASDEP) of the Global Agriculture and Food Security Program (GAFSP) in Nipembele and Lamin Kambo. The analysis elucidated the risks and opportunities for female workers engaged in fishing and crop agriculture, which were then used as inputs to inform project formulation.

18 To the best extent possible, the assessment is based on official statistics and published research results pertaining to and field assessments conducted in the target regions. Where relevant information could not be found or was limited on that subject, the assessment has relied upon such data as available; the assessment may refer to the general consensus among the professionals in the field or anecdotes.

3. LEGAL, ADMINISTRATIVE AND SPIRITUAL FRAMEWORK

### 3.1 Equality, Inclusion and Protection: Women, Youth and Children

#### 3.1.1 Treaties, Constitution and Law

19 As is the case with the majority of the countries in the world, The Gambia has ratified the core international treaties that enshrine equality between women and men in all spheres of life, most notably the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). It has also ratified the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights and the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights. The Gambia is a party to the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women. Other countries whose dominant population is of Muslim faith as is The Gambia, the country has not expressed reservations on the rights of women and children in view of contradiction with sharia.* However, it has not ratified the African Charter on Human and People’s Rights which promotes and protects human rights and basic freedoms in Africa.

| International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights | Ensures the equal right of women and men to the enjoyment of all economic, social and cultural rights. | Ratified |
| International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights | Declares that all people have the right of self-determination and the right to freely determine their political status and freely pursue their economic, social and cultural development. Together with the Universal Declaration of Human Rights and the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights it forms a comprehensive body of human rights law which promotes, protects and monitors human rights. | Ratified |

20 The Constitution of The Gambia 1997 declares under Section 28 Rights of Women that "[w]omen shall be accorded full and equal dignity of the person with men" and "shall have the right to equal treatment in all spheres of public and private life". In addition, women are entitled to education. They also accords right to identity and education in conformity with the international treaties, but some clauses of the Constitution and national legislations remain discriminatory against women. On the one hand, Section 28 of the Constitution provides that "women shall be accorded full and equal dignity of the person with men" and "the right to equal treatment with men, including equal opportunities in political life". On the other hand, Section 30 of the Constitution states: "[t]he principle of equality contradicts the CEDAW; it adds that "[i]n subject to the provisions of subsection (5), no law shall make any


---
**Page 160**
---

provision which is discriminatory either of itself or in effect.”] but subsection (5) (c) and (d) rule out application of non-discrimination principle on “adoption, marriage, divorce, burial, devolution of property on death” for members of persons of law” as well as “application in the case of members of a particular race or tribe of customary law with respect to any matter in the case of persons who, under that law, are subject to that law.

21 The Constitution states under Section 27 Right to marry that: "(1) Men and women of full age and capacity shall have the right to marry and found a family. (2) Marriage shall be based on the free and full consent of the intended parties.” It also guarantees the freedom “to practice any religion and to manifest it in worship, observance, practice, teaching, association and movement.” Section 20 of The Constitution accords political rights to all persons in The Gambia "without unreasonable restrictions." Similarly, the "Directive Principles of State Policy" of the Constitution contains a provision that "The State shall not discriminate against any citizen," but the Constitution adds that "[t]hese principles shall not confer legal rights or be enforced by law except as far as they are based on the ground.

22 The Constitution incorporates the customary law and Sharia by stipulating that: “In addition to this Constitution, the laws of the Gambia consist of— (a) Customary law so far as concerns members of the communities to which it applies; (b) The sharia so far as regards matters of marriage, divorce and inheritance among members of the communities to which it applies.” Over 95% of the population in the country were Muslims. Thus, the Constitution provides that “The laws of the Gambia shall be based on English law, customary law, and sharia’s;” thus based on English law, customary law, and sharia’s, where English law includes the common law, equity principles, statutes, and subordinate legislation, while customary law is administered by custom and Muslims. The customary law is administered by the District Tribunals and sharia’s by the qadis courts? on the matters of marriage, divorce and inheritance. Both courts are placed under the Magistrates’ Courts.”

23 The Alternative Dispute Resolution Act 2005 established an alternative dispute resolution secretariat to facilitate dispute resolution,” but most citizens are unaware of this mechanism and believe that court proceedings are the only manner to resolve disputes” in case dialogues at the ground level turn out ineffective. Neither are lawyers, judges and magistrates equipped with sufficient knowledge of

25 The Women’s Act 2010 covers a wide range of topics concerning women and was amended in 2015 to illegalize FGM, but does not prohibit practices harmful to women, such as levirate marriage and unequal inheritance.16 The Act, like the Constitution, contains provisions which allow personal law to prevail in matters relating to marriage, divorce, maintenance, guardianship of children, adoption and annulment of marriage; widows’ rights; and inheritance.17 The Sexual Offences Act 2013 does not recognize FGM as a crime under its jurisdiction but refers to the Criminal Code for offences on Gender-Based Violence against Women, Updating General Recommendation No. 19 issued in 2017.18

26 Various national laws are not necessarily in line with each other. The Women’s Act does not go beyond the Constitution on the subject of marriage. Hence, four types of marriages are recognized in the country: Islamic marriages governed by the Shari’a Marriage Act 1941; Christian marriages governed by the Christian Marriage Act 1862; civil marriages, governed by the Civil Marriage Act 1938; and customary marriages, influenced by Shari’a.19 At the same time, the Criminal Code of 1951, which prohibits bigamy, is still in force. The Code also criminalizes abduction and “seizement” of girls under age 16.

27 While the Women’s Act stipulates six months of maternity leave for all pregnant women in Part V “Prohibition of Discrimination against Women in Employment,” the Labour Act provides for 12 weeks of maternity leave. The Code also allows a woman to claim compensation from her employer if during her period of service with the same employer has been interrupted by one or more periods, none of which exceeds seven months and who has in aggregate not less than eighteen months service with the same employer. The Code provides that the amount of compensation shall be equal to 1/30th of his salary within the limits laid down by shari’ah, which is one-third of the estate to a woman.20 Exceptions similar to those at the Constitutional level apply in respect of marriage and divorce.21 The law also recognizes women’s right to parental property. It is subject to personal law and puts children born out of wedlock as well as girls at a disadvantage.22 The exception clause of the Act could in principle also be applied to child marriage.

28 The Trafficking in Persons Act 2007, Section 28, stipulates a fine not less than 50,000 Dalasi (roughly US$13,000) and imprisonment for up to five years. The Act provides for maximum sentences of minimum imprisonment of 15 years and maximum life.23 Under the Torture Offences Act 2003

# Section 13, buying, selling, preparing or trafficking in children or removing them from Gambia for human or any other form of exploitation and abuse

## Section 7 of the Act states that a "person who gets or holds a child so that he or she can be sexually abused by another person or so that the child can be used as a prostitute can be imprisoned for 10 years." The Trafficking in Persons Act 2007, Part II Application of Certain Provisions of the Criminal Code, makes trafficking punishable under the provisions of the Criminal Code, but the Trafficking Act imposes a penalty of five years' imprisonment for trafficking of a child (Section 13), while the Children's Act 2005 stipulates life imprisonment for trafficking of a person under age 18 (Section 4).

## 3.1.2 Forced Marriage: Law in Practice

### 29 In spite of Section 24 (Rights of Women) of the Constitution, a testimony of two women is equal to that of one man under the influential Sharia[60] for property, inheritance has been governed mostly by Sharia and customary law.[61] The patriarchal and patrilineal nature of customary law has resulted in exclusion of women from inheritance rights. According to customary law, a woman inherits her husband's property unless she remarries a member of her husband’s family.[62] Women are thus increasingly taking their grievances over inheritance issues to the court[63] and are often supported by those who are or are frowned upon and occasionally shunned by the male members of their families.[64]

### 30 Under the customary law, it is the husband who owns the house of the couple and decides whether the wife may claim the rights to the house after separation, divorce or annulment of marriage, while the wife has no right over her husband’s property. The wife also does not have any right over joint property derived from the marriage.[65] The decisions are usually made without taking into account the wife's contribution to the acquisition of the property.[66] Upon annulation of marriage, women are often asked to leave their homes and properties.

### 31 Most marriages are not registered with the government and many instances of forced marriage are not reported. [67] Polygamy is most commonly found West and Central Africa, and The Gambia ranks third highest in the region of 16 countries at 30% of individuals belonging to polygamous households in contrast to 2% worldwide.[68] Thirty percent of them in The Gambia are Muslims and 12% Christians.

32 The Sexual Offences Act 2013 stipulates the punishments of offenders and construction of a shelter for rehabilitation. As of 2015, effective remedial and rehabilitation services were unavailable, mainly because of insufficient resources allocated to institutions mandated to provide such services. As required by Section 7(4) of the Act, the government was obliged to establish shelters for survivors; but its effective functioning had not been realized and thus the government was in violation of Part II Sections 4, 5, 6 and 7 in 2015. It remained difficult to access official data on how often these Acts have been implemented.

33 The amended Children's Act prohibits child marriage and establishes the minimum age for marriage as 18 years. The Criminal Code 1931 stipulates that "defilement" of girls under age 16 is an offence. Customary law allows girls of 13 years to be married off by their parents, especially in rural areas. In 2015, the minimum age for marriage was 18 years; however, child marriages were still reported before the age of 15. Female genital mutilation (FGM) became illegal by the amendment of the Women's Act in 2015, but its prevalence rate was 76% in 2010. The rate varies among ethnic groups; it is estimated that FGM affects between 100% of Mandingas and the Soninkés (Sarakole) women, 90% of Fulu women and 65-70% of Jola women.

34 Human rights is one of the priority areas of the latest national policy on gender 2010–2020 (described in detail under 3.1 Gender Policy below). The policy states that it is "critical that matters relating to human rights are addressed within the framework of the existing legal and political system, which is expressly made applicable by virtue of the constitution and other laws of The Gambia." It identifies the need for a constitutional amendment to ensure equality between men and women. The dignity of the person to both man and woman, prevail over all other constitutional provisions that recognize customary laws and practices, that tend to subjugate and abrogate these fundamental rights that women should enjoy in order to address "to redress these inconsistencies because most of the discrimination against women arises directly from the discriminatory practices perpetuated under the guise of religious and customary law."

growth through poverty eradication." The vision of the policy is that all attain dignified living standards.

The four missions of the policy are to: achieve gender equality and equality at policy, programme and project level; address inequalities across all sectors; achieve sustainable eradication of gender poverty and deliver an acceptable quality of life; eliminate all forms of gender-based discrimination and violence; and empower women in their roles as agents for structural transformation.

The guiding principles are: gender equity and equality; women-in-development and gender-and-development approaches; affirmative action; partnership; cultural pluralism and tolerance.


36 It notes that the Ministry of Women's Affairs (MWA), National Women's Council (NWC) and the Women Bureau (WB) are responsible for specific policy formulation, implementation, coordination, monitoring and evaluation of the gender policy and that each has responsibility for the policy. Coordination rests with each of these institutions in accordance with its mandate. It also notes that the role of the Gender Policy is to enhance the effectiveness of all other policies and programmes of ministerial policies.


37 It sets out a series as education health, sustainable livelihoods development; governance, human rights, poverty reduction and economic empowerment. Under sustainable livelihoods development, the policy states that it focuses on "gender issues in agricultural production to redress the constraints faced by women farmers." The policy notes that women are the primary producers of food and play an important role in smallholder agriculture by constituting over 50% of full-time farmers and accomplishing nearly 70% of all the agricultural work and food production, but that their productivity is limited by lack of access, control and ownership of resources such as land, credit, inputs, extension services, among others. Under sustainable development, the Policy has: nutrionnary security; agricultural production; water resources and supply; and fisheries.


38 The goal of agricultural production under sustainable development of the policy is to "ensure equal access for women and men to land, technology, inputs, credit, training and employment and skills and inputs by 2026," which is supported by following six objectives, aligned with the Agriculture and Natural Resources Policy 2017-2026; but with more nuanced discussion on gender:

1. To promote national efforts for food self-sufficiency;
2. To increase access to credit and extension services for women and men in both matrilinile and patrilineal systems;
3. To encourage increased access by women to agricultural extension services;
4. To improve the availability, quality, access and control of credit by disadvantaged farmers particularly women;
5. To lobby for increased accessibility to processing and marketing of agricultural produce; and
6. To advocate for the generation of appropriate and affordable gender sensitive technologies.


Fisheries under sustainable development does not have a goal, but its objective is "to encourage the participation of indigenes, men and women in fish farming, processing and marketing." With respect to the priority area, poverty reduction and economic empowerment, the goal is "[t]o achieve national poverty level by 30% and ensure equitable distribution of national resources between male and female by 2026."

The Policy discusses the women's development approach as "a 'mainstreaming' development approach so as to improve their condition and bridge gender gaps." It describes the gender and development approach as a strategy to "support women and men in their roles in development through advocating for structural transformation in reproductive and productive spheres."

39 The Mid-Term Review of the National Gender Policy 2010-2020 conducted in 2017 found that among the 11 strategies under the sub-theme “Agriculture, Business and Industry,” by far an enabling environment for the elimination of gender disparities in access to training, credits, appropriate ICT technologies, value adding, labor saving devices, and markets for the expansion of agricultural production was the most challenging. Only two out of eleven strategies were partially implemented or fully implemented. The sub-theme of Fisheries aimed “to enhance the participation of indigenous, men, women and youths in fish farming, processing, marketing and post harvesting techniques,” and all three strategies under this sub-theme were partially implemented. Other challenges identified included: the sub-themes; “gender insensitivity” at institutional and policy levels; lack of equipment for aquaculture among women; and dependence on donor funds for gender related activities, among others.

41 Following the African Union’s African Youth Charter, The Gambia defines youth or young people as every person between 15-30 years old. The government recognizes that there is a large number of people relevant to this age group which deserve special attention: potential youths (persons of ages 0–14); students in secondary, tertiary and higher educational institutions; out-of-school youth; unemployed and underemployed youth; young women who are married or have children; young people involved in social crimes and delinquency; commercial sex workers; and fully employed youth. Under female youth, the government notes that young women who are married or have children, as well as early marriages and only marriages and teenage pregnancies recorded across the country is also an indication that young women require specific support measures in this regard.” Under rural youth, it notes that the youth in the urban areas or those living in peri-urban areas are more likely to migrate to urban areas and adds that the agriculture sector plays an important role in absorbing young rural workers.

42 The Policy’s main guiding principles and core values are: needs of potential youth (0–14 years) formation; youth as the most important and valuable resource of the nation; national commitment to mainstreaming gender equality into all policies, programs and projects; the need to prioritize the youth in economic and development planning; youth-driven and -centred programmes for youth; national commitment to youth participation in decision-making processes. The Policy goal is “to establish a holistic, inclusive, harmonious and cohesive cohort involved with adequate education, training, strong professional ethics, spiritual and moral values; and a level of independence, patriotism and commitment that gives them the options to choose development and progress in alignment with the national vision.”

48 The concern seems to remain today: an observer remarked that the fears of political-power monopolization by certain ethnicities are disproportional to organized attempts to do so and that accusations of ethnic favoritism are often features of inter-ethnic dynamics. Similarly, observations on the relative numbers of tribes have become contentious, as there exists no absolute majority tribe covering the entire country.

### 3.1 Religion in Legal Sphere

49 As discussed at length, the Constitution of The Gambia 1997 declares that women and men have equal rights. It also incorporates the customary law and shariah, which are not necessarily compatible with the provision of equal rights. Religions do not rule every aspect of the lives of the Gambians, but are the corncernes with respect to women's reproductive health, marriage and inheritance, which are reinforced by the customary laws. In fact, in many respects, one of its principal areas: human rights, last Gambia Gender National Policy2018 lists a strategy to "foster the review of all harmful customary practices perpetuated under the disguise of religion."

### 3.2 Fishery, Crop Agriculture, Livestock and Climate Change Policies on Women and Youth

#### 3.2.1 Gender and Policy on Agriculture and Natural Resources

50 The Agricultural and Natural Resources (ANR) Policy 2017-2028: Of The Gambia2018 is focused about the increasing share of agriculture and natural resources in national income, and exports while the sector remains of great importance to the economy. All products from this sector have been overwhelmingly reliant on importation, except fruits, vegetables, onions, milk and fish. Given the disintegration of the Association of Small Scale Farmers in The Gambia (ASSFAG), which joined the Gambian Women Farmers' Group (GAMWAF), the horticulture subsector, the Policy deems it indispensable to support the involvement of the private entities and women if the country wishes to revitalize the subsector.

51 As for the factors contributing to the economic decline of the ANR sector, the Policy lists: climate change; declining primary commodity prices; and monopolistic domestic policy on poverty and food security. The Policy also notes that the horticulture subsector is in a state of stagnation, which promotes private sector participation, diversification of the production base, domestic savings, healthy environment, sustainable land use, efficient resource management process, and at the same time, discourages rural-urban migration and natural resources degradation.

52 Ten constraints related to the system and institutions are identified in the Policy: technical departments; farmer organization; extension; agricultural research; rural finance and input/output marketing; agricultural mechanization; land and water resources; gender and youth; household food security

security; and the environment. Under the constraints related to gender and youth, the Policy states that their contribution to agriculture and household welfare is limited by: lack of property rights, small-scale of operation, use of traditional production technologies; low access to productive inputs including capital and improved inputs; lack of access to technical information and knowledge; and inadequate distribution systems that discriminates against women and leads to degradation of the environment.

53 The Policy recognizes in its rationale that the majority in the sector are smallholders and hence should aim for improvement of their efficiency and surplus generation, in particular among women farmers and operators. It also includes narrowing the gender gap in capacity. Based on the above rationale, the National Policy contains five main strategies: (i) increasing access to resources; (ii) enhancing participation and representation of substantiated farmers/operators especially women and youths in modern and commercialized agricultural enterprises and trade.

54 One of the core policies of the Policy is the promotion of the private sector – including expanding market outlets for small-scale producers especially women farmers. The Policy also contains system sub-sector/ enterprise policies: macroeconomic policy; food and cash crop policy; seed production system; gender and youth mainstreaming policy; livestock and range management sub-sector policy; forestation policy; education system; ANR institutions/services policy; forestry policy; parks and wildlife policy; marketing policy; food security policy; horticulture sub-sector policy; fisheries sub-sector policy; agricultural mechanization policy; credit policy; and extension services policy.

55 Under food and cash crop policy, women farmers are referred to under sweet potato cultivation that they are able to produce two crops a year by taking advantage of seasonal moisture of the soil as well as water from shallow wells. Other parts of food and cash crop policy does not make reference to women or youth.

56 With respect to the gender and youth mainstreaming policy, the Policy proposes six threads which it considers are in line with the country’s commitment under the Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW): land tenure; access to credit; appropriate technologies for female farmers; women’s participation in decision-making processes; and women’s representation. The gender and youth mainstreaming policy pays attention to women in the fishing industry by proposing support in their bargaining and purchasing fish for processing with their own capital and notes that this measure is also aimed at improving their income.

57 Another thread entitled training touches upon training on formation of self-managed (dafa) groups to negotiate prices in the markets. The thread also points out the necessity of awareness raising on food and nutritional security as well as training in food preservation and processing to ensure food and nutritional security for women. The third thread – marketing - also emphasizes the importance of women’s forming groups for price negotiation.

58 Under livestock and range management sub-sector policy, women are mentioned in relation with small ruminants and poultry, which are considered “women’s animals.” While women are also involved in feeding and managing these animals, they are not mentioned as such. In relation to other animals, donkeys, pigs, ducks, turkeys, guinea fowls and rabbits, they are not mentioned in relation with them in the Policy. Topics such as food production system, ANR institutions/services policy, forestry policy, parks and wildlife policy, marketing policy, credit policy and extension services policy are also gender neutral.

59 The Horticulture sub-sector policy included in the AAN Policy proposes establishment of a National Horticultural Development Authority whose main objective is to establish organized production and marketing system for horticultural products mainly produced by small growers, especially women. While it is a sub-sector highly dominated by female producers, they are not mentioned under all production systems policy, agro-ecology, gender in agriculture or Foreiculture. Similarly, fisheries sub-sector policy, agricultural mechanization policy, environmental management policy on land and water use policy do not make any explicit reference to women.

3.2.2 Youth and Policy on Agriculture and Natural Resources

60 As mentioned in the analysis of women in Agriculture and Natural Resources Policy, the youth are together with women under institutional support services and system-wide constraints, one of the objectives is to provide access to land (land tenure security, land use rights, appropriate technologies and training). Although information specific to the youth is not found in these sections, the second of the six modules of the AAN Policy refers to aging farm labor "due to exodus of able-bodied rural youths" and the youth's "preference for non-farm jobs."

3.2.3 Gender and Climate Change

61 The National Climate Change Policy of The Gambia 2018(2) provides directions to implement national development strategies in a climate-resilient manner through institutional arrangements for coordination and mainstreaming: an integrated approach to resource mobilization and human resources development, and the integration of gender perspectives into all policy areas. It also outlines strategies that mainstream climate change, disaster risk reduction, gender and environmental management, for sustainable social, political and economic development."

62 The Policy is based on the following principles: equity and social inclusion; inter-generational equity; opportunity for women and youth; addressing polluter guys; sustainable development; environmental justice; informed participation; evidence-based; innovation; and do not ruin a decent environment. One of them – environmental justice – is "on addressing social inequalities, particularly regarding gender, age, ethnicity, and socioeconomic status, which would be aggravated by climate change, and enabling access to justice for all."

63 The four focal areas of the Policy are:

1. Climate resilient food systems and landscapes: Agriculture; food security; forestry and natural resources management; water resource management.
2. Low emissions and resilient economy: Energy; transport, infrastructure, and the key economic sectors; industrial development.
3. Climate resilient people: Health; education; equitable social development; and human settlements;
4. Managing coastlines in a changing environment: climate-aware Integrated Coastal Zone Management.

(2) Department of Water Resources, Ministry of Environment, Climate Change, Water, Forestry and Wildlife 2018. "The National Climate Change Policy of The Gambia." <http://thepeoipn.gov.gm/act/gambia/act/national-climate-change-council-inaugurated> (accessed March 2015).

**Page 171**

The Policy objectives are:

1. Advancing the understanding, capacity and social empowerment of all Gambians so that they can respond quickly to climate change;
2. Ensuring adequate climate change research for informed decision making, and promote timely access to climate information and early warning of climate risks;
3. Putting in place a comprehensive set of national adaptation measures that promote effective management of ecosystems and biodiversity, reduce vulnerability to climate change effects, and mitigate greenhouse gas emissions, to achieve sustainable low carbon socioeconomic development;
4. Effectively integrate climate change into all sectors and across all scales, through mainstreaming, cross-sectoral coordination, intersectoral and sectoral frameworks, and through effective policy coordination and implementation;
5. Build the resilience of communities and ensure health and welfare through participatory planning and decision-making processes that emphasize the meaningful inclusion of women and vulnerable groups;
6. Integrate community-based adaptation with ecosystem-based approaches to strengthen livelihoods and food security; develop more climate-resilient livelihoods, by investing in sustainable natural resource management initiatives;
7. Coordinate national and international efforts for resource mobilization to address climate change by mainstreaming climate finance into development and promoting innovative financing mechanisms.

For establishing climate resilient food systems and landscapes (first focal area), the Policy proposes: participatory integrated watershed management; adaptation strategies in agriculture, forestry, fishery, and livestock sectors; sustainable land use practices; rehabilitation and restoration of the River Gambia watershed. The adaptation strategies in agriculture include: selection and promotion of drought-tolerant crop varieties; diversification of crops to reduce stress such as droughts, pests, diseases and soil toxicities; product diversification; and sustainable use of irrigation water. Forest sector strategies include: fuel-efficient cook stoves; and afforestation and reforestation (e.g., restoration of degraded lands).

The strategies of fishery sector are: sustainable fishery management (e.g., use of appropriate fishing methods); capacity development of the Fisheries Department; awareness raising among the public; improved access to microfinance for artisanal operators, including young women processors; and so on. The first focal area also includes adaptation measures in livestock sector such as: building a flow regulation system and water-use regulations. Other strategies include: adoption to diminishing groundwater recharge through cross-sectorial planning; and development of small-scale rainwater harvesting systems.

Besides the references above to gender under the principle of environmental justice and to young female processors in connection with climate change adaptation, women or gender are explicitly or implicitly mentioned with respect to National Climate Change Council (NCCC) and Inter-ministerial Climate Committee (IMCC), whose members include the Minister on gender; and to the National Climate Committee (NCC), which is composed of representatives of farmers, women, scientific community.

children’s and youth groups, communities, workers and trade unions, business and industry, non-governmental organizations, and local authorities.10

68 The Policy also proposes collaborative implementation with the civil society and the private sector. In this regard, it suggests the role of non-governmental entities as the ones to lead in tracking climate change expenditures and outcomes, with a strong focus on gender equality and informed inclusion of women.” The Policy states that the indicators and outcomes will be disaggregated by age and gender.” This is intended to provide learning and to guide responses towards assisting the poorest and most vulnerable people and groups.12

3.2.4 Youth and Climate Change Policy

69 According to the National Climate Change Policy of The Gambia 2016,18 the youth are represented at the national level on the topic of climate change; the Minister on youth issues is a member of the National Climate Change Council (NCC) and Inter-ministerial Climate Committee (IMCC), and youth is one of the four thematic areas under the NCC.19 The Policy states that “collaborative implementation with the civil society and the private sector that the youth represent” is a vital important sector in climate change policy making, and that “the youth should be engaged as key actors in climate change responses should be ensured.” It adds that “[youth] entrepreneurs and leaders should be engaged in all policy development and review initiatives, as well as in capacity development, monitoring and tracking progress of the implementation of policies and actions, especially through the identifying them as a vulnerable group.

3.2.5 Mechanism for Gender Mainstreaming

70 The National Gender Policy 2010-2020 defines the National Gender Machinery as composed by the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and Gender, the National Women’s Council and the Women’s Bureau,10 which are responsible for the implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the policy. It also states that individual stakeholders shall be responsible for the implementation of components relevant to its institutional mandate, while noting that the Policy does not replace institutional policies but enhances their implementation process and ensures their gender sensitivity.

71 Under the Policy, each stakeholder organization must nominate a gender focal point and establish gender units. The network composed of gender focal points is responsible for: advising the Ministry and institutions on gender mainstreaming; providing technical support to the Ministry and institutions in identification; identifying priority gender issues; planning for relevant interventions; and assessing and reviewing

The Council and Committee are national level bodies for climate change policy implementation. The National Women’s Council (NWC) is a consultative body that is mandated to work with sectors and institutions on integrating climate change into the national and sub-national plans, and coordinating gender mainstreaming of climate change policies and programs. The Women’s Bureau works closely with the Ministry of Planning, Policy and Development and the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs.

The major ones are those of the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Gender which is tasked to oversee coordination and harmonization of gender equality policies and strategies across all government institutions. The ministry also coordinates at all levels, lobby, advocate and mobilize resources for Policy implementation; and provide support to gender units and focal points. These include the National Council on Women (NCW) which is a consultative body that provides advice to the Government, local government bodies, CSOs and the private sector, coordinate, monitor and evaluate Policy implementation; provide backstop services to gender units and focal points in all government institutions; and ensure that the NCW's work is consistent with the National Women's Goals. The National Council on Women's Goals and responsibilities are to: advise the government on gender; oversee Policy implementation at the departmental level, including monitoring and evaluation of progress made by each institution; and lobby and advocate on gender in government laws.

The other institutions that they are to translate the Policy into institutional specific policies, strategies and programs; support gender units and focal points by building their capacity in gender analysis, planning and providing budget lines for the implementation of gender equality policies and strategies. These include the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Gender equity, institute and implement affirmative actions on a short-term basis to reduce gender gaps; partner with the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Gender to ensure that all government institutions have gender units or focal points in place; and ensure that institution policies and programmes are gender sensitive and benefit women and men. The NGOs, CSOs and CBOs also can be involved in the implementation of the Policy by providing technical assistance and support for institutionalizing programmes addressing key areas of the Policy, etc.

The Terms of Reference (ToR) of the Gender Machinery is in terms of umbrella, under the jurisdiction of the Department of Parks and Wildlife. There are many substance activities taking place at the Complex, one of which is youth harvesting.

75 Fishery specific issues that affected women's lives were: illegal fishing by industrial fleets (Department of Fisheries) and lack of mangrove restoration at the Tana Wetland Complex (Department of Parks and Wildlife). Other more general obstacles observed were lack of bargaining power and marketing/literacy/numeracy skills (Ministry of Agriculture), access to funds (Departments of Parks and Wildlife, Ministry of Agriculture), and lack of information on fish prices. The issue of land-based domestic violence was also raised as a major issue by the Women’s Bureau. Unbalanced work responsibilities were generally reported by women in the Department of Agriculture. The Department of Fisheries was of the opinion that penury of fresh water in the rural areas was a major issue. Unsustainability of projects were attributed to not integrating local knowledge in design and implementation. The Ministry of Water and Resources, on its part, did not consider fishing as an economic activity. The Women’s Bureau also noted that the lack of involvement of Fishing Site Managers who belong to the Ministry of Fishery and Water Resources, on the other hand, did not consider women as landing site users and could not provide information on the conditions of fish processors who are physically involved in the process.

76 One each of national and international non-governmental organizations (NGOs) was interviewed with respect to their views and activities on gender and youth. Agency for the Development of Women and children; and Action Aid International. They were well aware of CEDAW in addition to the country’s National Policy on Gender Equality. However, they did not have a clear understanding of its impact. The organizations asserted their commitment to strengthening the capacity of women and youth, but the collaboration mechanism in place under the Policy was not very clear to them.

77 When asked about the challenges that women and the youth face, the community leaders interviewed (Banjari, Tando Biring, Jaffuda, Muthoni Kangeti and Karang) did not refer to separate issues for both, except in terms of material needs, which matched well with what each group expressed themselves. [12] They asserted that women and the youth are important participants in decision-making, voting is encouraged, but not authorized to make final decisions.

3.2.6 Marketing for Youth Mainstreaming

78 The National Youth Policy of The Gambia 2019-2024[12] identified the lack of mechanisms for mainstreaming youth development and empowerment issues in sector policies and strategies as one of the obstacles in attaining the objectives of the previous Youth Policy. In recognition of this hurdle, the current policy has set aside a ten percent allocation to ensure that there is adequate funding available quality youth, mainstreaming their empowerment in national development through formal and informal formation, and imbuing them with a set of core fundamental values and beliefs that determine how attitude towards life and work will be shaped.

79 One of the ten priority areas of the Policy is to advocate for mainstreaming youth in economic planning and programming as prerequisites to growth and employment creation. ### 4. GENDER AND YOUTH IN RURAL GAMBIA
#### 4.1 Basic Statistics

82 Gambia was categorized as one of the “Low Development” countries in 2019 - a group consisting
of 33 countries and territories among 189 worldwide – according to the United Nations Development
Programme's Human Development Index (HDI), a summary measure for assessing long-term progress in
a long list of social and economic indicators. According to the UNDP’s Human Development Report, 2019,
the value of the index steadily improved, supported by increases in life expectancy and education attained
while the Gross National Income per capita stagnated.* In terms of overall ranking only a slight improvement was noted from 165th place in 2018 to 164th in 2019. The Gambia is ranked among countries who are accounted for inequality, the value is lowered by 32.5%, slightly over the average loss due to inequality
among the countries and that among Sub-Saharan African countries, which are 31.4% and 30.3%, respectively.* 119


83 The Gender Development Index of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) – which measures gender inequalities in health, education and command over economic resources – puts
the country in Group 5, a group of countries with the least gender equality.* Table 3 demonstrates the
