The Gender Inequality Index reflects gender-based inequalities in reproductive health, empowerment and economic activity, which can be interpreted as the loss in human development due to inequality between female and male achievements in these three dimensions. The index statistics—597.0 deaths from maternal mortality per 100,000 live births; 82.2 adolescent birth rate per 1,000 women aged 15–19; 10.3% of parliamentary seats held by women; 31.5% of adult women having attained secondary level of education or more compared to 44% of the same among adult men; labor market participation rate of 51.2% for women and 68.0% for men—placed the country the 148th among 182 countries in 2019 (Table 4).

# 4.1.2 Population, Ethnic Groups and Languages

## General and Female Population: The population in The Gambia has been steadily increasing since 1960 to reach over 1.83 million in 2019. In 2019, 50.4% were female. The annual growth rate peaked at 2.7% in 1970 and then declined to a high of 2.0% in the early 1960s and the world average in 2019 (2.0% and 1.3%, respectively), which will lead to doubling of the population in slightly less than 25 years if the rate is maintained. The population density is one of the highest in Africa with nearly 180 persons per square kilometer. The urbanization process has been rapid, especially since independence. The cities that are the growing seats of local administration, began in the early 2000s, and close to 60% of the total population today is estimated to live in the urban or peri-urban areas. The population of seven districts which contain the target area are shown in Table 5.

### Table 5: Population in Target Districts (2019)

Source: Gambia Bureau of Statistics, 2013. Population and Housing Census: Youth Report. Banjul, Republic of Gambia.

## Youth: The Gambia today defines youth or young people as every person between the ages of 15 and 35 years, following the approach used in Africa’s Charter, but at the time of 2013 Population and Housing Census, the youth was defined as persons aged 13–34. The analysis in this subsection is based on the former definition of 13–34. According to the 2013 Census, the sources of data inaccuracy include: age misreporting, undercounting, and overcounting.

Between the two Censuses in 2003 and 2013, the youth population increased by 41.8% or nearly to 690,836 persons, which is slightly faster than the national annual population growth rate of 3.1% during the same period. In 2013, the youth comprised roughly 55% of the total population. The latest Census data show that in 2013, about 47.8% of the total population were aged between 15 and 29 years; 37.6% were aged between 13 and 14 years; 31.0% between 15 and 19 years; 26.6% between 20 and 24 years, etc.


---
**Page 178**
---

23.1% between 25 and 29 years and 7.3% aged 30 years. At the time of the last Census, 63.0% of the male population lived in the urban areas while 48.7% of the female did (reflecting the higher tendency among males to migrate than females). The youth in the Gambia is also more mobile and less settled compared with other countries within the Local Government Areas (LGAs). The 2013 Census characterized the youth in Gambia as the most mobile age group among the population, which migrates for various reasons, such as employment, education, family formation, or reclassification.

80 Ethnic Groups: The Gambian River basin has been a focal point for immigrants fleeing the western Sudanic wars dating from the 12th century.137 The people with the longest history of the residence are the Jola, who have lived in the Gambia since the 9th century. They are more present in Banjul and as in Senegal.138 The Fula in the upriver areas established a kingdom and had major power during the 19th century.139 The upstream also hosts concentration of Soninke. The 2013 Census distinguishes ten categories: Mandinka/Jahanka, Fulbe/Tukulor/Lorobo, Wolof (Wolof), Jola/Soninké, Sarakole, Serer; Creole/Aku, Manjago, Bambara, and other.140 At the table below indicates, the ethnic composition has lately been in a flux.

### 4.1 Literacy and Education

#### 91. Literacy: The literacy rates according to gender and age indicate that The Gambia is catching up with other countries in Sub-Saharan Africa. In 2015, for the person-age group of 15 years and above by gender for adult total literacy rate was 36%, but it was 48% for male literacy rate in the same year. The percentage-point difference for female youth literacy between Gambia and Sub-Saharan African average was smaller than that for male youth literacy, while the situation is reversed for adult literacy, implying that women are catching up with men.

#### 92. Education: The Right to Education Section of the Constitution states that the basic education shall be free, compulsory and available to all.[17] But slightly less than one-third of the youth had never attended school in 2013.[18] Another one-third was attending school and slightly over one-third had attended in the past at the time of 2011 Census.[19] During 2015–2017, the enrollment rate increased progressively for all education levels. The net primary school enrollment rate for children aged six to eleven years old in The Gambia 2017 indicated that 16.7% and 24.2% of girls and boys aged 7-12, respectively, were out of school.[20] Most children who are out of school are from the poorest households or those living in remote areas where education beyond primary school or never attend school.[20] Around 2005, education was deemed by the great majority of poor-to-19-year-olds as the single most important factor in progressing to a “good career,” but the 20% richest adults were more likely than those from poorer households to say that education would have done little to advance their employment or earning opportunities and that what mattered most was social mobility.[21]

93 In 2019, 82.9% of lower basic education schools had access to safe drinking water; 79.4% of all schools (including those in urban areas and rural/urban mixed schools) had adequately separated toilets, and 94.2% had permanent classrooms.[105] Improved safety and hygiene conditions in schools are ensuring that more girls are enrolled and retained in school.[106] In the same year, 60% of senior secondary students were in government schools;[107] this is a significant increase from 48.2% in 2015. The pupil-teacher ratio improved between 2015 and 2019 for upper basic education level from 23 to 18.[108] During the same period, the ratios remained at the same moderately high to low levels for lower basic education and senior secondary education.

In urban areas and towns, where girls lost by two percentage points.[113] The situation was at worst among rural areas and girls: 5% for rural girls.[114] The relationship of children's skills to mothers' education level and to wealth appeared to be positive in all cases.

### 96 According to the 2013 Census, 16% of the youth had attained tertiary education; 32% lower secondary education; and 52% upper secondary education. Only 4% had vocational education and 3% reached tertiary level, painting a problematic picture in terms of their future employability and employment.19 Students were most likely to leave school when they are between 15 and 19 years old.17 In the rural areas, 60% of students drop out before completing secondary education; while only 38% do so in urban areas where still in school, against 61% in rural area.17 Formal education was limited outside cities, and for higher quality educational training, the students needed to migrate to the Greater Banjul Area or other major cities. The school attendance rate did not increase as employment increased, which indicates that the students become inactive after dropping out of school.19

### 97 The West African Senior School Certificate Examination (WASSCE) is for senior secondary school students from the five Anglophone countries in West Africa: The Gambia, Nigeria, Ghana, Sierra Leone and Liberia. In 2018, 354,600 candidates wrote WASSCE; of these, 97% were admitted to universities and other higher institution of learning.19 In 2018, 12,121 candidates (54.6% female) registered for the Gambia Senior School Certificate Examination (GSSCE). The pass rate was 37%, with five subjects or more, including English and mathematics, while the rate for male students was 4.6%.19 Among all students, 3.7% obtained credit passes in nine subjects and 17.6% in one subject; and the overall performance of female students was better than that of their male counterparts.

### 98 The statistics on education paints a picture that women are slightly ahead of men in The Gambia as is the case with literacy. In 2015, the gross enrolment rate for the low basic education attained gender parity of 1.08.100 The expected years of schooling (the number of years of schooling that a child of school entrance age can expect to complete) for girls in The Gambia is already higher than for boys. The expected years of schooling for female children is 3.13 years longer than the male counterpart, while the expected years of schooling for male children is only two years; whereas the mean years of schooling is only 0.6 years shorter than the average of the same countries, while for male the mean years are 1.4 years shorter. However, the actual mean years of schooling is shorter for girls than for boys (Table

### 4.1.4 Poverty

101 Gambian economy is an overwhelmingly agricultural one, with groundnuts as the most important cash crop. Tobacco and remittances from Gambians living abroad are major foreign exchange earners and contributed to about 35% of Gross Domestic Product (GDP) in the early 2010s. In 2019, GDP per capita in The Gambia was USD 788, less than half of the Sub-Saharan average, even when we exclude the high-income countries in the region. Purchasing Power Parity GDP per capita had more or less continued to increase from 1990 to 2010, but then stagnated and started to decline after 2010. The country has experienced a “lost decade” over the past century[38] and the decline continues until 2014.[39] It was yet to recover to the 2010 level in 2019.[40] Poverty is higher in the rural than in the urban areas.

102 UNDP identified multidimensional poverty, suffered by individuals in three dimensions: health; education and standard of living. If the score for equal or greater to 80%, but less than 33.3%, the household and its members are vulnerable to multi-dimensional poverty, if the deprivation score is 33.3% or greater, they live in multidimensional poverty. In 2019, 45.6% of the population was vulnerable to poverty; 10.4% of them live in severe multidimensional poverty.[41] In 2018,[42] 37.6% of the population were multidimensionally poor and an additional 22.9% were vulnerable to multidimensional poverty; an average Gambian’s deprivation score was 9.9%, significantly in rural multipoverty.

103 The level of poverty in The Gambia increased between 1992 and 2005 from 41% to 58%[43] and has stayed at that level in the last five years.[44] During 1993-2003, the disparity between the rural and urban areas also widened.[45] The Gini index was 35.9 in 2015, but historical data does not exist.[46] The latest data on inequality is from 2018[47] and shows that the Gini index has increased to 37.6%.[48] The COVID-19 pandemic is expected to have dampened GDP growth in The Gambia between 2.5% and negative 2.4%,[49] and deepened the inequality gap in 2020. The economic impacts of the pandemic have stemmed from fewer tourists, trade disruption and lower commodity prices.[50]

### 4.1.5 Employment

104 Women versus Men: Since the 1990s, the unemployment rate for Gambian women stayed slightly above 30%, while that for women in Sub-Saharan Africa also remained nearly constant around 6%;[51]


---
**Page 184**
---

rates were 12.0% and 6.4%, respectively in 2019. The unemployment rates have evolved differently for males, females and youth from about 7.3% in 2008 (6.5% female) in the Gambia and from 0.4% to 1.6% during the same period for Sub-Saharan Africans as a whole. The latter has had rapid gains in employment, only to lose them between 2002 and 2008; its unemployment rate has been climbing since 2012, largely due to the decline of the informal sector. In contrast, the Gambia’s unemployment rate and that for the Sub-Saharan average moved in parallel with each other and in line with the respective male unemployment rates; the male unemployment rates showed larger swings and stayed approximately 3 percentage points higher than those for females.

100. The comparative history of the unemployment rates implies that female employment in Sub-Saharan Africa is insulated from the world economy: its informal nature and strong connection to local economies persists. It is believed that 70% of Gambian women are engaged in low productivity rural subsistence agriculture, while the engagement of Gambian males has grown in formal economies, albeit to a lesser extent. Overall, the female in The Gambia is more economically active than the Sub-Saharan average.

106. Youth: The picture changes when the focus is narrowed to the youth. Trajectories similar to the total unemployment rates were observed for female unemployment among youths aged 15–24 in the Gambia alone and also in Sub-Saharan Africa, which indicates greater integration of the youth in the economies outside the borders. The rate at 35.7% in 2019 was much higher than 12.0% for the female total in the Sub-Saharan average.

107 For male youths aged 25–34, the Gambians fared better than the Sub-Saharan average by 2-3 percentage points throughout the period 1990-2019: the unemployment rate in 2019 was 8.9% and 12.2%, respectively for The Gambia and Sub-Saharan average, although the rates were higher than those for females. However, since 2015, the male youth unemployment rate has been falling since 2015, as unemployment for 15-34 year olds is abated in the Gambia and soars in sub-Saharan Africa as a whole. Around 2000, 39.6% and 48.7 % of female and male youths, respectively were economically active.

### Types of Employment: The unemployment rates segmented by gender and education level (Table 9) show that the unemployment rate is higher for women at all education levels, but the gender gap is the largest when the highest education level attained is intermediate. The share of youth not in education, employment or training, segregated by gender, also points to the same situation. The informal sector was described as a major source of employment for young men and women, particularly for family survival.[29] It is the informal sector that provides the majority of work for women.

### 4.1.6 Marriage

**Page 47**

114 **Age:** The 2013 Census showed that well over half of Gambian women, but only slightly over 10% of men, were married before the age of 25. Before turning 30, 89% of Gambian women and 30% of men had been married or had been in a union. Among the age group 24–34, 67% better for the age group 40-44: 97.0% of women and 89.6% of men were married or had been in marriage at some time in their lives before the age of 45.[1] Around 2015, 36% of women aged 20–24 reported that they were married before the age of 18;[2] this is a significant increase from 2009 when only 17.5% of women in this age group had been married at some point.[3] The proportion of early marriage among Gambia women was one of the highest rate of child marriage in the world.[4] According to the Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey 2018, 65% of women aged 20–24 were married before their 18th birthday compared with only 37.9% for male counterparts.[5] In 2018, more than half of the women aged 15-19 and married had a spouse who was older by ten years or more, and the proportion was higher for women aged 15–19 than for those aged 20–
**4.1.6 Marriage**

115 In a study that targeted urban Muslim poor in The Gambia and Tanzania, it was concluded that education was an important factor in delaying marriage but that the perception of both parents and the girls themselves (that the drawbacks outweighed benefits) and the hidden costs of schooling (extra fees, transportation to school, etc.) were major obstacles. In addition, early marriage is a common practice in urban Gambia and Tanzania; three contributing factors to early marriage which are intertwined: poverty, cultural norms, and lack of education. The perception that early marriage is considered by the parents and the girls as the best available option for girls from the urban poor, and more than often there are no meaningful alternatives to marriage including work opportunities in the rural areas. Girls who do not attend school are more likely to be married at a young age compared with those whose girls do well in school.[6] Girls who have no access to education or have lost the motivation to attend school often engage in intimate relationships, either to obtain financial support from boyfriends or as a way of avoiding marriage. The lack of knowledge about sexual and reproductive health care is also an important element in early marriage among girls,[7] and girls are becoming increasingly more sexually active, at least in the urban areas.[8] The prevalence of child marriage differed

### Types of Union

The 2013 Census indicated that 64.6% of the ever-married population aged 12 years and older were in monogamous marriages, while 35.3% were in polygynous (more precisely, polygynous) unions. The Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey 2018 reported that 36.2% of women aged 15–49 were in polygynous unions, implying increase in the practice from 2013 to 2018. In 2013, polygynous unions were more common among rural women (47.8%) than urban women (36.2%), while in 2018 these figures were 59.7% and 42.7%, respectively), which the Census explained as a fact of polygyny (more precisely, polygynous), when a man takes on three wives for example; three women would be reported as being in polygynous unions.

Among the persons in polygynous unions in 2013, the most common occupation was agriculture for both men and women. In 2018, the proportion of rural women who were in a union of polygyny in the rural areas rose to 43.6% and that of urban women aged 15–49 and rural and urban areas respectively, were in polygynous marriages/unions. The most common level of educational attainment was primary education for both men and women who were in monogamous relationships and those who were in polygynous relationships. The most common education level was none at age 75 among persons in polygynous unions compared to 60% of those in monogamous unions. In the same year, 28.9% of women aged 14–49 years and 30.3% of men were in a union of polygyny. The Survey in 2018 indicated that women with handicaps were more likely to be in a polygynous relationship: 48.9% and 36.4% for women aged 18–49, and with and without functional limitations respectively. Women who were in a polygynous union were less dependent on types of unions.

A survey conducted in the late 1990s in four Sub-Saharan countries (Ghana, Kenya, Senegal and Zimbabwe) concluded that if someone's education was a contributing factor in monogamous marriages, while it did not affect the likelihood of polygynous marriage, then the wife's education mattered. Comparing the ethnicity of the head of household, Gambian women aged 15–49 were most likely to be in a polygynous marriage when the head was Sarakole (56.5%) and least likely when the head was Jola (30%). The survey also indicated that women who had been married for more than five years showed rates higher than 30%. Women were more likely to be divorced, separated or widowed than men: 6.6% among rural women and 1.0% among rural men, and slightly higher for both groups in the urban areas.

119 The Census commenced that polygamy is a "non-indigenous cultural practice" which "is deeply rooted in African society and is widely practised among Muslims and Christians also in many parts of the continent". It has helped various tribes survive in a rather unadverse and hostile physical environment.203 The rationale for polygamy (more precisely, polygyny) in Africa are that it is a remedy for female infertility, lack of male heirs, or to provide a wife for a widower. Polygyny is also practised in these conditions are forbidden; social exclusion (the social norm is to be married and have children, leading to levirate marriage in some cases); acquisition of labour: lack of male heir, sick, absent or working mothers (who cannot breastfeed their babies).204 In the Muslim world, polygyny is seen as a religious practice. Reasons are seen primarily from men's point of view to overcome inconveniences and fulfill desires.

120 When women initiate divorce, husbands often do not respect court rulings in terms of child support, and in most cases the child left under the custody of the mother without any contribution from the father. In some countries, such as Gambia, there is a reluctance for women to seek legal attention because qadli courts are presided over by men;205 and it is said that there is reluctance to appoint female qadis.206 Under customary law, a wife needs to remarry a member of her husband's family in order to inherit her husband's property.207

## 4.2 Labor Division, Decision Making and Autonomy
### 4.2.1 History

In 2014, between 25,000 and 30,000 people were engaged in artisanal fishing and about 2,000 people were employed in the industrial sub-sector. Another 200,000 persons were indirectly dependent on fisheries and related activities in a country of slightly over 2 million. Of all engaged in the fishery...

**Page 191**

## The fisheries sector in the Gambia

The fisheries sector is a key component of the Gambian economy and culture. It provides livelihoods for many people and contributes significantly to the country's GDP.

### Women's role in fishing

As a rule, women and men produce different products, operate on different scales and serve different markets. In the Gambia, 100% of sardine fisheries are managed by women, while most pelagic fish such as tuna (Sarda sarda and Sardinella maderensis), bonito (Euthynnus alletteratus), horse mackerel (Trachurus trachurus, Trachurus carlsoni and Caranx hippos) serve as a source of protein for men. Women are also responsible for the domestic market, while men handle export-oriented fish such as tuna.

### Women's role in processing

Women play an important role in the processing sector. They process most of the small-scale fish traders who work with women and handle these tasks themselves, but hire women to do the work. Women deal in the domestic market for fresh and cured fish, while men export processed fish such as smoked and dried fish.

### Women's role in marketing

Women are also involved in marketing. They sell their products directly to consumers or through middlemen on a daily basis with low profit margins. Roughly 2000 people worked in the industrial sector in 2017, mainly processing factories and well-known brands such as "Gambia Fish" and "Fishland". These are located close to the fish markets where women market their products.

### Women's role in export

Some of them export unprocessed fish about 80% of which is exported to Europe. Others export processed fish such as smoked and dried fish to neighboring West African countries, including Nigeria. Some artisanal catch of high-value fish species (shrimps, oysters, lobsters and cephalopods) are usually purchased by the industrial fishing companies for processing in factories and exported mainly to the EU.

### Women's role in social inclusion

The fisheries sector in the Gambia trade value addition and social inclusiveness with a focus on women. The report for the European Union DG DEVCO Value Chain Analysis of the Gambia.

[49] Due to illiteracy, they are at a great disadvantage when the business concerns written documents, especially financial matters which men tend to control.[50]

### 130
Women in The Gambia and Senegal devote less time than the average African women to agriculture at 50%, compared with 68% in Cameroon.194 Nonetheless, approximately half of the full-time farmers are estimated to be women, who contribute to about 70% of all crop food production despite their low productivity, which is caused by lack of access, control and ownership of productive resources (e.g., land, credit, inputs). Women also produce a large proportion of vegetables and other crops, such as cassava and corn,195 which are considered women's crops. It is not women alone who take care of all stages of vegetables and rice production. Men prepare the land for both vegetables and rice. They also join women in the food preparation process.


### 131
Men are heavily involved in cultivation of cash crops, such as groundnut, as well as coconuts (pearl millet),196 cassava and corn,197 which are considered men's crops. As is the case with vegetables and rice, men are not exclusively responsible from land preparation to sale; for example, shelling of groundnuts is done mainly by women. In some cases, however, men have more control over the cash crop than women do: they control how much they sell and who they solicit the others' help as necessary.198 In areas where rice production has become possible due to suitable infrastructure, women grow the same crops as men, but independently.199


### 132
The separation of tasks is such that what the knowledge on crops is also gender dependent.200 Women and men from the same compound work independently from one another without sharing labor and other inputs in crop production. This division of labor is not only a reflection of different levels of control exerting concerning what is considered their own production processes, especially for women. In fact, rice used to be a woman's crop, but the adoption of centralized pump irrigation transformed rice into a man's crop.201 The same happened with groundnuts: the introduction of improved varieties was designed and introduced to benefit women.202 It was also noted that women in The Gambia are willing to trade some autonomy in exchange for greater male participation in shelling groundnuts, which eased the post-harvest workload, and reduced competition for women. When vegetable gardens were introduced in The Gambia, there has been a debate over whose crops they should be.[28] It is said to have ended in a compromise with significant power given to women,[29] as witnessed by the gender categorization of vegetables today. While vegetable gardens promoted by development agencies are generally believed to have contributed significantly to economic empowerment and food security, there has been some debate over whether they also foster water supply, fences or proper organization among women for this task.[30] Some men are quite willing to venture into the trade, as they are well equipped plants and the income it generates. ### 1.2 | Livestock

134. According to the latest Agricultural Census of The Gambia 2011(2012) 0.8% of the population aged 10 and above engaged in livestock work as their primary occupation.[32] Over 3% of the same population were engaged in livestock keeping as a secondary occupation.[33] When compared with the previous census, this shows an increase from 0.5% to 0.8%. The livestock sector is dominated by cattle and sheep. Nearly 40% households owned cattle, over 60% goats, over 35% sheep and 40% donkeys; over 10% horses.[34] pigs, over 40 chicken and other poultry in 2011/2012.[35] Many households in Banjul keep pigeons and some kept ducks. The livestock sector is a mixed one with both urban and rural areas, pulling a two-wheel cart to carry people and various goods, in addition to field and farm work.[36] It is common for each household to keep several types of animals.[37]

135 While cattle is considered "men's" animal, well over 10% of cattle was owned by women.[38] Although small ruminants, such as goats and sheep, are "women's" animal, only roughly a quarter to one- third are owned by women. [39]

### 4.2 Household Work

Data pertaining to less than 40% of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) indicators are collected in The Gambia, including unpaid care and domestic work. The focus group discussions with women processors associated with the Banjul landing site revealed that while they spend their time processing fish, they also have other responsibilities such as cooking, cleaning, washing, sewing, and caregiving, with the processors/sellers themselves supplementing two hours per day. During the week, women spent an average of eight hours per day; men did not help in household chores or caregiving. The female youth noted that both caring-mothers when the workload became heavy, such as during the oyster seasons in Tamba.

In rural communities, women disproportionately bear the burden for childcare while caregiving, while women spend on average four to five hours a day to cook. For those with no other children or children old enough to take care of younger siblings at home, mothers took children with them to their work location, which was perceived as a difficult situation. Such labor division between women who earn cash income and those who stay at home, usually the young, to look after the house is common in The Gambia.

Gambian girls regularly perform a wide range of activities including cooking, cleaning, washing, sewing and looking after younger siblings; whereas boys are expected to sweep the compound, to run errands, and to help with agricultural work. At school, this gender disparity is in line with the social norm that domestic chores are women's work. One young man in Bintang said he helps his wife with work around the house, such as sweeping and cooking.

### 4.2.5 Supplementary Income

139 An overwhelming majority of households report crop agriculture as primary occupation, and the most prevalent secondary occupation is crop farming. Most families have diverse sources of income; non-farm employment (especially trade) and remittances are important sources besides crop agriculture.

140 In general, children start participating in unpaid work around the house, including family farm plots, at around 7 years of age. As many as half of the children aged 11-12 in 2005 were likely to have engaged in some form of agricultural activity. Children are also involved in other forms of small family businesses—or engagement in own-account informal services and commerce—as running errands or street vending. Children may devote to these activities 1–2 hours before or after school hours in urban areas, but more than 30 hours a week in rural areas, especially among girls aged 9–18, with higher frequency among boys than girls and in the rural than in the urban areas. Among children aged 5–7 years old, there is no significant difference between the number of hours worked by girls and boys. Among those aged 15-17, 0.9% worked 43 hours or more per week; 1.5% among boys and 0.4% among girls. The children's engagement in work roughly decreases with the mothers' education and wealth, but not smoothly, suggesting variety in the nature of work that the children undertake.

141 The children’s eagerness to contribute to household income was said to be not only motivated by poverty, but also by strong sense of obligation to return to their parents what they owe for their existence. This is especially true among those who are more educated and whose parents, the more blessings one accrues. Nowadays their own material well-being plays a factor.

142 In 2018, about a quarter of the children aged 5-7 were involved in child labor; girls slightly more than boys, proportionately more than twice as much rural children as urban children, and proportionally more children living in poor households. The proportion of children working was higher under precarious conditions: boys slightly more than girls, proportionately more than twice as much in the rural than in the urban areas, and proportionately more from poorer households. Overall 13.5% of children worked at or above age 7 years old. The proportion of children working above age-specific threshold decreased with household wealth.

143 The majority of children participate in household chores across age groups, and the gender gap is about 10 percentage points or less for each age group: 5-11; 12-14 and 15-17 years old. Neither the

**144.** Human trafficking, involving both nationals and foreigners, has been a long-standing issue in The Gambia and has not yet shown a clear sign of abatement. As of 2018, no conviction has been made under the Trafficking in Persons Act 2007, and the reported cases of human trafficking and forced labor according to the UN Special Rapporteur on the sale and sexual exploitation of children, Maude de Boer-Buquicchio, are limited. Among mostly women and girls, some are trafficked abroad, while others are subjected to sex trafficking and forced labor in The Gambia. In 2018, 34 cases were reported involving 59 victims. Of these boys involved in a lesser extent, the number of boys exploited in sex trafficking is estimated to be only one or two. According to data found by NGOs in rural border in neighboring West African countries, Gambian migrants attempting to travel to Europe are trafficked and sold into forced labor in Libya. In September 2017 through August and October 2018, an international organization repeated at least 3,500 Gambians from Libya, many of whom were at risk for trafficking. The National Agency against Trafficking in Persons (NAATIP) with the assistance of UNICEF and other partners has been working to strengthen national laws and government officials to build capacity in the prevention and detection of human trafficking. NAATIP also organizes sensitization missions for communities around border posts on the dangers of human trafficking.

**145.** Commercial sexual exploitation is said to take place in tourist destinations, hotels, bars, brothels and nightclubs—and in densely populated and impoverished areas. Some corrupt Madrasa teachers are said to force their male students into begging, street vending, and agricultural work. The UN Special Rapporteur on the sale and sexual exploitation of children, Maude de Boer-Buquicchio, found that homeless and undocumented children are exposed to high risks of trafficking and exploitation. Are the children working in family farms, in the informal sector, in petty trading and in domestic work at home, as well as children who are said to be "orphans" or "of no means" (religious teacher). There is no comprehensive data regarding children on the streets.

### 4.2.6 Decision Making and Autonomy

#### 140. Overall: Men are by default the heads of households, about 84% of the households in The Gambia. According to focus group discussions and interviews with community leaders, women and youth are not involved in decision making at home. Women do not have the same power as men adults. In case the parents are involved in the same work as the youth, the parents speak for all. At home, women make decisions on day-to-day issues and are consulted for bigger family matters such as buying food, clothing, and other household items. According to a survey conducted in 2013, close to 40% of women aged 15-49 participated in making decisions all of: own health care; major household purchases; and visiting family.

#### 141. Income: Women tend to earn income through their own economic activities, and some in the urban areas earn income by hiring a worker to take care of the household work. The husbands give a portion of their incomes to wives, who use the money and their own income to purchase food as well as clothing for themselves and their children. Some women also send their children through school with their income, especially in the urban areas. It is common that the everyday needs of the household are met mainly by the wives’ income with husbands' supplementing as they wish.

#### 148: In contrast to cattle, income from the sale of small ruminants and poultry is mostly earned by women only. According to a 2003 qualitative study on gender and intra-household allocation in The Gambia, about 54% of respondents said that when men and women preferred husbands to receive cash transfers. When a group of women establishes a bank account to keep their funds, it is commonly a man who opens the account for them. According to a survey conducted by the World Bank in 2019, between 75% and 90% of women were the sole decision maker on how to use their savings, loans, and remittances, and most of them did not access formal loan forms. For formal loans, a guarantor is required for women to obtain credit from banks or other financial institutions in The Gambia; however, they tend to shy away from assuming control when it involves official institutions, implying that it is the

**149.** *Assam:* The Constitution of the Gamaa 1997 declares that women and men have equal rights but also incorporates the customary law and shari'ah, which are not necessarily compatible with the provisions of equal rights. It has been observed that the political will is not strong enough to repeal the provisions of Section 33(3) of the 1997 Constitution and the like in other laws, such as the Women's Act, which allows personal property to be transferred to a husband even if applicable to custody of marriage, birth, and devolution of property on death.**40**

**150** Land tenure systems are governed by statutory norms and customary practices, and the latter prevails in the rural areas. Under the customary tenure arrangements, heads of lineages and households have the right to use land for agricultural purposes. The inheritance of land is divided mostly by shari'ah and customary law; Qur'an allocates daughters half the share of their brothers, and customary law excludes women from land inheritance, while for widow inheritance of her husband's property is not allowed under both laws. In a study conducted in 2015, it was observed that discussions indicated that the inheritance issues are discussed by the family but the husbands make the final decisions. The study also found that women were not involved in the decision-making process and some of them did not even know about the signatures of the accounts, partly because of higher prevalence of illiteracy among women.**40** Women own the tools and equipment that they bought for their own trade, commonly except for boats.*40*

**151** Farming: Men are mainly responsible for physically demanding work (e.g., land preparation), and women do most of the other tasks such as planting seeds, weeding, harvesting, and post-harvest processing. Women's participation in farm activities is increasing, but it varies by region. In some areas, women's participation in farm activities has increased significantly over time.**40**

Women are the crops and animals, as well as men's crops and animals to designate which group has the ultimate control of them. The crops and animals that belong to a woman are given more attention than those belonging to her husband. Although vegetables are considered mostly women's crops, men are ready to engage in it thanks to the assistance by the donors and the earning that the products bring.*40*

**152** Health: About two-fifths of women of reproductive age had decision-making power over their own reproduction, while 68% of women aged 15–49 years reported that they were jointly entitled to. Nearly 40% of women aged 15-49 in 2013 participated in making decisions on all: of own health care; major household purchases; and visiting family.*40* Over 40% of the age group of women

## Violence

It was estimated in 2020 that about one in three women in The Gambia experience sexual or physical violence in their lifetime, similar to the global prevalence rate. In 2013, 7.3% of Gambian women aged 15–49 subjected to physical or sexual violence in the last 12 months. Sexual and gender-based violence is a major public health problem in The Gambia, with high rates among young and stateless men.

Women see their ability to provide food, shelter, and clothing for their households as the source of their legitimacy as an authority over their households. The latest pandemic has led to loss of jobs and income, which may increase the vulnerability of women and girls to violence in order to ensure that they continue to exert power and authority over their wives and households.

The incidents are largely perpetrated during marriage, with 54% of cases occurring within the first year of marriage.

### FGM

In The Gambia, 28.7% of girls aged 10–19 have undergone female genital mutilation (FGM). The practice is more prevalent in the urban areas (73.3%) than in the rural areas (71.3%). So was the opinion among women that it should be continued (44.6% of urban women versus 42.5% of rural women).

The prevalence rate for FGM varies by region, with a higher prevalence in urban and rural areas, respectively. Each age group of women were approximately evenly split between for and against FGM. Among the ethnic groups compared (Mandinka, Wolof, Fulah, Jola, Sarakole and other ethnic groups), Mandinka (95.3%) and Sarakole (94.0% women aged 15–49) were most likely to have gone through FGM, while Wolof women of the same age group were least likely.

### Women's Decisions

Women making their own informed decisions regarding sexual relations, contraceptive use and reproductive health care are common in The Gambia. In 2013, 75% of Gambian women aged 15–49 reported that they made their own decisions about these matters.

155. Child marriage remains common: in 2018, 9.5% of women aged 15–49 were married before turning 15, and the rate was 12.9% in the rural areas. By contrast, only 0.3% of men aged 15-49 were married before the age of 15 in the same year. Most marriages are not officially registered and many instances of child marriage go unreported.

In 2015 and 2016, the Women’s Act and Children’s Act were revised to legally prohibit FGM and child marriage. Various organs were also established in the recent years in relation to violence against women: a gender unit in 2014 at the Attorney General's Chambers to train police officers, prosecutors and law enforcement personnel; a Gender Unit in the Ministry of Justice, Legal Affairs, Human Rights, Gender and Social Welfare in 2019; National Human Rights Commission in 2019; community child protection committees; and youth neighborhood watch groups. Effective enforcement of these laws was weak due to lack of resources, including human resource capacity, budgetary allocation, infrastructure, technical and financial and administrative capacity to oversee and respond to reported cases, including child-friendly spaces. The law on the prohibition of FGM is considered a major achievement in the national sex is considered the main reason for FGM and early marriage. Where one of the two sex is common, the other is practiced less, indicating interchangeable nature. The culture favors informal settling of problems and tends to be more tolerant towards child marriage than FGM. Women are often reluctant to report their experiences. As of 2019, no alleged perpetrate of sale, sexual abuse/exploitation or human trafficking was known to have been prosecuted or convicted.

Nearly a quarter of mothers and caretakers of children aged 3–14 believed in 2018 that physical punishment was necessary for children to learn discipline and be properly, and the rate was slightly higher in rural than urban areas. The rate decreased with the mothers’ and caretakers’ education to about 20% and a similar trend was seen for the wealth level.

155 Women think that violence inflicted by the husbands is justified when the wife neglects the children (35.3% in 2013 and 34.6% in 2018); when she burns the food (10.6% in 2013 and 11.8% in 2018).

### 4.2.7 Conflict Resolution

#### Community:
At all community leaders interviewed, it was the land-site user communities or villages emphasized the importance of dialogues in conflict resolution and not escalating the issue to the formal court system. Land-site communities in Banda Kertang and Kartong are important for unity among the members, which is a moral lesson to be bequeathed to the future generation. The leader in Jahlary reported grievance resolution by the Village Development Committee—which is required by the government—was not always successful because of lack of knowledge on how to resolve them. In Medina Lamin Kanteh, a council of elders were in charge of conflict resolution for most cases, including those related to land and water disputes. The women leaders from other communities made judgments on more weighty issues, such as land conflict. The leaders in Jahlary and Kartong reported that both women and youth are consulted in the decision making process.

#### Household:
When asked who in the family is responsible for resolving needs, women tend to answer it is their husbands. However, this is not always true; sometimes, it is the women who spend their income for daily family needs supplemented by whatever proportion of their own income that husbands give to the wives. Women are consulted on decisions regarding assets, inheritance and marriage, but it is the husband who makes final decisions. The leaders in Jahlary reported that women are aware that otherwise men may be discontented and create problems for the marriage and the household. If women are dissatisfied with a donor's project, they are willing to go to the officials themselves to have them resolved as long as it does not affect their own lives.

### 4.3 Access to Resources and Services

#### Land, Crop, Animals, Equipment and Infrastructure:
As discussed above, women do not own or inherit land, but have access to land through their male family members and shoulder a fair share or more of the agricultural work. The land in the rural areas is customarily controlled by the traditional chiefs (akilado) who allocate parcels within the village boundaries. Women are allowed to cultivate only small plots of land for their own use. Most so-called women's crops are mainly non-cash crops, but vegetables are an exception to the rule. Women’s animals are also smaller in size and poorer, which tend to bring in less cash and whose ownership belonged overwhelmingly to men.

Women are often allotted the least attractive units of infrastructure, which tends to receive less attention and investment. Equipment and related infrastructure may be donated explicitly for women, but since the users are not sufficiently consulted, they may turn out to be inconvenient or not properly functioning. The oyster farm in Banda Kertang is an example where the donated equipment was not used because the donated oyster racks are not in use; a distance between the flame and the oysters cannot be adjusted to consume less fuel. The smoking facility constructed by a donor in Bintang does not have

### 4.3.2 Extension Service

164 **Fishery:** The Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources provides extension services in fishery and aquaculture through its Department of Fisheries. In 2010, the Department had 33 extension staff, covering all regions except for Bintang. Some extension workers were trained by the Forestry Department on management and fish handling. The processors/learners in Bintang indicated that an important issue related to training was the lack of practical training. In addition, the extension service in Bintang was halting the overexploitation of small fish by industrial fishermen more important to their livelihoods than training.

165 **Food and Agriculture Sector Development Project (FASDP), a FAO project funded by the Global Agriculture and Food Security Program (GAFSP):** contained technical assistance on aquaculture, but the content was not reported. The farmers involved had ponds created under the project, but the sustainability of these ponds is unknown. The extension workers in Bintang did not seem to have clear ideas on how to proceed without the inputs from the project.

166 **Agriculture:** Agricultural extension workers and services have been widely acknowledged as scarce and inadequate in the country by all stakeholders. The Ministry of Agriculture counted 829 extension officers, which meant one officer per 1,000 farmers, according to a report in 2013. The

activities of the Communication, Extension and Education Services Unit of the Ministry included printing of leaflets, posters, pointers, etc., design and facilitation of training of trainers; planning and coordination of extension services including the organization of field training programmes in regional offices.### The topics covered by the service were: agricultural inputs; soil and water management; food and nutrition management; horticulture; training; and monitoring and evaluation (M&E).167.

The lackluster performance of the extension system was attributed to 2013: inadequate training programmes for extension officers; poor coordination between extension workers/farmer/rice; limited knowledge and skills of some extension workers in sustainable agricultural production; and lack of resources, including financial resources, for the delivery of the services.### Around 2019, the Ministry of Agriculture identified three constraints in the provision of sufficient extension services, and the Department of Community Development of the Ministry of Lands and Regional Government (DCCD) noted that extension workers need to be updated to meet the differential needs of women, the youth, the disabled and so on.### 168.

Around 2020, the Planning Support Unit of the Ministry of Agriculture pointed to the low number and inadequate organization of extension workers as well as lack of transportation means.### One NGO stated that the extension officers were not adequately trained in sustainable agriculture.### Another NGO reported that the extension workers who originate from the area are suited to the job.### An international NGO working in The Gambia has its own extension workers.### A National Extension Services Policy and Strategy was developed by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Food Security (MAFFS) as of February 2022. An Adaptation Fund (AF) project which includes an activity to train extension officers, along with other activities on climate change adaptation was under review by the AF's Project Programme Review Committee in March 2021.### 169.

Historically and around the world, extension services have been geared toward men, and women have constituted a very small fraction of all recipients of extension services, as low as 5%.### Although data and information on the impact of extension services and workers in The Gambia are not readily available, it is known that most of the rural women work in agriculture and livestock farming for male-dominated activities, such as production of cash crops.### It has been noted that extension workers in horticulture (which recently mainly women) and livestock (which consists women, except for cattle raised in the open) objectives are less strong than in other subjects.### Less extension services geared

## The Third World Conference of Ministers and Heads of State of the World Meteorological Organization (WMO)

The WMO launched a process in 2009 to create a Global Framework for Climate Services, and an Intergovernmental Council on Climate Services was established to oversee the implementation of the framework. In 2013, the Gambia joined the initiative as one of its founding members.

### The National Framework for Climate Services (NIFCS-GAM)

The Gambia responded in 2018 to formulate a National Framework for Climate Services (NIFCS-GAM), which is a framework for exchange between experts. In preparing this document, weather and water (in this case, Department of Water Resources) were other experts in the sector concerned, but also with end-users.

### Other Main Institutional Stakeholders

The other main institutional stakeholders include: National Disaster Management Agency, National Environment Agency, Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Energy, Action-Aid The Gambia, Gambia Civil Aviation Authority, Department of Fisheries, and Ministry of Tourism. The overarching goal of the NIFCS-GAM is "[to] enable better management of the risks of climate change in the country by providing a framework for the development of science-based climate information and prediction services into planning, policy and practice."

### The NIFCS-GAM and the Action Plan (2020-2024)

The analysis that "the drivers of rural vulnerability include the absence of capacity to overcome the impacts of climate change, particularly the increasingly shortened growing period. This is due to a number of factors: the increasing proportion of the population made up of young people, the main workforce, towards the urban centres and abroad, enlarging the number of women-headed households, and the aging of the rural population." The Action Plan 2020-2024 would enhance resilience to the shortening of the growing period. It also notes that "[g]ender has not been significantly mainstreamed into the existing ANBI policy's sub-sector policies and strategies though female-headed households are a significant proportion of the total rural population and key actors in rural resilience efforts." It adds that "[j]hhs is a significant departure from the Gender and Women Empowerment Policy 2010-2020 which calls for the effective mainstreaming of gender perspectives into emerging crisis situations, including food security and nutrition. The Action Plan 2020-2024 includes a section devoted to 'Priority Activities to be Undertaken to Reduce Gaps and Meet User Needs.' The NIFCS-GAM identifies as a key challenge the "last mile" of an early warning system, i.e., in reaching the most remote

173 Gender differentiated needs for climate information have been documented in Africa: by content and means of access in Senegal (stronger demand among women for information on the onset of rainy season, while men are more interested in information about the end of the rainy season), and across their larger financial resources allowed them to purchase phones).108 Gender differences are also noted elsewhere. In Ethiopia, 52% of female-headed households received weather-related information compared to 76% for male-headed households.109 In Senegal, 34% of female-headed households did not report and act on early warnings on climate change compared to 38% of male headed households.110 While the perception of the climate change did not seem to differ between men and women in Ethiopia, women were more likely than men to say that they receive, share, understand, and act upon weather-related information as observed in Vietnam.111 While the NFGS-GAM alludes to such differences between women and men, the accompanying Action Plan 2020-2024 does not have reference to the youth.

174 The NFGS-GAM analyses the state of the youth in Gambia, citing the unemployment rates among the youth in 2012, which was 38% against 30% for the overall population.102 It adds that female youths are less likely to be employed or in education: 51% of female youth neither employed or in school against 27% of male youth.103 In addition, the NFGS-GAM notes that young people in Europe and North America, together with low labor productivity, high poverty and fertility rates, are identified as factors contributing to an “ageing population.”104 A project implemented by the United Nations Environment Programme financed by the Global Environment Facility on an early warning system is said to have given hope to the youth and encouraged them to stay in the rural areas. The Action Plan 2020-2024 does not have reference to the youth.

### 4.3 Mobile Access and Communication Technologies

The women in the focus group discussions indicated that when in need of money they resort to pools funded among them (usuus) or borrow from their family and friends.152 The processes in Banjul expressed a lack of capital, consisting mainly of fishing and drying area, into which they put a handful of fish directly from the fishermen, and not from the middlemen. They also noted that they always purchase in smaller quantities but with markups. Collective purchase of fish by the boat used to be practiced in Banjul, but this has been discontinued due to lack of capital. The Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources launched a fund of GMD 10,000,000.00 (whose sources are fees on fishing rights, licenses, etc.) to support women and youth in the sector in January 2021; five individuals, three of whom are women, have received funds as of March 2021.153

According to the 2013 Census on information and communication technology (ICT), nearly 80% of both female and male Gambians had access to a mobile phone (Table 12).154 The proportion of population with access was around 90% for male aged 20-69. For women, no age group marked the 90% access rate, but it was highest among those aged 30–49 years old. The gender gap in mobile phone access is higher than the gender gap in access to other ICTs (Table 13), indicating that more women with access to mobile phones do not use the phones than men with access. The gender gap in access to computers was much higher and most pronounced for the age group of 20–69 years old, while there is no significant difference between genders when it comes to traditional literacy, but also digital literacy (Table 14); the high access rates to mobile phones among women are more likely to be associated with their use in traditional settings than when they have 3G phones or higher, since they use the phones primarily for making calls through WhatsApp. Mobile cellular prescription has increased from 94.2 per 100 persons in 2013 to 139.5 in 2020.155

### 4.3.6 Traditional Sources of Information

180 Access to the traditional media—newspaper, radio and television—is much better represented among both female and male populations in the country except for newspaper, in 2013 **[1]** The most popular medium is radio, reflecting the rates of literacy and poverty; access to journalism is hampered by illiteracy and that to television by economic means to purchase a television set.**[1]** The same pattern persisted among women and men in all age groups. In 2013, newspaper access was 5.9% for both sexes overall seeming decreasing except for men’s access to television. Access is further constrained in the rural areas because of low circulation of newspaper and magazines and unavailability of electricity needed for television sets.**[1]** The most popular medium among women and men aged between 20–34 years, access to newspapers and magazines was 5.9% in the rural areas versus 21.3% in the urban areas, while that for radio was 88.8% in the rural areas versus 86.6% in the urban areas.**[1]**

### 4.3.7 Environmental Awareness: Climate Change and Ecosystems

182 The women who participated in focused group discussions had first-hand experiences in climate change: dwindling number and quantity of fish species in the ocean and river; loss of oyster habitat near the coast; and changes in the vegetation on mangroves and other coastal areas. They heard the topic quite often on the television and radio, but did not know what was causing the changes. The women in Jahlivi were involved in reforestation (mangroves on the coast and other trees inland) as climate change is a major concern for them. However, they are not aware of any training or support being given to aquaculture if the fish stocks look on keeping shrunk due to climate change and if a development partner wants to introduce the practice.

183 On the one hand, women in fishery know that mangroves are important for the lives of fish and oysters and are vital for their livelihoods. They knew that their grandmothers would not have as much mangroves as their grandparents did. On the other hand, many appeared to continue harvesting the mangroves for firewood or charcoal, despite a fine imposed by the government in such instances. The processors at the Baruj site use charcoal from island areas for their smoking and drying, but sometimes hucks from coconuts and groundnuts.

184 The availability of drinking water at home was not considered as a problem; however, they expressed problems with out-of-town markets, including lack of access to other points in the village, lack of water source in the market and insufficiency of quality of water available. At the same time, they suggested water for the new project of aquaculture to come from the same or new boreholes and were willing to take the opportunity to engage in an activity which serves as an alternative source of income.

### 4.3.8 Access to Markets

185 Women do not have access to products and markets with high return, due to gender-based division of labor within households as well as markets, which allocates women the less profitable. This division is accentuated by little attention and investment devoted to what is considered female products or markets. The lack of transport facilities also contributes to this situation, which puts women at a great disadvantage as they are without capital and cannot afford proper storage facilities or long-distance transportation. They are also without connection with dealers in foreign markets for their produce. Women have no access to the market for fish and oysters sold by a private foreign market for smoked and dried fish of the kind processed by women, but the travel restrictions imposed by the latest pandemic has greatly diminished this outlet.

186 No organized market information was available to women who participated in the focused discussions. They were not aware that they have a competitive advantage as they have always been copied by direct observations themselves. Whereas markets for fish and oyster products were not an issue, those for vegetables were considered insufficient in size by women farmers. This was partly caused by foreign products being sold at lower prices than local produce. The women also complained about the vegetables of inferior quality grown in the country. The women indicated that they did not know how to find markets for their products and appeared unaware of possible subsidies, acquisition of quality seeds, diversification of crops, collaboration with the industry which imports vegetable seeds, and so on.


---

general, the low investment that the society accords its women in terms of literacy appears to be compounding their disadvantaged situation.

### 4.3 Other Professional Needs

187 The common unmet needs expressed by women and community leaders concerned infrastructure/equipment attributes for their work pits, ponds, smelting and storage facilities for women in fishery and agriculture, and toilets for those in crop agriculture. Women whose inputs were fish indicated that midden who bought boatfuls of fish from fishermen and sold to the women in smaller quantities at a higher price. The women also expressed dissatisfaction with the lack of toilets at the landing site community leaders, who were otherwise informative about the plight of women working at the sites. Women at the landing sites also cited as lack of toilets and drinking water at the sites was a problem; another issue was that they had to walk long distances for these facilities.

188 Lack of capital as one of the largest obstacles was mentioned by women at all three landing sites visited, in addition to the community leader of the Banjul site. They did not appear to be aware that their chances of obtaining capital would increase by many folds if they became functionally literate. When asked about the type of project they would like to take up with a loan, most women said it could be overcome with the support of development partners. The smoking facility in Bintang was built by a donor as a coping mechanism against the loss of income from fish sales. At the same time, the community is waiting for a new facility to be built by a donor. At the same time, women engaged in farming were willing to take up a new project, such as poultry and aquaculture, if a donor would give them an opportunity to do so. The lack of capital was also cited as a major obstacle at the Banjul site; but absent ownership of past aid projects seemed to have deprived them of the capacity to plan strategically, which is likely exacerbated by their illiteracy, lack of formal education and patriarchal culture.

### 4.4 Water and Sanitation

189 Water: According to the Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey 2013 [65], 90% of the population had access to an improved source of water; such as piped water, tube well, borehole, protected well and bottled water. The urban areas were the poorest in terms of richness in water wealth were 76%, respectively, and for the rural and urban areas were 86% and 92%, respectively. [65] The time required to an improved source of water was reported as less than 30 minutes by 41% of households; 18% reported up to 30 minutes and 12% more than 30 minutes.[65] Over 60% of urban households reported that improved drinking water was available on premises, while 7.25% needed to spend 30 minutes to reach a source of water; the corresponding figures for rural areas were 48% and 19%, respectively (1%). but for more than half of the households in the three poorest quintiles up to 30 minutes of travel was required.[65] It was overwhelmingly women over 15 years of age who collected water: 85.1% in the country and 90.5%, and 77.8% in the rural and urban areas, respectively.[65]

190 Overall, the proportion of households which had access to an improved drinking-water source located on premises, free of E. coli and parasites while meeting this low at 33.8% (45.6%) in urban areas and 1.9% in rural areas. While a sufficient amount of drinking water was mostly available for 87.5% overall, 82.2% in rural and 89.8% in urban areas), the unavailability of sources affected more than 10% of the rural households (13.4%) and less than 1% of the urban households. In terms of water quality, 67.5%, 62.1% and 63.5% of rural and urban households, respectively, drank water that contained the bacteria. About 70% and 40% of the drinking water of rural and urban households, respectively, indicated high (between 11 and 99) or very high (≥100) levels of turbidity. In terms of treatment, only a small majority of the households did not treat water: 71.3% in the rural areas and 86.9% in the urban areas.

191 Sanitation Almost all households in the country had some kind of handwashing facility. Improved sanitation – flush toilets, pour-flush toilets, ventilated improved pit latrine and latrine with slab – were used by 75%, 20%, 48% and 36% of rural households respectively. Open defecation was observed among 2.0% of rural households and 0.6% of urban households. Improved sanitation facilities tended to be shared, especially in rural settings: 27.8% and 59.9% of rural and urban households had access to a single latrine for more than five people respectively. Only 13.4% were treated for unimproved facilities: 43.9% (rural) and 17.6% (urban). Unsafe disposal of excreta from on-site sanitation facilities was observed in 20.5% of rural households and 8.5% urban households. The type of drinking water contamination is responsible for the high level of contamination observed. Safe disposal of children’s stool is not complete, which is a likely contributing factor: 83.1% of children had their last stool deposited in an unsafe place.

192 Approximately 95% of women in the country used appropriate menstrual hygiene materials with a private place to wash and change while at home, and the rate was higher at 98.0% for rural women. The National Development Plan 2020-2023 aspires to increase the proportion of population with access to safe drinking water to 100%, improve sanitation facilities to 75%, and become open defecation free by 2021.

The vast majority used solid fuel, little over half using three stone stove or open fire. The poorer the household, the more likely it used these devices: 94.1% of the poorest

## 4.6 Health

### 4.6.1 General Health

#### Overview
In 2018, life expectancy at birth for Gambian women was at par with the Sub-Saharan average and slightly higher than that of men (Table 16). The mortality rates for adults were lower than the world average for both gender (Table 16). The mortality rate for children under five years old in The Gambia is somewhat lower than that for the Sub-Saharan average and has decreased significantly from 42.9% to 30%. It is also well above the world average of 18.4%, indicating high prevalence of deaths from communicable diseases.

#### Causes of Death
More than half of the deaths were caused by communicable diseases and maternal, prenatal, and nutrition conditions, and about two-fifths by non-communicable diseases. Non-communicable diseases are considered under-diagnosed and underreported as a cause of illness and death. While the proportion of deaths from communicable diseases has decreased significantly in The Gambia since 2018, it is still well above the world average.

#### Factors Influencing Health
The factors influencing health include:
- **Access to clean water**: Only about half of Gambian households have access to clean water.
- **Sanitation and hygiene**: Only a quarter of households have access to sanitation facilities.
- **Health services**: The number of health workers per 1,000 people is below the Sub-Saharan average.

#### Health Services
The health system in The Gambia faces several challenges:
- **Limited resources**: There are insufficient medical supplies and equipment.
- **High workload**: Health workers often have to manage a large caseload with limited resources.
- **Low morale**: Health workers may experience burnout due to the high workload and lack of support.

#### Conclusion
The health system in The Gambia needs significant improvements to address these challenges and improve overall health outcomes. This includes increasing access to clean water, sanitation facilities, and medical supplies, as well as providing better training and support for health workers.


---
**Page 215**
---

preventable diseases and hence unsatisfactory health system in the country. Nonetheless, the country is less likely affected by major diseases than the average of Sub-Saharan Africa, most of whose countries post higher GDP than The Gambia (Table 17).

The incidence of tuberculosis (TB) is high, with the detection and treatment are at higher success rates than the average of Sub-Saharan Africa. The incidence of malaria is also quite contained compared to Sub-Saharan average.

198 Food Security and Nutrition: The country produces only half of its food consumption needs today; "food security" has been defined since 2011/12 when it depended 37% on its staple food imports and 6% on imported food.104 Self-sufficiency in the country's staple crops – has fallen from about 50% in 2011/12105 to less than 10%.106 The poverty rate has been stable at about 48% in the last five years, but food insecurity is not evenly distributed. In 2017, 39.5% of households were severely food insecure, 7.4% moderately food insecure, 29.1% marginally food secure and 62.8% food secure.107 The majority of the food insecure households were mostly in the rural areas of Central River, Upper River and North Bank regions.108 Female-headed households (53.8%) are more severely food insecure than male-headed ones (46.8% and 16.7%), respectively, and there were proportionally more severely food insecure households headed by women than those by men (0.90% and 0.8%, respectively).109

199 A third of the total population and food intake short of minimum energy requirements, with cereals as staple foods, was estimated to be undernourished in 2016/17 compared to two-fifths in 2015 and fats & carbs around 2010.110 Approximately two in every three households had a highly diverse diet and the urban households enjoyed higher diversity than the rural ones in 2016.111 The households with lower diversity were in the North Bank, Upper River and Central River regions.112 Urban households are less nutritionally secure at household level compared to men.113 The prevalence of undernourishment was 11.5% in 2017/18, down from 14.6% in 2016/17 and 19.5% in 2015/16.114 The prevalence rate has been constantly below the Sub-Saharan average and the gap is widening.115 Micronutrient deficiencies (iron, vitamin A and iodine) were considered widespread and affected women and under-five children more than men and older children.116 The proportion of children who were stunted, wasted, thin, education and labor productivity in 2018 was equivalent to 5.1 % of GDP.117

200 Children: The Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey 2018 indicates that under five mortality was 57, infant mortality 41, and neonatal mortality 31, all per 1,000 live births (20) and all considerably above world average of 36.9 for 57 and 38, respectively.118 The rates were higher in the rural areas than in urban areas, except for infant/mortality rate.119 Mother’s education did not seem to influence neonatal mortality.120

208 Contraception: Use of contraception among women of reproductive age (15-49) is very low at 16.8%, with almost all using a modern method.167 Male condoms are used by only 0.1% of the same group. The proportion increased with education and the number of children living in the home, but barely with wealth.163 Less than half of the women who were of reproductive age and married (or in a union) had ever been counselled on family planning methods or received information about family planning was unmet.167 Among women of the same age group, but not married or in a union, more than 90% desired contraception and over 60% reported that their needs were not met;168 unsafe abortion is considered to be a major public health problem among young people.169 Young people should make their own informed decisions regarding sexual relations, contraceptive use and reproductive health care.169

### 4.3 Health System

209 The Gambia's primary health expenditure per capita, in purchasing power parity terms, was USD 47.7 in 2018, low compared to the average of Sub-Saharan Africa or of the world, which were USD 84.6 and 848.6, respectively.169 The current health expenditures as percentage of GDP in 2018 were 3.1% (The Gambia), 5.1% (Sub-Saharan Africa) and 8.9% (the world).170 Together with the high immunization rates among children under five years old, this suggests that the country has made progress towards the external health expenditure was considered at 35.1% of the current health expenditure in 2018 in The Gambia (against 12.3% of the same for Sub-Saharan Africa in 2018), although it had more than halved in proportion to GDP.

### 4.4 Health Services

210 In 2014, public health care services were provided through: seven public hospitals at the tertiary level; six major health centers and 41 minor health centers at the secondary level; 40 community clinics and 634 Primary Health Village at the primary level.171 Over 60 health care providers of other types – private, traditional healers, and religious leaders – were also available in the country. The major public hospitals were located in Banjul and its environs. Private pharmacies existed in a large number and provided health services as did traditional healers.172

# 4.7 Coping with Negative Shocks

## Social Protection
Various social protection projects, schemes and programmes exist in The Gambia, but limitations in fiscal, monetary and financial buffers do not allow the country to establish a full-fledged social safety net. The government has introduced some measures to protect people from falling to the extreme poor that is predictable, comprehensible in reach and adequate in amount: unemployment insurance and maternity benefits for the employees in the formal sector; national health insurance programme for civil servants and other public servants; and conditional cash transfers.

The percentage of women aged 15-49 with health insurance in 2018 was a mere 2.4%, and it was the employers who provided the insurance to 93.2% of them. The coverage was somewhat better for men of the same age group at 3.9% with 92.6% of them covered by their employers. In other words, the vast majority of people who were insured had no choice but to rely on informal social protection.

### Informal Social Protection
The practice of informal social protection is based on the time-honored tradition of kinship-support through cash or in-kind transfers. All women who participated in focused group discussions depended on information social protection, which consisted of borrowing money from family members and friends. In addition to these formal mechanisms that are more structured, but still informal, mechanisms are also in place, such as kofis, osusu (both are types of revolving funds) and jokat (charitable giving by the Muslim better-off). But kofis and osusu appeared not as widely used as they were before. In times of economic shocks, people always find ways to cope by relying on the kin.

### Exceptions
Two exceptions among the seven discussion groups were Njoben and Kartong. In Njoben, women had access to the Social Development Fund, a national NGO which operates as a microfinance institution with resources from the World Bank. At Amajai land grant site, the self-formed professionals' association operated revolving funds for the members, while having access to microfinance.

### Sources of Negative Shocks
The female and youth participants in the focused group discussions asserted that catch from the sea was diminishing, which reduced the volume of their merchandise and income.
