# 4.8 Aspirations of the Youth

## The Rural Areas Around the World

219 In the rural areas around the world, the female youth often belong more to the group of women, instead of being part of the male-dominated fishing community. The participants in the focused group discussions were aged 15-35, whose occupations were: fishing, fish selling and boat building at landing sites; both fishing and farming in Jayah, and farming at other villages. Some identified that they had better opportunities than their parents because they could earn more money from better opportunities existed in the urban areas and abroad. The former group was comprised of those involved in fisheries, and the latter included those in Noplen, Jahlulu and Medina Lamin Kanteh, which were further inland and closer to the river, while the fishing community was located on the coast. The fishing youths pointed out that their profession allows attainment of food security.

220 The youths who worked in fishery had noticed the diminishing amount of catch, which they attributed to insufficient policing of the fishing activities and climate change. They did not expect the climate change to have any effect on their livelihoods because it was beyond their control. This was the opinion that their lives were at the whim of the changing weather over which they do not have control. The Banjul youths felt that the government should take action against climate change. Climate change were ineffective and unsustainable. The youths who worked at landing sites were willing to try their hand at new types of livelihoods; the youth in Banjul preferred something related to fishery.

221 For the youth, economic hardships were caused by celestial factors, such as drought or a lack of fish stock. Fishermen in Karang mentioned that they had to borrow money from relatives and friends on a weekly basis, whereas the youth in Bingtang thought that these were infrequent. On such occasions, they borrowed money from the extended families. The economic hardships were more directly related to the climate change than those experienced by their parents. The youths who worked at landing sites in Banjul Kanteh – both farming communities – were less informed about climate change. Youth farmers mentioned engaging in petty trade, construction work and migrating to the urban areas in times of economic distress.

222 The youths in both sectors were aware that they have more livelihood options than their parents. Some mentioned that they had access to more information. Many believed that self-employment through small enterprises was the way to improve their lives and mentioned acquiring skills (e.g., carpentry, tailoring) as a means of improving their livelihoods. For fishing youth in Banjul, training for better fisher for training and capital for such endeavors, donor support was expected. Prosperity was the social goal for

## 5. SOCIAL PARTICIPATION OF RURAL GAMBIAN WOMEN

### S.1 Rural Women’s Voice in Politics

223. Women's voice is heard increasingly more in The Gambia. The country has a good number of female judges, including the Chief Justice of The Gambia and the President of the National Criminal Court, who is a former Attorney General and Minister of Justice.[167] Among the 12 new judicial appointments to the superior court of the Gambia a few years ago, five women were judges.[168] Of the newly appointed magistrates in The Gambia, four are women. [169]

A woman was named a justice of the Supreme Court of The Gambia, the highest court in the country. [170] In 2013, the National Assembly passed a law that requires that the Olympic Committee and the Gambian Christian Council have enshrined in their Constitutions that in case the President is a man, the Vice-President should be a woman.[171] The remarkable progress concerns primarily the upper tier of the political ladder at national level; in the early 2000s and in 2015, women were much less represented at lower levels (Table 20).

development committees, but since the Area Councils—which role is to address the social needs of the population—are male dominated, the rural women’s voices are not well represented. Of 1373 villages, five percent still practice the role of alkali (traditional family head) in the early 2000s. ### Gender Inequality is said to be manifestation of social stratification on a larger scale in the Gambian society, which assigns jobs and functions to particular families and gender as well as renders family and community structures hierarchical. Social stratification excludes the selected segments of the society, including women, from participating in decision-making processes that can impact the country from making the best use of its human resources and to slow it development.

#### 5.2 Harvester/Processor/Seller and Farmer Organizations

As of 2017, it was reported that 60 years of cooperative development efforts in The Gambia did not succeed in institutionalizing viable economic groups. The key constraints identified were: involvement of the government in excess to harness the collective strength for political ends; lack of evolution of farmer groups into producer cooperatives; and lack of sustainable financing mechanism for the groups’ investments.

The professionals working at each site, including not only women and but also men who work as fishermen, are not considered part of the community by the Ministry of Fisheries and Water Resources. Whereas Land Site Managers, who are officials of the Ministry of Fishery and Water Resources, did not consider women as part of the community defined by landing sites and lacked information on the needs of women in the communities. The site managers were unaware that what face and their material needs, such as cans. Their conception did not include what may require negotiation with the local government or other stakeholders. Women’s participation was considered one of the biggest problems for them at all three landing sites visited. In the leader at Banjul cited the working capital, however, as one of the needs of women and hence made an indirect reference to the midstream issue. The site manager at Bintang said that women were not considered part of the local community or users, but some differentiated them by needs (Kartong and Bintang landing sites and Medina Lamin Kanteh).

The fish and oyster harvesters, processors, and sellers, who participated in the focused group discussions for the proposed project preparation, invariably belonged to a local association comprised of

# 8. RISKS AND OPPORTUNITIES FOR RURAL WOMEN FACING CLIMATE CHANGE

## Livelihood Skills
The women in the fishery sector are mostly illiterate and acquire the skills to harvest and process marine products by helping their mothers who also worked as harvesters and processors.

processors. As they have clearly noticed, the primary input necessary for their profession is decreasing, but they do not have a viable strategy for the future. The women living in similar situations dream of acquiring capital and would enable them to become involved in trades such as carpentry and tailoring. The government has promoted vocational training, which has reached as far as regional capitals.

232 With climate change reducing the amount of fish available, at least some of the women currently engaged in harvesting, profiting and selling wild oysters and fish will need to engage in new activities. Aquaculture is one such activity that young and older women seem more able to use the knowledge and skills that they have already.

233 Empowerment and Decision Making Power: Most women in the fishery sector earn more than their husbands and many put their children through school with their income alone, but their economic empowerment does not translate into decision-making power regarding the family. Women are often viewed as wives and husbands. The women may be consulted on strategic issues, such as purchases and sale of assets, inheritance and marriage, but the final decisions are made by men. Women allow such arrangements to continue because they fear that if they speak up, they will be accused of being ungrateful or rebellious. Such sentiment is reflected in the fact that a significant proportion of women themselves think that men are justified in making all major decisions regarding family matters such as children's education and health, etc. Domestic violence is rarely reported. When it comes to problems related to their work but not directly with the family, women do not hesitate to go to the officials to lodge their complaints.

234 If women are to take up new activities under the project, household members who are not participating in the project must be involved and encouraged to participate so that they can also have more time to devote to additional activities. If the country is to make the best use of its human resources, all must be given decision-making power regardless of gender, in particular equal power between wife and husband. Women should be allowed to make decisions regarding their own lives as well as how they work mostly independently of each other, while they help the other at certain gender-determined stages of production. As a result, resources are not used efficiently at the household level because the availability of some equipment or inputs is dependent on one person's decision and not that of another. For example, a woman's crop or animal, even in the same household. Open dialogues between wife and husband to construct a common vision for their future will help them to understand each other's contributions of each household member and guide them to more equitable participation in family life.

235 The project personnel may be tempted to engage in sexual exploitation, abuse or harassment (SEAH), or even gender based violence (GBV), as they acquire new power from being associated with the project. Project staff should be trained on how to avoid such situations and also be made aware that the prevailing gender-based power-dominion of favor of women and provoke SEAH and GBV by men at home and in the community. Men may perceive so even if that is not the case and interpret the changes as a threat to their status. Project staff should be trained on how to avoid such situations and also be made aware that the project trains project-related personnel on the subject, including some of their supervisors who have stronger power over them. The project will ensure that all project staff are trained in the UN Women and FAO Grievance Redress Mechanisms (GRM) will be strengthened through training of GRM respondents and linkage to GBV referral pathways so that SEAH and GBV related grievances are adequately managed in inclusive, safe and non-discriminatory ways. The project will ensure that the GRM is well designed and operationalized to provide timely services and redress to survivors. The issue of unequal gender relations will be approached using a household methodology, which sees a household as at the community level. Additional training on GBV will also be provided for households in the project area.


---
**Page 228**
---

# Community Gatekeepers

Men assume the role of landing site managers, who belong to the Ministry of Fisheries and Marine Resources. Although they oversee the entire landing sites, they do not regard women who work on the sites as part of the site community. The informal landing-site community leaders and allibok are much more aware of the obstacles that women face, but reported only material related to their own concerns.

# Literacy and Numeracy

Many women are illiterate and have difficulty running their small businesses well enough to generate more income than their husbands. They are limited by lack of literacy and numeracy skills, respectively, which makes it difficult for them to take advantage of their inability. Women may fit the posts of President and Secretary of their local professional associations, but they do not hold any leadership positions in the association.

# Negotiation Skills

The lack of decision-making opportunities at a strategic level at home, coupled with lack of literacy and numeracy skills, appears to have deprived the women of the capacity to make strategic planning with respect to their livelihoods, e.g., finding new markets and adapting to climate change. It may have been exacerbated by inefficient ownership of past aid projects. Without long-term visions accompanied by strategic thinking, it would be difficult to adjust their livelihoods to climate change which is increasingly exerting negative effects.

# Female Youth

The female youth are very often subsumed under the larger category of women, and their distinguishing qualities (e.g., higher literacy and numeracy) are not highlighted unlike the male youth who are more likely to be involved in fishing. The female youth have a high unemployment rate; the unemployment rate for female youths with intermediate level of education is higher than for other levels or for male youths. Teenage pregnancy rates are high in the rural areas. Poverty and statically poor high risk of HIV/AIDS among young people, especially girls, are also common problems among female youth.


---
**Page 229**
---

rural women could be engaged in work that makes good use of their strength: higher literacy and numeracy skills compared to that of rural women of older generations.

### Ownership and Sustainability

As has been noted by both the National Environment Agency and the Department of Agriculture, women's voices have not been sufficiently integrated in many of the aid projects, leading to unsatisfactory and unsustainable results. Women and alkalo in Bintang pointed out the insufficient ventilation of a smoking structure built by a donor, which appears to be partly due to the lack of involvement of women in the project design stage.

Women in Tambo reported that they abandoned the new oven boiling stoves provided by the donor, because it required more fuel than they could afford.

### Involvement of Women Users

Involvement of women users in location selection, design, installation and maintenance of equipment is essential for their effective use. This not only ensures sustainability of the equipment, but also empowers women by equipping them with the knowledge and skills related to the tools of their trade.

### Safeguarding the Environment

Women who participated in the focused group discussions knew that climate was changing and that it would not revert to what it was before. They were also aware that the mangroves they had planted were being eaten up by crabs, which they could not control or report. Knowing these facts, they did not discourage using mangroves for roof fishing and dry fishing, and did not express, at least explicitly, concerns about their conflicted interests with respect to mangroves.

They described unsatisfactory situations with respect to drinking water at home; out-of-order pumps, lack of spare parts, insufficient quantity of water available. At the same time, they were willing to engage in aquaculture if a donor is to propose an aquaculture project, with the assumption that water will be found and pipes laid by the donor. However, they did not express any interest in this type of project as it does not seem aware that it can also repel insects, including mosquitoes. In sum, women do not think they are responsible for safeguarding the environment, which will limit the sustainability of their actions.

## 7. PRINCIPLES OF PROJECT FORMULATION AND IMPLEMENTATION

### 7.1 Principles of Project Formulation

#### 7.1.1 Climate Change and Decision Making

Climate change is a social challenge profound enough that it does not allow leaving human capital undervalued as it has been to date, it demands both women and men to make strategic decisions at all levels so that their livelihoods are well adapted to the changes. Women, however, have not had the experience of making such decisions in the past because they were not involved in the decision-making aspects of women so that they will be able to effectively and strategically plan for the future. Empowerment of women needs to be accepted at home and in the community; men and community gatekeepers will be sensitized on the issue and will take part in gender empowerment for their acceptance and ownership.

#### 7.1.2 Involvement of Women Users

Climate change requires strategic decision-making, which leads to strategic actions for livelihoods. Women are savvy enough to have survived and even flourished without literacy and numeracy, but have been limited to the informal sector and low-productivity products with meager profit margins. Some


---
**Page 230**
---

of the crucial factors that enable women to go beyond these limits are their access to formal information and systems and acquisition of negotiation skills

### 7.1.3 Female Youth as Agents of Change

248 The female youth are not recognized as a distinct group among women or the youth, and their particular vulnerability kinds as well as strengths are hardly taken into account in planning at any level Focusing on the fact that young women with intermediate education, which is the most common level for girls with intermediate education, the proposed project will integrate this social segment more clearly into the society by taking advantage of their literacy, both traditional and modern. The youth will be trained to become environmental agents who will teach other sectors and aunts obtain the skills. They will also be trained on the environment and participate in environmental management together, with the older generation. The project also makes use of the characteristics of the youth that they are more motivated than the older generations to transform the society for the better.

#### 7.1.3.1 Environmental Agents

249 Not only the needs of women, but also their knowledge and skills are often insufficiently considered in various kinds of projects, generating little ownership among targeted women. Without ownership, projects are seen as gifts and hence a one-off activity, after which the pre-project condition prevails again. The proposed project will ensure that women have full ownership over the environment they are managing it and to that their advantage; they do not have ownership of the environment. The proposed project will integrate women's inputs, and at the same time, equip them with knowledge necessary to assume full ownership of the equipment and the environment that are essential to their livelihoods.

### 7.2 Principles of Project Implementation

250 Based on the above project formulation principles, the project activities will be implemented as below to enhance the participation of women and female youth in fishery with decision-making power. All activities and mitigation measures will be in accordance with FAO and Green Climate Fund Policies on gender equality and inclusion (Administrative Circular N° 2015/36); GCF Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses (2018); FAO Policy on Gender Equality 2020-2030; GCF Gender Policy (2019); FAO Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (2015); GCF Policy against Corruption N° 2015/37 (2015); GCF Revised Policy on the Prevention and Protection of Women and Girls from Violence, including Sexual Harassment and Abuse of Authority N° 2015/39 (2015); GCF Revised Policy on the Prevention and Protection of Women and Girls from Violence, including Sexual Harassment and Abuse of Authority N° 2015/40 (2015); GCF Policy against Fraud and other Corruption Practices N° 2015/38 (2015); and GCF Policy on Procurement Activities N° 2017/1. A gender and social inclusion expert will be a member of the project's Technical Advisory Committee. The aspects related to women and youth of the project will be monitored according to the indicators in the Gender Action Plan.

#### 7.2.1 Building on and Enhancing the Strengths of Women

251 The project will fully integrate inputs from women and engage them as much as possible in activities that are related to equipment and facilities for processors. The beneficiaries and the procurement team will be involved in the selection of equipment, including its type, size, location, and work sequence for installation, and the beneficiaries will be allowed to observe the team's work on the site and be updated on the progress on a weekly basis. Women will be consulted on various aspects of equipping, such as types of fish, location and dimensions of aquaculture waterbodies. Any training


---
**Page 231**
---

with female participants will involve them from the planning stage and allow instructors and beneficiaries to jointly define the content for enhanced effectiveness of the training and alignment of expectations.

### 7.2.2 Investment in Women

252 To make full use of the human capital in the country, the proposed project will compensate for the investment that the country had the tendency to neglect: it will provide literacy and numeracy trainings to female youth. The skills will encourage women to venture into the formal sector and capture the market segments that are more profitable, as they are already equipped with some business acumen.

In the long run, the empowerment of women through these fundamental skills is expected to lead to transformation of the domestic market, which has traditionally been dominated by men, overwhelmingly serving one with higher productivity and profit.

253 Women will be thoroughly consulted beforehand for organization of activities – on childcare and transportation needs as well as activities – and context of all activities; as they have many more house-bound obligations than men and their needs and strengths are more often set aside. All trainings and consultations under the proposed project will have slots reserved for women, including those organized by government officials and technicians. The training will cover various aspects of equipment and fish handling facilities; and fish-feed production. Women will be involved in review and agreement preparation of all contracts and agreements. They will also be trained to start small-scale dredging for expansion of the Banjul landing site and aquaculture training experience tasks commonly restricted to men. Training on sustainable management of clam and oyster culture as well as on quality standards, packaging, sales, etc., and fish processing workers will specifically target women.

In order to accommodate the high need for extension information, a extension service will have a voice-over version.

#### 7.2.3 Female Youth as a Distinct Group

255 The female youth will be recognized as a distinct group with its own strengths and weaknesses. Their strength in traditional and ICT literacy and numeracy will be tapped to empower their mothers’ generation who constitute the main actors in fish processing/selling and oyster harvesting/growing/ selling, clam farming, etc., and to provide them with skills for income-generating activities. For girls aged 14 years in the rural areas, the female youth will be trained on numeracy and pedagogy, in addition to environmental management.

256 All trainings and consultations as well as activities for women under the proposed project will have slots reserved for female youth, in addition to slots for youth-established fish-feeding units. The government officials and technicians who are female youths will get opportunities in a similar manner. By contributing to women’s empowerment in this manner, the female youth will be better integrated into the economy.

### 7.2.4 A Social Acceptance of Gender Empowerment

257 Culturally prescribed roles along gender lines drive women and men to pursue different household strategies, often disparaged. The household methodology shifts its focus from what women


---
**Page 232**
---

do not possess to what the family members aspire to, in particular, what they want to become, what they want to be engaged in and how they can achieve that goal. The methodology's objective is for Family members to engage in a dialogue on the time-honored guided dialogues among them, which allow women and men to understand and change gender norms and their connection to poverty. The methodology also aims at increasing women's access to resources and promoting gender-sensitive participation. The topics discussed will include sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) and gender-based violence (GBV), where appropriate. The project proposes application of household methodologies in the community level in conjunction with a focus on the interest of gender equality of rights will be greater than other female beneficiaries.

258. The important last mile in ensuring sustainable behavioral change is that the community gatekeepers and other key figures give a stamp of approval to the changes that have taken place, especially those related to GBV. Community gatekeepers are defined as those who can influence changes welcome to the entire community. The community leaders will be sensitized on the positive effects of gender empowerment at the household and community levels so that they may effectively endorse the changes in their communities. The project proposes a training for community gatekeepers based on the interest of the targeted groups and the skills of the trainers/facilitators to suscitate the interest and to guide them over time.

### 7.2.5 Sexual Exploitation, Harassment and Abuse and Gender-Based Violence

259. Gender empowerment is not complete if there is no effective mechanism that transmits the messages from the community gatekeepers to other members of the community. The message should not be condoned, while the project introduces additional possibilities of SEAH/GBV. The project personnel may use their power related to the project and engage in SEAH/GBV. The involvement of women in new activities and roles can lead to a situation where they are exposed to sexual harassment, abuse, and provoke SEAH and GBV by their male family members and other men in the community. It is widely accepted that women who are involved in new activities and roles are more likely to be targeted as victims. The trigger for SEAH and GBV can be anything that the perpetrator perceives as a threat to higher power over the ones whom he/she thinks or, is said to, be inferior to him/her. In other words, there is no situation where women who are involved in new activities and roles are not exposed to some form of political aggression (i.e., his/her perception of threat, or non-existence thereof, to her/his superiors). This includes prostitution, which is a common practice among women. The first is to use the other's body as he/she wishes with money, a type of supremacy over the service provider.

260. It is also well known that some socially respected persons or organizations can be perpetrators. In other words, SEAH/GBV can occur anywhere, anytime and in any way; what matters is the perception of the perpetrator. The project proposes to address the issues of SEAH/GBV, the proposed project integrates the household methodology, which includes re-education of power relations between gender, generation, etc., based on dialogues, the time-honored method of conflict resolution. In the Gambia, women who are involved in new activities and roles or who are not married to their spouses are protected also from men who are not their family members (Activity 3.2.3). Community gatekeepers who tend to set the tone for the community men are sensitized and involved in community-led measures against SEAH/GBV.

261. The project also acknowledges that the understanding of SEAH and GBV by the Gambian society and the international community does not neatly overlap. While the conflicts at community levels are resolved in an inclusive manner through dialogues among the members, the prevailing norm with respect to SEAH and GBV may be based on patriarchal traditions. According to the Gambia Multiple Indicator


---
**Page 233**
---

Cluster Survey 2018: We view to half of the women believe that husband beating his wife is justified when she neglects the children, argues with him, goes out without telling him, etc., despite the economic consequences for many women. The same survey found that 75% of all pregnant women aged 16-49 had undergone female genital mutilation. The practice was more prevalent in the urban areas (77.3%) than in the rural areas (68%). This is a cultural issue and not related to poverty (urban women versus 42.5% of rural women). It is neither effective nor culturally sensitive to work according to the definition of SEAH/GBV of the international community without agreeing with the local population what constitutes violence.

262 For these reasons, the project tackles the issues at their root rather than change the mentality of potential aggressors/victims and emphasizes prevention, which is a more effective mitigation measure than after-event management. The household methodology, based on dialogues, is adopted to allow the beneficiaries to express themselves freely without fear of retaliation or being considered "opposed" unwelcome by the victims and why such attitudes/behaviors/actions should be changed. In the event that SEAH takes place, the project will, as after incident management (the other type of mitigation), convene gatherings with all community members in order to discuss the causes of similar incidents by the same aggressor(s) and others in the communities: first with the FAO Gender Focal Points and ESGA/SEAH focal points, then with the project team, including the project manager (who is also above (excluding practitioners and project management members in case they are men), another woman with men alone and finally with the entire community members. This method respects the Gambian culture which gives great weight to the family and community and allows for a more effective dialogue between the professionals along the GBV referral pathway in the relevant district, followed up by the same after-management as for SEAH.

263 The risk of the SEAH/GBV starts at home: gender-based domestic violence was raised as a major issue by the Women Bureau of the Gambian, but not harassment/silence by strangers/acquaintances, implying the gravity of the former compared to the latter. Men in the family are not necessarily supportive of empowerment of their wives; therefore, the household methodology is used to sensitize men on gender and general empowerment. Once the whole family group has been sensitized, the entire family can come up with strategies to overcome the threats that women may face at workplace and on the road. For most women, the home is considered a safe place where they can express themselves freely, which is used by the entire community. The household methodology, consisting of dialogues between men and men across generations, is applied with a household as unit and at a local community level.

264 The FAO Gambian’s Ethics Focal Point and Alternate Focal Point, Project Management Unit and other relevant entities will be trained on the new guidelines for the prevention of sexual harassment in the National Assembly Matters - will be trained on the subject line in with the GCF and FAO policies on SEAH and GBV, and will participate in the community dialogues as needed. The Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) of FAO will be strengthened so that SEAH and GBV incidents are properly managed; GRM of FAO will be strengthened so that SEAH and GBV incidents are properly managed; GRM of FAO will be strengthened so that SEAH and GBV incidents are properly managed;

The country offices of FAO and UNFPA in The Gambia are drafting an agreement to incorporate on establishing operational referral pathways for making Post Exposure Preventives (PEPs) kits available. Professionals who would be involved in operationalizing referral pathways – such as the police, medical doctors, psychologists, lawyers and social workers – will be trained to ensure that they can provide information and be carried out to inform the general population about GBV and the referral pathways. ### 7.2.6 Integration of the Environment in Business

Awareness on the environment among women and female youth will be raised to make their decisions on business and household management sustainable by taking the environmental impacts into account. The training will focus on enhancing their ability to observe nature, monitor and respond with climate change adaptation strategies. The importance of using indigenous knowledge systems will also be emphasized. As with the trainings related to equipment and facilities, female beneficiaries will be involved from the planning stage and allow instructors and beneficiaries to jointly define the content and align expectations. They will also participate in the training design process, including site selection, monitoring and evaluation and related campaigns. They will also serve as forest management committee members and contribute to citizen monitoring of fishery sector and associated ecosystems.

8.2 Consultation Questions

### 8.2.1 Core Consultation Questions for Institutions

#### Gender, ethnic minorities and youth
- Understanding of gender/youth issues, including legislations, regulations and international conventions:
    - Are you aware of the international agreement commitments made by the government pertaining to equal rights, access to resources and opportunities?
    - Are you aware of the National Gender Policy and Action Plan?
    - Which components of the National Gender Policy and Action Plan does your office support? Do you make specific efforts to consider gender issues in your work?
    - Do you make specific efforts to consider issues faced by gender/youth?
- Knowledge of formal rights, laws, regulations, and commitment of the country to international conventions:
    - The country has policies, laws and regulations to safeguard the rights of all persons, regardless of gender, belief and age, including National Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan. Are you aware of these?
    - Which components of the National Gender Policy and Action Plan does your work support?
- When working with women and youth:
    - Do you have information indicating that women's conditions and needs are different from men's?
    - Do you promote your gender concerns at meetings, internal and external, including these objectives?
    - How do you encourage private commercial entities to consider the needs of women at all stages of their operation?
    - Do you consider gender issues when assessing and monitoring your programmes by differentiating between men and women or men and youth?
    - Do your publications clearly state your focus, priorities and activities on women and youth?
    - Have the objectives led to improved situations for women and youths?

#### Governance and grievance resolution
- What is the mechanism for community members to communicate the problems under your jurisdiction?
- Do you think the community members are well aware of the relevant laws and your mandate?
- How do the problems brought to you solve?
- How do you monitor whether the solutions that you provide are well implemented?

#### Roles and responsibilities in production and livelihood systems
- Types of livelihoods: fish, fish-lending related, processing/marketing, oyster (or other mangrove-related), agriculture, livestock.
- Who is the head of your household?
    - What is your work?
    - What are the roles of other household members?
    - What is the biggest problems related to your work?
    - Are you part of producers' groups or cooperatives?

- What are the crops cultivated for consumption at home? For sale?
- Who is responsible for selling these crops from your home? For sale?

- Who is responsible for fuel?

- Do you think you have the same amount of mangroves/fish/shellfish as your grandparents did?

## Access to and control of resources

### Production assets: economic ownership, access and control

- What are the assets that are used to generate income (e.g., boat, fishing gear, hatchery, drying oven/rock, land, livestock)?
- Who owns each asset?
- Should each asset belong to one family?
- What are the difficulties in accessing these facilities (e.g., fishing/drying/smoking gear) co-owned by all households in the village? If owned by several villages?
- What may be the difficulties? If owned by several villages?

### Income generation: access to secondary sources, items and control

- Who decides on how to spend income?

## Access to resources: water

- What is your source of drinking water (e.g., well, river, pond)?
- Is it shared by livestock and/or other animals?
- Do you have enough water for all your needs?
- What do you think about water quality?
- Do you have enough water for all your needs?

## Access to resources: fuel

- What is the source of cooking fuel?
- If it is wood, where does it come from? Who uses it?
- From whom do you buy them?
- Access to market and sale
- Who goes to the market for purchase?
- Who goes to the market for sale?

## Access to resources: transportation

- What are your choices of frequency, cost, possibility of independent travel?
- What kind of transportation is available? For what purpose? For women/handicapped?

- Is the frequency of the available transportation satisfactory?
- Is the cost of the available transportation reasonable?

Skills, knowledge and information

Decision-making and governance

- Who decides about inheritance?

Conflict resolution mechanism: among individuals and different social groups, including violence

- What are the differences between problems solved by communities and formal institutions?

Living conditions
- Main health problems: What are the main health problems?
- What may be the causes?
- Health services: public/private, number of nurses/doctors per population, distance to nearest hospital/clinic, availability of medical supplies, number of medical facilities.
- How many public clinics/hospitals are there in the vicinity?
- How far are they from the community?

- How do you cope with financial difficulties?
- What kind of opportunities are good (e.g., agriculture, craft making)?

- From which sources?
- Does the weather/climate go back to what it was during your grandparents' time?
- Adapted coping mechanism: alteration of practices/crop types/occupation/migration
- How do you deal with changes in weather/climate?
- Are there any problems?
- If the weather/climate keeps on changing, will that affect the availability of food?

# PART II: GENDER ACTION PLAN

## INTRODUCTION

206 The day-to-day implementation of the Gender Action Plan for the project will be led by a full-time Environmental/Social Safeguards and Gender Specialist, who will be recruited by the project, an arrangement which will strengthen integration of gender aspects in safeguards. experiences on the ground indicate that having a dedicated gender specialist is more effective than having gender issues given to one professional and those on gender to another. The Specialist will work as part of the Project Management and Implementation Unit (PMIU), and thus under the direct supervision of the Project Coordinator. The Gender Action Plan will be implemented in close collaboration with UNFPA Gambia for the implementation of project activities at national and village levels. The Specialist will collaborate with the Technical Advisory Committee, which includes representatives from government, civil society as well as those of youth into the complementary support that the Entities provide for the project. The Specialist will be responsible for the monitoring and quarterly reporting of the indicators under the Gender Action Plan and will also contribute to the annual report informing GCF's of the project’s environmental and social performance as part of GCF’s reporting to GCF.

267 FAO Ethics Focal Point, Ethics Alternate Focal Point and Gender Alternate Focal Point and other project-related personnel at executing agencies will be trained to ensure that the proposed activities are implemented in a manner that is free from gender-based violence. The project is also in discussion with UNFPA Gambia to secure its assistance in GBV related issues, e.g., establishment and operation of a GBV focal point, training of staff on GBV issues, sensitization of staff and community participation in community level dialogues for conflict resolution. Technical Advisory Committee will include as members a gender focal point expert, the representative of the Ministry of Women’s Affairs, Youth and Sports and all female representatives related to the project including women farmers and lawyers.

268 For organization/logistics and content of all activities, female beneficiaries will be consulted sufficiently prior to the event so that their household obligations, needs and strengths, which are numerous and essential in the lives of all, but often taken lightly, would be well accommodated. Such matters include childcare and transportation needs and timing of activities. The budget column of the Gender Action Plan below reflects the amount allocated for these gender-related issues. The total amount of funds that women and female youths benefit from is larger than what is shown in the Gender Action Plan below. The table that follows indicates the timeline and milestones of activities.

269 The table from the funding proposal is reproduced below to demonstrate how gender and youth are mainstreamed into the project’s proposal. The indicators in the Gender Action Plan have separate targets for women overall and female youths.


| Adaptation barriers | How the project will address the identified barriers |
| --- | --- |
| Very limited data and analysis on CC impacts, especially weak knowledge on adaptation measures | Under Output 3.1, the project will disseminate the comprehensive climate analysis report to all relevant stakeholders including government, public sector experts on climate change and adaptation and mitigation measures. |

No disposable income for investing and weak access to credit

Recognising the financial difficulties that a matching grant approach would impose on women in particular, Output 3.2 will ensure that the project is designed with a focus on strengthening beneficiaries' financial literacy through training and capacity building of women's groups and interested farmers [74] who can be engaged by the project. In addition, platforms to facilitate access to credit for women will be established in subsequent segments of fisheries value chains will be facilitated in Output 3.2.

Similarly, the investments under Output 2.1, which mostly represent communal enterprises, will be designed with a focus on strengthening the capacity of women's groups. Under Output 3.2, the group training will focus on strengthening the local communities' capacities for the establishment and management of credit unions and other financial institutions.

Under Output 3.2 strengthening beneficiaries' financial literacy through training and capacity building of women's groups and interested farmers [74] who can be engaged by the project. In addition, platforms to facilitate access to credit for women will be established in subsequent segments of fisheries value chains will be facilitated in Output 3.2.

Under Output 3.2 strengthening beneficiaries' financial literacy through training and capacity building of public and private sector actors including financial institutions, users of fish products, and other service providers, thereby improving the availability of appropriate credit services for women's enterprises and small-scale producers in the fisheries value chain. Under Output 3.1 measures will be active to stimulate policy and regulatory reforms needed also to improve financial inclusion.

Financial literacy for women is much smaller than for men

Under Output 3.2, women and men will examine their goals as a family and the roles they would play in reaching those goals, which serves an sensitisation on the women's responsibilities, needs and strengths and creates understanding of gender issues. ### Insufficient capacity for natural resource management

Under Output 3.1, the project will finance the identified capacity development activities to improve the management of mangrove resources and other coastal resources, while integrating climate change in its policies and regulations, including changing construction standards for fish landing sites and related infrastructure.

In addition, under Output 3.2, the communities involved in the mangrove (fisheries) habitat conservation will be supported in their efforts in sustainable exception management at local level, with support from national institutions.

### Lack of systematic inter-sectoral coordination

As part of its coordination and implementation arrangements, the project will establish two bodies: a Technical Working Group (TWG) and a Management Committee (MC). The meetings and activities of these two bodies will be financed by the project. The TWG is expected to be transformed into an individual transformation of these two bodies into permanent inter-sectoral coordination mechanisms.

### Target beneficiaries and formulations of policies, plans and interventions

Under Outputs 3.1 and 3.2, public service personnel and technicians will be trained in sustainable exception management for mangrove resources and fishery habitats.

Under Output 3.2, roundtable discussions on private investment will aim to discuss investment for women at least 40% of the time.

# Activity 3.2.3: Support to gender and youth empowerment through the application of the Household Methodology

## Sub-activities:
* **3.2.3.1: Household Methodology Training**
    * Target: 50% of participants.
* **3.2.3.2: Application of the Household Methodology for large scale training (co-funded and executed by UN Women)**
* **3.2.3.3: Facilitation of Household Methodology meetings with project beneficiaries to sensitization of community gatekeepers, households, and youth on sustainable livelihoods, investing in women’s economic empowerment, and community-led support measures against SEAH and GBV**

## Deliverables:
* Year 1: Empowerment process for 600 households

|---|---|

| Project Year 1 | 2,600 USD (PHB, 400 in PHS and 200 in PHG) |
